# State Issues Affecting Public Schools, 2025-26: Cited Facts

**Summary**

- **What this file is:** cited facts on the state laws, court cases, budgets, elections and trends affecting New Hampshire public schools in 2025-26, from a list of the 50 main state-level issues drawn up on November 10, 2026. Each topic adds to the library's rules and how-it-works topics: a bill's history and votes, a ruling's holding, the current status, the numbers and what happens next. Facts already in other files are not repeated.
- **Size:** 50 topics, 450 facts, in eight sections: School Funding Law and Revenue (4 topics, 38 facts); Local Control, School Budgets and Who Decides (8 topics, 65 facts); Special Education Aid, School Buildings, Medicaid and Federal Funding (7 topics, 66 facts); School Choice (8 topics, 62 facts); Rights and Classroom Rules (5 topics, 48 facts); School Climate, Students and School Standards (6 topics, 65 facts); Students and Safety (7 topics, 66 facts); Teachers, Pay and Dual Enrollment (5 topics, 40 facts).
- **Sources:** primary records first: the General Court's bill status pages and chaptered bill texts, the RSA, the Governor's signing and veto releases, New Hampshire Supreme Court and federal court orders, NH DOE, LBA, Department of Revenue Administration, DHHS and Secretary of State records, and federal agencies. News is cited only where no primary record exists yet (WMUR's unofficial Question 2 tally; the Concord Monitor's report of the State's legal spending), and those facts say so.
- **Dates:** every source was read on November 11, 2026. gc.nh.gov stopped answering for part of that day, so some of its pages were read from same-day Wayback snapshots (as_of "Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11"); check those against the live site at review.
- **Review dates:** enacted NH law and rules 2027-08-15; anything still open (pending appeals and lawsuits, uncertified results, waitlists, committee reports) 2027-05-11; everything else, including federal law, 2027-11-11.
- **Raw copies:** `raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/`.
- **Covered by existing topics instead:** the ConVal ruling (money-conval-case), this year's adequacy aid rates (money-adequacy-aid-amounts, money-fy2027-aid-and-tax), chronic absenteeism (chronic-absenteeism-nh-data) and HB 564's SAU budget vote (budget-sau-budget-vote).
- **Question 2 by district:** each school district's 2026 vote is in the district_question_two table (`GET /api/v1/question-two/district`); budget-question-2-2026-results gives the overall picture.

---

# School Funding Law and Revenue

**Sources and notes for this section**

- 4 topics, 38 facts. Researched November 11, 2026 (all `as_of` dates). Raw copies are in `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/` (plus `money-conval-aftermath/` for the ConVal case pages checked).
- Sources: the General Court's bill status pages and chaptered bill text (HB 1815 and SB 659), the current RSA text, the LBA fiscal note, the Senate committee and hearing reports; the New Hampshire Supreme Court's docket card, orders and the State's brief in Case No. 2026-0150; a Supreme Court order in JD-2026-0004; NH DOE's state revenue and expenditure summaries (2007-08, 2015-16, 2023-24, 2024-25), the FY2025 and FY2026 adequacy aid files and a district DOE-25 report; the Department of Revenue Administration's 2026 Annual Report and its business tax and interest and dividends tax pages.
- News is cited once, for the State's legal spending, which has no public primary record (see Not retrieved).
- Review dates: enacted law 2027-08-15; open matters (the Rand appeal) 2027-05-11; data and other records 2027-11-11.

## money-hb-1815-adequacy-definition: HB 1815 (2026): How the State Defines Its School Funding Duty

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1815 (2026), passed after the ConVal ruling: how it redefines an adequate education and the state's "shared responsibility" for funding it.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1815 (2026), "relative to education financing," was signed by Governor Ayotte on March 27, 2026 and became 2026 chapter 19, effective May 26, 2026 (60 days after passage).
   - citation: HB 1815 (2026), chapter 19, section 4
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: chaptered text (pdf.aspx?id=30254) ends "Approved: March 27, 2026 / Effective Date: May 26, 2026"; raw copies hb1815-billinfo.html and hb1815-chaptered.pdf
2. HB 1815 was sponsored by Republican Representatives Bob Lynn and Dan McGuire. The House passed it 188-162 on a roll call on March 5, 2026, after the Education Funding Committee voted 10-7 to recommend it; the Senate passed it 16-8 on March 26, 2026, after its Education Finance Committee voted 3-2 to recommend it.
   - citation: HB 1815 (2026) bill docket (HJ 6 P. 60; SJ 7); Senate committee report, March 13, 2026
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: the first name "Bob" is from the Senate hearing report ("Rep. Bob Lynn, Windham – Prime Sponsor"); the status page lists "Lynn (R), Dan McGuire (R)"
3. HB 1815 rewrote the policy and purpose section of New Hampshire's adequate education law, RSA 193-E:1, I-II. As rewritten, it says an adequate education is provided "through an integrated public education system of shared responsibility between state and local government," and that local governmental entities, including school districts, are created by the state and "are therefore indivisible from the state for the purposes of providing an adequate education."
   - citation: RSA 193-E:1, II (2026, 19:1, eff. May 26, 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-E/193-E-1.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. Since HB 1815, RSA 193-E:1, II also says the state and local governmental entities "function collectively to ensure that an adequate version of the academic program defined in RSA 193-E:2-a is provided," and that how they "choose to raise, allocate, and spend financial resources to implement this integrated public education system is a political policy matter reserved to legislative and executive judgment and control."
   - citation: RSA 193-E:1, II (2026, 19:1, eff. May 26, 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-E/193-E-1.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. HB 1815 changed the opening of RSA 193-E:2-a, I(a) to read "An adequate education shall be defined and identified as the school approval standards in the following learning areas," removing the words "Beginning in the school year 2008-2009, and for each year thereafter, the specific criteria and substantive educational program that deliver the opportunity for an."
   - citation: HB 1815 (2026), chapter 19, section 2; RSA 193-E:2-a, I(a)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-E/193-E-2-a.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: the removed words are shown in brackets in the chaptered text (hb1815-chaptered.pdf, page 2). RSA 193-E:2-a, IV(a), which says the minimum standards for public school approval "shall constitute the opportunity for the delivery of an adequate education," was not amended by HB 1815 and is still in the online RSA.
6. HB 1815's third section is the 2026 rewrite of RSA 198:40-a, III, under which the per-pupil adequacy amounts, including all differentiated aid, are "computed toward" the cost of an adequate education.
   - citation: HB 1815 (2026), chapter 19, section 3; RSA 198:40-a, III
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: the wording change itself is in money-adequacy-aid-amounts fact 4 ("2026, 19:3"); this fact only ties chapter 19 to HB 1815
7. The Legislative Budget Assistant's fiscal note on HB 1815 says the bill "does not change the grant amounts awarded to districts, charter schools, or education freedom accounts pursuant to RSA 198:40-a" and "will have no fiscal impact."
   - citation: LBA fiscal note, HB 1815-FN as introduced (revised February 10, 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: fiscal note is page 4 of the introduced version (pdf.aspx?id=24628), raw copy hb1815-introduced.pdf
8. For FY2026 (the 2025-26 school year, when HB 1815 passed), NH DOE's base adequacy rate was $4,265.64 per pupil, plus $2,392.92 for each pupil eligible for free or reduced-price meals.
   - citation: NH DOE, "FY 2026 Adequacy Grants Explained" (April 2026)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-2026-adequacy-grants-explained-april-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the FY2027 rates ($4,351 base) are already in money-adequacy-aid-amounts; FY2025 was $4,182.00 (fy-2025-adequacy-grants-explained-april-2025.pdf). Raw copy saved from additional-data/raw/town/doe
9. At the Senate Education Finance Committee hearing on HB 1815 on March 13, 2026, 20 individuals were recorded in support and 401 in opposition. The committee's report summarizes the prime sponsor, Rep. Bob Lynn, as testifying that state funding is designated for the academic programs required for an adequate education while "collateral expenses such as nursing and administration" remain the responsibility of local districts, and that his bill "doesn't change the current funding."
   - citation: Senate Education Finance Committee, Hearing Report on HB 1815-FN (completed March 20, 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: hearing report pdf.aspx?id=19436&q=HearingRpt, raw copy hb1815-senate-report-b.pdf
10. The same Senate hearing report summarizes opponents' testimony: the New Hampshire School Boards Association's Becky Wilson noted that "courts tie adequacy to minimum standards, but the bill limits the state's responsibility to academics only," and the New Hampshire School Funding Fairness Project's Zack Sheehan said the bill would remove judicial review if the legislature reduced school funding to $1 per student.
    - citation: Senate Education Finance Committee, Hearing Report on HB 1815-FN (completed March 20, 2026)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: the report says Rep. Lynn, asked whether the bill would allow funding as low as one dollar per student, "replied that this bill does not allow that"
11. SB 659 (2026), the Senate's version of HB 1815, sponsored by Sen. Carson (District 14) and 12 other Republican senators, was recommended 3-2 by the Senate Education Finance Committee but was laid on the table by voice vote on March 26, 2026, the day the Senate passed HB 1815; the bill record shows no later action.
    - citation: SB 659 (2026) bill docket (SJ 7)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2477&inflect=2
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: the enacting text of SB 659 as introduced is word-for-word the same as HB 1815 (derived: compared after removing headers and line numbers; raw sb659-introduced.pdf and hb1815-introduced.pdf). Status page shows "General Status: SENATE", "LAID ON TABLE"

## money-rand-appeal-2026: The Rand School Funding Appeal at the NH Supreme Court (2026)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: The State's appeal of the Rand school funding ruling (No. 2026-0150): the request to overturn Claremont, the recusal motions, status and legal costs.

1. The State's appeal in the Rand school funding case is New Hampshire Supreme Court Case No. 2026-0150, Steven Rand & a. v. State of New Hampshire, an appeal by right from Rockingham County Superior Court case 215-2022-CV-00167 (Judge David W. Ruoff). The court's public docket card lists its status as "Briefing" and lists "Coalition Communities" as another party.
   - citation: N.H. Supreme Court, Public Docket Card, Case No. 2026-0150 (printed August 18, 2026)
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/inline-documents/sonh/2026-0150-public-docket-card.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: case page https://www.courts.nh.gov/media/requested-cases/supreme-court/civil/2026-0150-steven-rand-v-state-new-hampshire (read in a visible Chrome window; curl is blocked); raw copies sc-2026-0150-case-page.html and 2026-0150-public-docket-card.pdf
2. On March 16, 2026, the Rand plaintiffs asked four of the five justices (Chief Justice Gordon MacDonald and Justices Patrick Donovan, Bryan Gould and Daniel Will) to recuse themselves from the appeal, and asked the court to suspend its Rule 21A recusal procedure, under which a motion to recuse "shall be decided by the justice whose recusal is being sought" and a motion to reconsider that ruling goes to the full court. The court denied the motion to suspend Rule 21A on April 28, 2026.
   - citation: Public Docket Card, Case No. 2026-0150 (entries of March 16 and April 28, 2026); Order of April 28, 2026
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-04/04-28-2026-order-denying-plaintiffs-motion-to-suspend-court-s-recusal-procedures.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
3. Each of the four justices denied the motion to recuse him: Justice Donovan on May 6, Justice Gould on May 18, Chief Justice MacDonald on June 4 and Justice Will on June 5, 2026.
   - citation: Public Docket Card, Case No. 2026-0150 (entries of May 6, May 18, June 4 and June 5, 2026)
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/inline-documents/sonh/2026-0150-public-docket-card.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
4. Chief Justice MacDonald's June 4, 2026 order notes that he was attorney general from April 2017 until joining the court on March 5, 2021, while the Department of Justice defended the ConVal case, and that he had disqualified himself in ConVal. He concluded that Rand is not the same "matter in controversy" as ConVal and that "a reasonable person, fully informed of the facts and the legal principles applicable to judicial disqualification, would not question my impartiality."
   - citation: Order of Chief Justice MacDonald, Case No. 2026-0150 (June 4, 2026)
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-06/06-04-2026-order-denying-plaintiffs-motion-to-recuse-chief-justice-macdonald.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
5. Justice Will's June 5, 2026 order says he was the State's Solicitor General from about September 2018 to May 2021 and was lead counsel for the State in the first ConVal appeal, which he argued on September 24, 2020; he concluded that ConVal and Rand are distinct and that "a reasonable person, fully informed of the facts alleged, would not question my impartiality in this case."
   - citation: Order of Justice Will, Case No. 2026-0150 (June 5, 2026)
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-06/06-05-2026-order-denying-plaintiffs-motion-to-recuse-justice-will.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
6. The Rand plaintiffs asked the full court to reconsider each denial (Justice Donovan on May 15, Justice Gould on May 26, and Chief Justice MacDonald and Justice Will on June 15, 2026), and the State objected. The docket card, printed August 18, 2026, shows no ruling on these motions and lists "Disqualified Justices: None."
   - citation: Public Docket Card, Case No. 2026-0150
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/inline-documents/sonh/2026-0150-public-docket-card.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
7. Chief Justice MacDonald is no longer on the court: a New Hampshire Supreme Court order dated September 15, 2026, approving his stipulation with the Judicial Conduct Committee in a matter about court personnel decisions, states that he "has resigned as a judge."
   - citation: N.H. Supreme Court Order, JD-2026-0004, In the Matter of Gordon J. MacDonald (September 15, 2026), page 2
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-09/09-15-2026-final-order.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: scanned PDF (no text layer); read from page images. The order says he admitted violating three canons of the Code of Judicial Conduct concerning the removal and reappointment of Dianne Martin; it does not mention the Rand case. Linked from https://www.courts.nh.gov/resources/committees/judicial-conduct-committee/decisions
8. In the Rand appeal, the court's May 26, 2026 briefing order set the State's brief for July 10 and the plaintiffs' for August 24, 2026; on the State's assented-to motions the court moved these to August 10 and September 30 (June 12) and then to August 14 and October 6, 2026 (August 5).
   - citation: Briefing Order (May 26, 2026); Public Docket Card entry of June 12, 2026; Order of August 5, 2026, Case No. 2026-0150
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-08/8.5.26-order.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
9. The State filed its brief in the Rand appeal on August 14, 2026, asking for 20 minutes of oral argument. Its questions presented ask whether Claremont School District v. Governor (1993) ("Claremont I") and the cases that followed it should be overruled; if not, whether Claremont II (1997) and its progeny should be overruled; and whether how the State costs and funds an adequate education is a "non-justiciable political question."
   - citation: Brief for the State of New Hampshire, Case No. 2026-0150 (filed August 14, 2026), Questions Presented I-III
   - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-08/8.14.26-state-s-brief.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: the brief also asks whether the plaintiffs had standing, whether their Part II, Article 5 claim is viable, whether a local education property tax is "converted" into a State tax when spent on adequacy costs, and whether the trial court erred in using federal special education law (IDEA/FAPE) to judge adequacy funding (Questions IV-VII)
10. The State's Rand brief asks the court, "at a minimum," to modify Claremont I and its progeny "to make clear that, for the purposes of Part II, Article 83, the 'State' includes its political subdivisions such as municipalities and school districts," and says that view was "most recently endorsed in House Bill 1815 (2026)." It also says the court should overrule Claremont I and its progeny "in their entirety."
    - citation: Brief for the State of New Hampshire, Case No. 2026-0150, Preliminary Statement and Summary of the Argument (pages 12 and 57)
    - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-08/8.14.26-state-s-brief.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-05-11
11. On November 3, 2026, the American Civil Liberties Union, the ACLU of New Hampshire and NEA-New Hampshire filed an amicus brief in the Rand appeal in support of the plaintiffs, arguing that the court should not overturn the Claremont precedents.
    - citation: Brief of Amici Curiae ACLU, ACLU-NH and NEA-NH in Support of Plaintiffs, Case No. 2026-0150 (November 3, 2026)
    - source: https://assets.aclu.org/live/uploads/2026/11/2026.11.03-Rand-Amicus-Brief-TO-FILE.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-05-11
    - note: the brief, posted by the amici on https://www.aclu.org/cases/rand-v-state ("Date Filed: 11/03/2026"), is dated November 3, 2026; raw aclu-amicus-2026-11-03.pdf and aclu-rand-case-page.html. The court's case page listed no filing after August 14, 2026 when checked, so the plaintiffs' own brief was not retrieved
12. The Concord Monitor reported in December 2025, from totals the Attorney General's office provided under a right-to-know request, that the State had spent about $4 million on the ConVal and Rand cases, including a $2 million payment of attorneys' fees to school districts and their lawyers in one case; for Rand alone it reported $47,700.36 in departmental expenses and $358,373.76 for outside counsel as of October 31, 2025.
    - citation: Concord Monitor, republished by NHPR, December 30, 2025
    - source: https://www.nhpr.org/nh-news/2025-12-30/nh-spent-over-million-dollars-defending-school-funding-lawsuits
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-05-11
    - note: news cited because no primary record is public: the Department of Justice gave the Monitor a list of totals instead of the records. The $4 million total includes the Monitor's own estimate of staff time ($244,375 at $57.50 an hour). See Not retrieved

## money-school-revenue-by-source-trend: Who Pays for NH Public Schools: Local Taxes, State Aid and the Trend Since 2007-08

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How much NH school revenue comes from local property taxes, state aid (including the statewide property tax) and federal funds, and the falling state share.

1. In 2024-25, New Hampshire school districts' total net revenues were $4,151,043,466, according to NH DOE: local taxation $2,636,895,961 (63.5%), "Equitable Education Aid" from the state $1,063,615,245 (25.6%), other state sources $106,668,917 (2.6%), federal sources $250,486,088 (6.0%), tuition, food and other local revenue $89,504,486 (2.2%), and other revenue $3,872,769 (0.1%).
   - citation: NH DOE Bureau of School Finance, "State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts, 2024-2025" (January 8, 2026)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/summary-of-rev-exp-fy2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: listed on https://www.education.nh.gov/who-we-are/division-of-educator-and-analytic-resources/bureau-of-education-statistics/financial-reports; raw copies summary-of-rev-exp-fy2025.pdf/.csv/.xlsx
2. In 2007-08, New Hampshire school districts' total net revenues were $2,546,699,973, according to NH DOE: local taxation $1,376,945,977 (54.1%), Equitable Education Aid $889,645,105 (34.9%), other state sources $96,997,662 (3.8%) and federal sources $136,515,834 (5.4%).
   - citation: NH DOE, "State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts, 2007-2008" (December 8, 2008)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/summ_rev07_08.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. In NH DOE's state revenue summaries, Equitable Education Aid fell from 34.9% of school district revenue in 2007-08 to 30.7% in 2015-16, 26.3% in 2023-24 and 25.6% in 2024-25, while local taxation rose from 54.1% to 58.8%, 60.9% and 63.5%.
   - citation: NH DOE, "State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts," 2007-2008, 2015-2016, 2023-2024 and 2024-2025
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/who-we-are/division-of-educator-and-analytic-resources/bureau-of-education-statistics/financial-reports
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: percentages are NH DOE's own. 2015-16: local $1,789,047,812, EEA $932,368,455, total $3,040,719,768 (summ_rev15_16.csv). 2023-24: local $2,461,935,991, EEA $1,064,472,678, total $4,041,009,041 (summary-of-rev-exp-fy2024.csv). Other years (2008-09 on) are on the same page and were not downloaded
4. On the DOE-25 annual financial report that New Hampshire school districts file, the statewide education property tax is reported as revenue from state sources ("Statewide Enhanced Education Tax," account 3112), next to the adequacy aid grant (account 3111), even though it is a property tax raised in each town.
   - citation: NH DOE, DOE-25 report form, Revenue from State Sources, Unrestricted Grants-In-Aid, lines 1-2 (accounts 3111 and 3112)
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform/DOE25/DOE25Reports
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: read from the Amherst 2024-25 DOE-25 file (copied from education-backend assets/finance/2025 to raw amherst-doe-25-2025.xlsx). That the SWEPT is raised in each town: RSA 76:8 (money-statewide-education-property-tax)
5. For FY2025, NH DOE's adequacy calculation shows the statewide education property tax at $364,350,537 statewide and the adequacy grant at $700,016,284.46, for a "final total state grant" of $1,064,366,821.46.
   - citation: NH DOE, "FY 2025 Adequacy Aid - Final (April 1, 2025)," State Total row
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/adequacy-fy-25-muni-summary-4.1.25.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: this total is within 0.1% of the $1,063,615,245 "Equitable Education Aid" in the 2024-25 revenue summary (derived comparison), which indicates that Equitable Education Aid includes the statewide education property tax. Raw copies adequacy-fy-25-muni-summary-4.1.25.pdf/.xlsx
6. Counting the statewide education property tax together with local taxation, property taxes made up about 72% of New Hampshire school district net revenue in 2024-25 (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE, State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts, 2024-2025; NH DOE, FY 2025 Adequacy Aid - Final
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/summary-of-rev-exp-fy2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: derived: ($2,636,895,961 local taxation + $364,350,537 SWEPT) ÷ $4,151,043,466 = 72.3%. The SWEPT alone is 8.8% of the total and the rest of Equitable Education Aid ($699,264,708) is 16.8% (derived). Assumes "Local Taxation" is the local school property tax that RSA 76:8, III provides for
7. NH DOE's revenue summary leaves out money that moves between New Hampshire districts and student food sales ($161,870,517 deducted in 2024-25, mostly tuition from other districts), and shows bond and note proceeds ($82,046,688 in 2024-25) below the revenue total because they are repaid from other revenue.
   - citation: NH DOE, State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts, 2024-2025, footnotes * and **
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/summary-of-rev-exp-fy2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the summary also notes "Claremont has not provided trust account information at the time of this report"

## money-education-trust-fund-revenue-and-business-taxes: Education Trust Fund Revenue, Business Tax Rates and HB 155 (2026)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: Which taxes fund NH's Education Trust Fund, business tax (BPT, BET) rates and cuts, HB 155 (2026)'s BET trigger, and the interest and dividends tax repeal.

1. In fiscal year 2026 (July 2025 to June 2026), $661.8 million of the taxes the Department of Revenue Administration collects went to New Hampshire's Education Trust Fund: $440.0 million from business taxes, $90.0 million from the real estate transfer tax, $71.2 million from the tobacco tax, $50.1 million from the utility property tax and $10.5 million from the meals and rentals tax. In fiscal year 2025 the total was $622.0 million.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, "General and Education Trust Fund Revenue Breakdown by Source," p. 42 (citing NH Revenue Focus, June FY 2026)
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the table covers DRA-administered taxes only; lottery and other Education Trust Fund sources in RSA 198:39, II are not in it. Raw copy annual-report-2026.pdf (PDF page 46)
2. New Hampshire's two business taxes together raised $1,137.0 million for the general fund and Education Trust Fund in fiscal year 2026, up from $1,103.1 million in fiscal year 2025, according to the Department of Revenue Administration; $440.0 million of the fiscal 2026 amount went to the Education Trust Fund.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, p. 42
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. New Hampshire's business profits tax rate is 7.5% for taxable periods ending on or after December 31, 2023. It was 8.5% until a series of cuts began with taxable periods ending on or after December 31, 2016 (8.2%), followed by 7.9%, 7.7% and 7.6%.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, "Summary of Taxes Administered," p. 5 (RSA 77-A)
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the current rates are also on https://www.revenue.nh.gov/taxes-glance/business-taxes (raw dra-business-taxes.html)
4. New Hampshire's business enterprise tax rate is 0.55% for taxable periods ending on or after December 31, 2022. It was 0.75% until cuts began with taxable periods ending on or after December 31, 2016 (0.72%), followed by 0.675% and 0.60%.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, "Summary of Taxes Administered," p. 6 (RSA 77-E)
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
5. According to the Department of Revenue Administration, Laws of 2023, chapter 79 changed the formula for sending business profits tax revenue to the Education Trust Fund, setting it at 41%, effective July 1, 2023.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, "Historical Summary of Business Profits Tax, RSA 77-A," p. 56 (2023, chapter 79:191)
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: DRA's BET history table (p. 59) lists 2023, chapter 79:192 with the same wording about "BPT revenue"; the statute text (RSA 77-A:20 and 77-E:14) could not be checked because gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11 (see Not retrieved)
6. New Hampshire's HB 155 (2026), chapter 280, raises the business enterprise tax filing threshold to $400,000 of gross business receipts or enterprise value tax base beginning with tax year 2027, and adds RSA 77-E:2-a, which provides for automatic 0.05% cuts in the business enterprise tax rate when there is a "certified business tax surplus," until the rate reaches 0.25%. The Department of Revenue Administration lists the effective date as January 1, 2027.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, "Legislative Session in Review," p. 13, and "Historical Summary of Business Enterprise Tax, RSA 77-E," p. 59 (2026, chapter 280)
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: the bill record (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=511&inflect=2) and chaptered text could not be read on 2026-11-11, so the signing date, votes and the trigger's exact terms are not confirmed here (see Not retrieved). HB 155 also suspends the threshold's inflation adjustment for tax year 2027 and resumes it for tax year 2029 (DRA p. 13)
7. New Hampshire's interest and dividends tax was 5% for tax periods ending before December 31, 2023, 4% for periods ending on or after that date and 3% for periods ending on or after December 31, 2024, and it is repealed for taxable periods beginning after December 31, 2024.
   - citation: NH DRA, Interest & Dividends Tax page; DRA 2026 Annual Report, "Historical Summary of Interest and Dividends Tax, RSA 77," p. 65 (2023, chapter 79:85-88)
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/taxes-glance/interest-dividends-tax
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw dra-interest-dividends-tax.html; DRA's Technical Information Release TIR 2025-001 is linked from that page
8. Interest and dividends tax revenue fell from $93.1 million in fiscal year 2025 to $2.0 million in fiscal year 2026, the first full fiscal year after the repeal; none of it is transferred to the Education Trust Fund in DRA's breakdown.
   - citation: NH DRA, 2026 Annual Report, p. 42 and p. 25 ("FY 2026 was the first full fiscal year following the repeal of the Interest and Dividends Tax")
   - source: https://www.revenue.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt736/files/documents/annual-report-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: DRA's percent-change cell for this row reads "-4555.00%", an error in the source; not used

---

# Local Control, School Budgets and Who Decides

**Sources and notes for this section**

- Researched 2026-11-11. Eight topics, listed below with their fact counts.
- Primary sources: General Court bill dockets, status pages and bill texts (gc.nh.gov, read before the site stopped answering, or from Wayback snapshots); the RSA chapter pages for RSA 194-C and 198 saved from gc.nh.gov on 2026-11-08; the Secretary of State's 2026 ballot-question PDF and unofficial 2026 general election tallies (sos.nh.gov spreadsheets); Merrimack County Superior Court and NH Supreme Court orders; governor press releases (live pages and a Wayback snapshot); NH Department of Education pages and State Board minutes; the NH Constitution (nh.gov) and the Executive Council's own site; Claremont School Board minutes (SAU 6) and Kearsarge Regional School Board minutes.
- News source cited: WMUR's unofficial district tally of Question 2 (no official, certified results exist yet).
- Review dates: 2027-08-15 for laws and the constitution; 2027-05-11 for uncertified results and pending local processes; 2027-11-11 for everything else.
- Raw copies: `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<slug>/` for each slug below.
- HB 564 (SAU budgets as a separate warrant article) is 2026 chapter 153, which the existing topic budget-sau-budget-vote already covers, so it is not repeated here.

## budget-hb-1300-school-tax-cap: HB 1300 (2026) and Question 2: The School Tax Cap and SAU Spending Cap

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1300 (2026) and Question 2: the school property tax cap and 6% SAU cap, the 60% vote, how caps are figured and overridden, dates, and the court challenge.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1300 (2026) was signed by Governor Ayotte on July 15, 2026 and became 2026 chapter 324, effective September 1, 2026, except for its repeal section, which takes effect January 1, 2032. The House passed it 177-160 on March 11, 2026, and both chambers adopted the final committee of conference version on June 4, 2026 (Senate 15-8, House 183-170).
   - citation: HB 1300 (2026), chapter 324; bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=2870&sy=2026&txtsessionyear=2026
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Prime sponsors Rep. Aidan Ankarberg and Sen. Daniel Innis (status page). Raw: hb1300-docket.html, hb1300-status.html, hb1300-billinfo.html (billinfo.aspx?id=2080 shows chapter 324).
2. Under RSA 32:5-i, created by HB 1300 (2026), every town and city ward in New Hampshire must vote on a school district local tax cap and a fixed cap on the SAU central office budget at the November 2026 and November 2028 state general elections. The question appears on the ballot by operation of law, with no warrant article, petition or local vote needed to put it there.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, I (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: hb1300-chaptered-text.html (chaptered final version). The new section was not checked in the online RSA because gc.nh.gov stopped answering during this session.
3. The 2026 ballot printed the school tax cap as Question 2, "Statutory Question required by HB 1300, Chapter 324, 2026," and asked: "Shall the [name of municipality] limit property tax growth for [name(s) of school district(s)] under RSA 32:5-i? If adopted for a two-year period: (1) the local property tax levy may not grow beyond the prior year's amount, adjusted for inflation and new construction; (2) SAU central office spending may not exceed 6 percent of total school district appropriations; and (3) bonded capital costs are excluded from both limits. These caps apply only to administrative operations of the SAU central office and do not affect classroom instruction, school-based services, or other municipal expenditures. These limits may be overridden as provided in RSA 32:5-i. Adoption requires a three-fifths (3/5) majority vote."
   - citation: NH Secretary of State, 2026 General Election ballot questions, Question 2; RSA 32:5-i, V
   - source: https://mm.nh.gov/files/uploads/sos/docs/questions-on-general-election-ballot-2026-for-web.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: sos-ballot-questions-2026.pdf. Wording matches RSA 32:5-i, V in the chaptered text, except the PDF's line-break artifact "school -based".
4. Under RSA 32:5-i, the school tax cap binds a school district if three-fifths (60%) of the voters voting in all the towns and wards the district serves approve, and the SAU spending cap binds an SAU if three-fifths of the voters voting across all the towns and wards the SAU serves approve. Moderators report the results to the secretary of the SAU board, who certifies them to the Department of Revenue Administration.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, VI(a)-(c) (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Because the SAU vote is counted across all member districts, one question can produce no cap, one cap or both caps in a given district; the Superior Court's September 16, 2026 order walks through this (see the court facts below).
5. Under New Hampshire's school tax cap (RSA 32:5-i), a district's maximum allowable levy is the prior fiscal year's property tax levy, not counting bonded capital costs, multiplied by (1 + inflation + net new taxable property growth). "Inflation" is the 12-month change in the Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers, Northeast Region, published by the Bureau of Labor Statistics, using the March figure of the prior fiscal year.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, VIII(a)-(b) (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
6. Under RSA 32:5-i, "net new taxable property growth" in the school tax cap counts the rise in assessed value from new construction, expansions or improvements, subdivision or redevelopment, and property changing from exempt to taxable. It does not count market appreciation, revaluation or reassessment, or changes in assessment method, and the Department of Revenue Administration certifies it each year for each taxing jurisdiction.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, VIII(c)-(d) (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
7. Where adopted, New Hampshire's SAU cap limits SAU central office administrative budgets to 6% of the total appropriations of the SAU's school districts, not counting bonded capital projects. The cap covers superintendent and assistant superintendent services, business administration, human resources, finance, payroll, purchasing, district-level IT administration, legal services, public relations and similar central functions; it does not cover school-based administrators, classroom instruction, instructional support, special education, transportation, food service or building operations.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, IX (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The statute also counts district-level curriculum directors and similar staff who are not under a collective bargaining agreement or who teach less than half their time, and anyone reported to NH DOE as central office staff.
8. Under RSA 32:5-i, the "bonded capital costs" left out of both caps are principal and interest on bonds or notes issued solely to buy, build or make major structural renovations to real property, and only the portion the Department of Revenue Administration certifies each year.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, XVI (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
9. Under RSA 32:5-i, a district with an annual meeting can override the school tax cap or SAU cap by a ballot vote with a three-fifths "yes" majority of those voting yes or no; the vote is not on the officers' ballot unless the district uses official ballot (SB2) voting or a charter. A city district without an annual meeting overrides a cap by the supermajority set in its charter.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, X(a)-(b) (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
10. Under RSA 32:5-i, the Department of Revenue Administration may not approve a tax rate that exceeds a district's certified cap until the district shows its budget is within the cap or that a valid override vote was taken; without an override, the governing body must cut the appropriation by the amount over the cap. Any taxpayer in a member district may go to superior court to enforce the law.
    - citation: RSA 32:5-i, XI and XIII (2026, 324:1)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
11. A school tax cap or SAU cap approved in November 2026 applies beginning with the fiscal year 2028 budget, and one approved in November 2028 applies beginning with fiscal year 2030; RSA 32:5-i expires on January 1, 2032, when any cap adopted under it ends.
    - citation: RSA 32:5-i, XII and XVII; 2026, 324:3-4
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: Fiscal year 2028 runs July 1, 2027 to June 30, 2028, so for most districts the first capped budget is the one voted at the March 2027 annual meetings (derived from RSA 197:1, which has annual meetings appropriate money for the fiscal year starting the next July 1).
12. In Bates and Wieser v. Scanlan (Merrimack County Superior Court, No. 217-2026-CV-00671), Judge Daniel St. Hilaire on September 16, 2026 denied a request to keep Question 2 off the November 2026 ballot, finding the plaintiffs had not shown they were likely to win their claims that the question was confusing, misleading or wrongly combined two caps. The order called the plaintiffs' evidence about the caps' effect on classrooms "speculative and uncertain."
    - citation: Bates v. Scanlan, No. 217-2026-CV-00671, Order on Motion for Preliminary Injunctive Relief (Sept. 16, 2026)
    - source: https://npr.brightspotcdn.com/fc/fa/e14cc7c8487c894d257ea20c7a02/court-order-09-15-26.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11
    - note: Court order PDF posted by NHPR; the order is signed and served September 16, 2026 (the file name says 09-15-26). It denied a preliminary injunction; it was not a final judgment. Plaintiffs: Warner taxpayer David Bates and Campton School Board member Kelly Wieser. Raw: superior-court-order-2026-09-15.pdf.
13. The New Hampshire Supreme Court (Case No. 2026-0612, Petition of David Bates & a.) denied an emergency request to block the Question 2 ballots on September 17, 2026, and on September 28, 2026 denied the petition asking it to take the case, without giving reasons.
    - citation: Petition of David Bates & a., No. 2026-0612, orders of Sept. 17 and Sept. 28, 2026
    - source: https://www.courts.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt471/files/documents/2026-09/final-order-in-petition-of-david-bates-and-kelly-wieser.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11
    - note: Sept. 28 order: "Petition for original jurisdiction is denied. See Rule 11." Sept. 17 order (single justice, Gould, J.): "emergency motion for temporary restraining order is denied," posted by NHPR at https://npr.brightspotcdn.com/83/2a/db9d12234c2f992b184d750bf427/petition-of-david-bates-and-kelly-wieser.pdf. Raw: nhsc-final-order-bates-wieser.pdf, nhsc-petition-bates-wieser.pdf.

## budget-question-2-2026-results: Question 2 (2026) Results: Where the School Tax Cap Passed

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: Unofficial November 2026 Question 2 (school tax cap) results: how many districts and SAUs reached 60%, the largest districts, close calls and certification.

1. By WMUR's unofficial tally, updated November 9, 2026, 93 of the 179 New Hampshire school districts it listed voted 60% or more "yes" on Question 2, the school tax cap, and 86 fell short. WMUR added the town and ward results for multi-town districts using Secretary of State returns.
   - citation: WMUR, "Map: See town-by-town Question 2 results" (updated Nov. 9, 2026)
   - source: https://www.wmur.com/article/town-district-question-2-results-nh-110926/74379244
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Unofficial news tally, cited because no certified results exist yet (the Secretary of State's results page listed no Question 2 tallies on 2026-11-11). Counts are from the parsed table question_two_2026_districts.csv (179 rows, 93 PASS). Do not add district votes into a statewide total: towns in more than one district would be counted twice. Raw: wmur-question-2-results-2026-11-09.html and the two CSVs.
2. WMUR's unofficial Question 2 tally also adds up votes by SAU, which decides the 6% SAU spending cap; 60 of the 107 SAU totals on its page show 60% or more "yes" (derived count).
   - citation: WMUR, "Map: See town-by-town Question 2 results" (updated Nov. 9, 2026)
   - source: https://www.wmur.com/article/town-district-question-2-results-nh-110926/74379244
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Derived by counting the distinct "SAU n" lines on the saved page (107 labels, 60 marked PASS). Unofficial; see fact 1.
3. In Manchester, Question 2 drew 20,827 "yes" and 13,919 "no" votes, or 59.9% yes by WMUR's unofficial tally, just under the 60% needed for the school tax cap.
   - citation: WMUR, "Map: See town-by-town Question 2 results" (updated Nov. 9, 2026)
   - source: https://www.wmur.com/article/town-district-question-2-results-nh-110926/74379244
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - place: district Manchester
   - note: Unofficial; see fact 1. WMUR lists the same totals for SAU 37.
4. In Nashua, Question 2 drew 18,829 "yes" and 13,235 "no" votes, or 58.7% yes by WMUR's unofficial tally, below the 60% needed for the school tax cap.
   - citation: WMUR, "Map: See town-by-town Question 2 results" (updated Nov. 9, 2026)
   - source: https://www.wmur.com/article/town-district-question-2-results-nh-110926/74379244
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - place: district Nashua
   - note: Unofficial; see fact 1. WMUR lists the same totals for SAU 42.
5. By WMUR's unofficial Question 2 tally, 10 school districts fell short of 60% by less than one percentage point: Somersworth (59.0%), Warren (59.1%), Bedford (59.2%), Hollis (59.3%), Brentwood (59.5%), New Boston (59.5%), Litchfield (59.7%), Chichester (59.8%), Manchester (59.9%) and Wilton-Lyndeborough Cooperative (59.9%).
   - citation: WMUR, "Map: See town-by-town Question 2 results" (updated Nov. 9, 2026)
   - source: https://www.wmur.com/article/town-district-question-2-results-nh-110926/74379244
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Derived by filtering question_two_2026_districts.csv for yes shares from 59.0 to 59.9. Litchfield's 2,386 yes and 1,614 no compute to 59.65%, which WMUR shows as 59.7%. Unofficial; see fact 1.
6. Under RSA 32:5-i, any 10 registered voters could ask for a recount of the Question 2 vote within 7 days of the November 3, 2026 election, in writing to the school district clerk (for the tax cap) or the SAU board secretary (for the SAU cap), with a $10 fee; the recount must be held 5 to 10 days after the petition is received.
   - citation: RSA 32:5-i, VII(a)-(b) (2026, 324:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2080&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw copy is in raw/state-issues/budget-hb-1300-school-tax-cap/hb1300-chaptered-text.html. No recount requests were found.
7. WMUR's Question 2 results page says results for each school district "will not be final until each school board ratifies the results in their constituent city/town(s) and submits the results to the Department of Revenue Administration."
   - citation: WMUR, "Map: See town-by-town Question 2 results" (updated Nov. 9, 2026)
   - source: https://www.wmur.com/article/town-district-question-2-results-nh-110926/74379244
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: The statute has the SAU board secretary certify results to the Department of Revenue Administration (RSA 32:5-i, VI(c)); see budget-hb-1300-school-tax-cap fact 4.

## budget-hb-1610-unassigned-funds-veto: HB 1610 (2026): Keeping Unused School Funds (Vetoed)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1610 (2026) on districts keeping unused year-end funds (fund balance), the Governor's veto and the failed override.

1. Under current New Hampshire law, voters at a school district meeting may authorize the district, "indefinitely until specific rescission," to keep unused year-end unassigned general funds, as long as the total kept does not exceed 5% of the current fiscal year's net assessment under RSA 198:5.
   - citation: RSA 198:4-b, II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-b.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-198-mrg-2026-11-08.htm (chapter 198 page saved from gc.nh.gov on 2026-11-08; gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11).
2. Under current New Hampshire law, a school board must hold a public hearing, with notice published at least 7 days ahead, before spending retained unassigned funds, and must present and publish an annual accounting of those funds.
   - citation: RSA 198:4-b, II(a)-(b)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-b.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
3. New Hampshire's HB 1610 (2026) would have required voters to authorize keeping unassigned funds every year instead of indefinitely, and would have lowered the limit from 5% of the net assessment to 3% of the current year's total budget appropriation. Retained funds could have been spent only for an "unusual circumstance" under RSA 32:11.
   - citation: HB 1610 (2026), final version, section 1 (amending RSA 198:4-b, II)
   - source: https://web.archive.org/web/20260909094627/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1727&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-09
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: hb1610-final-text-wayback-20260909.html. The final text also struck the hearing and annual-report subparagraphs (a) and (b).
4. HB 1610 (2026) would have cancelled every existing indefinite authorization to keep unassigned funds and applied to tax rates for the fiscal year ending June 30, 2027; it would have taken effect on passage.
   - citation: HB 1610 (2026), final version, sections 2-3
   - source: https://web.archive.org/web/20260909094627/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1727&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-09
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
5. The New Hampshire House passed HB 1610 (2026) 182-167 on March 5, 2026; the Senate passed an amended version by voice vote on May 7, 2026; and the House agreed to the Senate version 186-163 on May 21, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1610 (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=2523&sy=2026&txtsessionyear=2026
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Prime sponsor Rep. John Sellers; House Education Funding Committee majority report 10-7, Senate Education Finance Committee 4-2. Raw: hb1610-docket.html, hb1610-status.html, hb1610-billinfo.html.
6. Governor Ayotte vetoed HB 1610 (2026) on July 2, 2026. Her veto statement said: "Unfortunately, this bill goes into effect immediately and as a result, will disrupt the will of voters in 95 school districts in New Hampshire who have already decided under current law how to utilize their year-end unassigned funds. For this reason, I have vetoed House Bill 1610."
   - citation: Office of the Governor, "Governor Signs 74 Bills into Law" (July 2, 2026), veto statement for HB 1610
   - source: https://web.archive.org/web/20260703005145/https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-74-bills-law
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-07-03
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: governor.nh.gov refused direct downloads (Access Denied). Raw: governor-signs-74-bills-wayback-20260703.html. The docket also records "Vetoed by Governor Ayotte 07/02/2026."
7. On August 19, 2026, the New Hampshire House voted 162-148 to override the HB 1610 veto, short of the two-thirds needed, so the veto was sustained and HB 1610 did not become law.
   - citation: HB 1610 (2026), bill docket ("Veto Sustained 08/19/2026: RC 162-148 Lacking Necessary Two-Thirds Vote HJ 16 P. 48")
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=2523&sy=2026&txtsessionyear=2026
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

## gov-sau-oversight-2026: SAU Study Commission and School Audits (SB 574 and SB 586, 2026)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: SB 574 (2026), a commission studying SAU efficiency and consolidation, and SB 586 (2026), new independent audits of SAUs, charter schools and some districts.

1. Governor Ayotte's July 2, 2026 release listed SB 574 ("Establishing a commission to study the efficiency and structure of school administrative units") and SB 586 (requiring independent audits of chartered public schools, SAUs and some cities and school districts) among the bills she signed that week.
   - citation: Office of the Governor, "Governor Signs 74 Bills into Law" (July 2, 2026)
   - source: https://web.archive.org/web/20260703005145/https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-74-bills-law
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-07-03
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw copy is in raw/state-issues/budget-hb-1610-unassigned-funds-veto/governor-signs-74-bills-wayback-20260703.html. Chapter numbers come from the RSA source notes (2026, 268 and 2026, 272).
2. New Hampshire's SB 574 (2026), chapter 268, created a commission attached to the Department of Education to study the efficiency of existing school system structures, effective July 2, 2026.
   - citation: RSA 194-C:13, I (2026, 268:1, eff. July 2, 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/194-C/194-C-13.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-194-C-mrg-2026-11-08.htm (chapter 194-C page saved from gc.nh.gov on 2026-11-08).
3. New Hampshire's SAU study commission has two senators, three representatives, the education commissioner or a designee, and one member each named by the NH School Administrators Association (a superintendent), the NH School Boards Association (a school board member), the NH Association of School Business Officials (a business administrator) and the School District Governance Association of New Hampshire.
   - citation: RSA 194-C:13, II (2026, 268:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/194-C/194-C-13.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. New Hampshire's SAU study commission was charged with reviewing enrollment shifts, identifying the number of SAUs and each SAU's cost per pupil and how SAUs can adjust resources to help reduce property taxes, identifying states that have consolidated or restructured school systems, and examining obstacles to consolidation and possible school closures.
   - citation: RSA 194-C:13, IV (2026, 268:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/194-C/194-C-13.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. New Hampshire's SAU study commission was due to report its findings and any proposed legislation to legislative leaders, the governor and the state library by November 1, 2026, the same date its law (RSA 194-C:13) was repealed.
   - citation: RSA 194-C:13, VI; 2026, 268:2-3
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/194-C/194-C-13.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Whether the report was filed was not found; see Not retrieved.
6. Since July 1, 2026, New Hampshire's SB 586 (2026), chapter 272, has required chartered public schools, SAUs, and cities or school districts not audited under RSA 671:5 to be audited by an independent public accountant, and has added SAUs to the governing bodies that must file reports with the commissioner of education.
   - citation: RSA 198:4-d, intro. and VI (repealed and reenacted by 2026, 272:1, eff. July 1, 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-d.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-198-mrg-2026-11-08.htm. The bill was approved July 2, 2026 with a stated effective date of July 1, 2026.
7. Before SB 586 (2026), RSA 198:4-d did not list SAUs and only said that a city, school district or chartered public school that was audited by an independent public accountant had to submit a copy of its audited financial statements.
   - citation: RSA 198:4-d, V, as in effect in September 2025
   - source: https://web.archive.org/web/20250924221801/https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-d.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-09-24
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-198-4-d-wayback-20250924.htm.
8. Under New Hampshire's school audit law, audit reports must include balance sheets, revenue sources, an itemized list of all employees with their pay, benefits, retiree health and pension liabilities, and an itemized list of assets, debts, bonds, leases and capital projects.
   - citation: RSA 198:4-d, VIII (2026, 272:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-d.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
9. Under New Hampshire's school audit law, the audit report for the last fiscal year is due to the Department of Education within 9 months after the year ends; the commissioner checks it for completeness within 3 months, and deficiencies must be corrected within 12 months of submission.
   - citation: RSA 198:4-d, VIII (2026, 272:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-d.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
10. Under New Hampshire's school audit law, the education commissioner must withhold state grant money, other than adequacy aid under RSA 198:40-a, from any city, school district, chartered public school or SAU whose audit or financial report is not complete and submitted, and the reports must be posted publicly in a searchable format.
    - citation: RSA 198:4-d, VIII-IX (2026, 272:1)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-4-d.htm
    - as_of: 2026-11-08
    - review_by: 2027-08-15

## gov-school-closures-2026: School Closures: HB 1374 (2026) History, Claremont's Bluff School and Kearsarge Regional

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How HB 1374 (2026), the school closure vote law, passed, and recent closures: Claremont's Bluff Elementary and Kearsarge's Sutton and Simonds plans.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1374 (2026), prime sponsor Rep. Mary Murphy, passed the House 179-149 on March 11, 2026 and the Senate with an amendment 14-8 on May 7, 2026; both chambers adopted the conference version on June 4, 2026 (Senate by voice vote, House 179-161). Governor Ayotte signed it on July 10, 2026 as chapter 303, effective October 8, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1374 (2026), chapter 303; bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=3033&sy=2026&txtsessionyear=2026
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The House vote on March 11 was a division vote. Final title: "modifying the procedures for withdrawal from a cooperative school district and the discontinuance of elementary and high schools and requiring the review of school district operating documents by school boards." Raw: hb1374-docket.html, hb1374-status.html. The closure rules themselves are in gov-school-closure-votes.
2. New Hampshire's SB 588 (2026), "requiring that all municipalities that are served by a school district vote on the closure of schools," was sent to interim study by the Senate on February 19, 2026 after a 5-0 committee recommendation, and did not pass on its own; HB 1374 (2026) is the law that contains the closure-vote rules.
   - citation: SB 588-FN (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=2188&sy=2026&txtsessionyear=2026
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Prime sponsor Sen. Daniel Innis. Raw: sb588-docket.html, sb588-status.html.
3. On September 17, 2025, the Claremont School Board voted 4-1 "to move the Bluff students, faculty, and classrooms fully intact to Disnard and Maple Avenue with a target date of October 20th pending and inspection of the building by the Claremont Fire Department," closing Bluff Elementary School.
   - citation: Claremont School Board, approved minutes of September 17, 2025
   - source: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1juOuijO6gh-JV8brbPFDM9GrsyirxjKXGjqK_DrKtNo
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - place: district Claremont
   - note: Minutes posted in SAU 6's "2025 Meeting Minutes" folder (https://drive.google.com/drive/folders/1b2-sN2brzqDzDb1Io-xZXop7U1zIAPp-), linked from sau6.org/school-boards. In favor: Madden, Crawford, Hawkins, Petrin; opposed: Howard; Chair Whitney absent. This was a board decision before the October 8, 2026 voter-approval rule. Raw: claremont-csb-minutes-2025-09-17-approved.txt/.pdf.
4. At the Claremont School Board's September 17, 2025 meeting, business administrator Matt Angell said the option to close Bluff School was about staff capacity.
   - citation: Claremont School Board, approved minutes of September 17, 2025
   - source: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1juOuijO6gh-JV8brbPFDM9GrsyirxjKXGjqK_DrKtNo
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - place: district Claremont
   - note: Minutes: "Matt Angel confirmed that the option to close Bluff School is about staff capacity." The minutes also record that an earlier plan was dropped after bus driver resignations.
5. On January 21, 2026, the Claremont School Board voted unanimously to place on the March 2026 warrant, and recommend, an article giving the board permission to sell or lease the former Bluff School property.
   - citation: Claremont School Board, approved minutes of January 21, 2026
   - source: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1b2siubh6uiLFNznoP2QSYShccWGuSsjjNQbHEM4Op_8
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - place: district Claremont
   - note: The board placed a similar article for 52 Maple Ave (the former Masonic Temple). Raw: claremont-csb-minutes-2026-01-21-approved.txt. The official March 2026 vote count was not found (see Not retrieved).
6. On March 18, 2026, the Claremont School Board discussed a request for qualifications for a commercial real estate broker to sell the Bluff and Masonic Lodge properties.
   - citation: Claremont School Board, approved minutes of March 18, 2026
   - source: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1mT4gKOwV7P2PoCoP_D7pUP6XyJ-TdYSOH3pqWd0CCOc
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - place: district Claremont
   - note: Raw: claremont-csb-minutes-2026-03-18-approved.txt. On April 15, 2026, a board member said the Bluff building could not be used "because it was voted on to be sold" (claremont-csb-minutes-2026-04-15-approved.txt).
7. On September 3, 2026, the Kearsarge Regional School Board voted 7-2 to adopt a two-school elementary configuration as its long-term goal, then voted 7-1 and 7-2 to direct the superintendent to prepare for closing Sutton Central Elementary School and Simonds Elementary School "effective at the end of the 2026-2027/2027-2028 school year subject to final closure authorization by this Board."
   - citation: Kearsarge Regional School Board, approved minutes of September 3, 2026
   - source: https://www.kearsarge.org/sites/g/files/vyhlif731/f/pages/3_september_2026_meeting_minutes_approved.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - place: district Kearsarge Regional
   - note: Sutton Central is in Sutton and Simonds is in Warner. One member recused himself from the Sutton Central vote. The minutes list two paths to consolidation, "Via citizen petition under HB 1374 or via Board-recommendation." Raw: 3_september_2026_meeting_minutes_approved.pdf.
8. On October 8, 2026, the Kearsarge Regional School Board was told that a petition under HB 1374 to close the Sutton school had been received and its signatures were being validated, that the district had to hold a meeting within 60 days, and it voted 5-4 to hold that meeting on December 5, 2026.
   - citation: Kearsarge Regional School Board, approved minutes of October 8, 2026
   - source: https://www.kearsarge.org/sites/g/files/vyhlif731/f/pages/8_october_2026_meeting_minutes_approved.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - place: district Kearsarge Regional
   - note: The minutes say "A petition was received to close Sutton Elementary School"; the district's school in Sutton is Sutton Central School. The November 5, 2026 agenda lists "HB1374 Dec 5 Special Hearing" (raw: krsd-agenda-2026-11-05.pdf). Outcome pending.
9. On October 22, 2026, the Kearsarge Regional School Board voted 5-1-1 to change its long-term goal to a three-school elementary configuration (New London, Bradford and Simonds), keeping its direction to plan for closing Sutton Central, and then voted 7-0-1 to stop planning for the closure of Simonds Elementary School.
   - citation: Kearsarge Regional School Board, approved minutes of October 22, 2026
   - source: https://www.kearsarge.org/sites/g/files/vyhlif731/f/pages/22_october_2026_meeting_minutes_approved.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - place: district Kearsarge Regional
   - note: The motion says the superintendent "shall continue planning for a closure under HB 1374 in the event that a lawful petition is received and approved by the voters" and that nothing in it "constitutes final closure authorization for any school." The Simonds motion says planning stops "unless and until the Board provides further direction." Raw: 22_october_2026_meeting_minutes_approved.pdf.

## gov-2026-election-results: The 2026 Election: Governor, Legislature and Veto Overrides

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: Unofficial November 2026 NH election results for governor, Senate and House, whether they're certified, and the two-thirds vote needed to override a veto.

1. The New Hampshire Secretary of State's 2026 general election results page says its tallies "accurately reflect the returns of votes reported by clerks but are subject to change if clerks submit amendments"; as of November 11, 2026 the results were not described as certified.
   - citation: NH Secretary of State, 2026 General Election Results
   - source: https://www.sos.nh.gov/2026-general-election-results
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Raw: sos-2026-ge-results-page-2026-11-11.html and a Wayback copy of 2026-11-06. sos.nh.gov blocks plain downloads; files were fetched through a headless browser.
2. In the Secretary of State's unofficial 2026 tallies for New Hampshire governor, Kelly Ayotte (Republican) received 324,734 votes, Cinde Warmington (Democrat) 296,834, Stephen Villee 9,630, and write-ins 745.
   - citation: NH Secretary of State, 2026 General Election, Governor summary by county
   - source: https://www.sos.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt561/files/media/media_document/2026-ge-governor-summary.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Spreadsheet "TOTALS" row; party letters in the file are d, r and l. Unofficial; see fact 1. Raw: 2026-ge-governor-summary.xlsx.
3. In the Secretary of State's unofficial 2026 tallies for the New Hampshire Senate, Democratic candidates led in 15 of the 24 districts and Republican candidates in 9 (derived).
   - citation: NH Secretary of State, 2026 General Election, State Senate district results
   - source: https://www.sos.nh.gov/2026-general-election-results
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Derived from the "TOTALS" rows of the 15 state-senate spreadsheets linked from the results page (raw: 2026-ge-state-senate-*.xlsx). Closest margins: District 8 (Jim Creighton, r, 12,539; David Trumble, d, 12,150) and District 14 (Sharon M. Carson, r, 12,959; Timothy T. Wyatt, d, 12,482). Unofficial; see fact 1.
4. In the Secretary of State's unofficial 2026 tallies for the 400-seat New Hampshire House, Democratic candidates led for 295 seats and Republican candidates for 98 (derived); in 9 districts the last winning and first losing candidates were 20 votes or fewer apart.
   - citation: NH Secretary of State, 2026 General Election, State Representatives results by county
   - source: https://www.sos.nh.gov/2026-general-election-results
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Derived by taking the top N vote-getters in each of 203 districts (N = seats shown in each district heading) from the 10 county spreadsheets (raw: 2026-ge-house-*.xlsx; script logic: recount columns used where present, as in Strafford District 1). The other 7 seats: 4 led by candidates labeled i, i&d or r&d, and 3 in Rockingham Districts 13 and 25, which had fewer named candidates than seats. Unofficial and may change after recounts; see fact 1.
5. Under the New Hampshire Constitution, a bill the governor vetoes becomes law only if two-thirds of the House and two-thirds of the Senate vote to pass it again, with each member's yes or no vote recorded in the journal.
   - citation: N.H. Const. pt. II, art. 44
   - source: https://www.nh.gov/glance/state-constitution/executive-power-governor
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: With all 24 senators voting, two-thirds is 16 votes; with all 400 representatives voting, it is 267 (derived). HB 1610's 2026 override failed 162-148 in the House as "Lacking Necessary Two-Thirds Vote" (budget-hb-1610-unassigned-funds-veto fact 7). Raw copy is in raw/state-issues/gov-executive-council-education/nh-constitution-executive-power-governor.html.

## gov-executive-council-education: The Executive Council's Role in Education

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: What NH's Executive Council does for schools (confirming the commissioner and State Board, approving contracts) and its unofficial 2026 election results.

1. The New Hampshire Constitution provides for five executive councilors, elected by ballot every two years from five districts of roughly equal population, "for advising the governor in the executive part of government."
   - citation: N.H. Const. pt. II, arts. 60 and 65
   - source: https://www.nh.gov/glance/state-constitution/council
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: nh-constitution-council.html.
2. Under the New Hampshire Constitution, the governor and the Executive Council "shall have a negative on each other, both in the nominations and appointments," so a governor's nominee needs the council's approval.
   - citation: N.H. Const. pt. II, art. 47
   - source: https://www.nh.gov/glance/state-constitution/executive-power-governor
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: For education, RSA 21-N:3 (commissioner) and RSA 21-N:10 (State Board of Education members) apply this; see gov-state-board-and-department.
3. According to the New Hampshire Executive Council, the governor "nominates" and the council "confirms" unclassified state employees, including commissioners, deputy commissioners and assistant commissioners, and the governor and council appoint more than 300 citizens to state boards and commissions each two-year term.
   - citation: NH Executive Council, "About Us"
   - source: https://council.nh.gov/about-us
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: council-about-us.html (rendered in a headless browser; the site blocks plain downloads).
4. Under the New Hampshire Constitution, no money may be paid out of the state treasury except by a warrant signed by the governor "by and with the advice and consent of the council," following the acts of the Legislature.
   - citation: N.H. Const. pt. II, art. 56
   - source: https://www.nh.gov/glance/state-constitution/executive-power-governor
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. According to the New Hampshire Executive Council, every state department, including the Department of Education, must get Governor and Council approval to accept and spend state and federal funds, to transfer money within its budget, and for contracts with a value of $10,000 or more, under the Department of Administrative Services Manual of Procedures, Section V.
   - citation: NH Executive Council, "About Us" (citing DAS Manual of Procedures, Section V, 2018)
   - source: https://council.nh.gov/about-us
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The page's wording is "all personal service contracts with a value of $10,000 and all contracts with a value of $10,000." The Department of Education is not named on the page; it is a state department covered by the rule.
6. In the Secretary of State's unofficial 2026 tallies for the New Hampshire Executive Council, the Democratic candidate led in all five districts: Luz Bay (64,227 to Joseph D. Kenney's 60,500) in District 1, Karen Liot Hill (81,364 to Tobin Menard's 44,197) in District 2, Lisa Kennedy Sheldon (70,251 to Janet Stevens's 64,617) in District 3, Jim O'Connell (60,540 to John Stephen's 52,547) in District 4, and Melanie Levesque (64,712 to Dave Wheeler's 55,615) in District 5.
   - citation: NH Secretary of State, 2026 General Election, Executive Council districts 1-5
   - source: https://www.sos.nh.gov/2026-general-election-results
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: From the "TOTALS" rows of 2026-ge-executive-council-1.xlsx through -5.xlsx (raw copies in raw/state-issues/gov-2026-election-results/). Unofficial and not certified as of 2026-11-11; the page says tallies may change if clerks submit amendments.

## gov-education-commissioner-and-state-board: The Education Commissioner (Caitlin Davis) and the Current State Board

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's education commissioner, Caitlin Davis (Frank Edelblut's successor in 2025), her confirmation, and the current State Board of Education members.

1. On June 10, 2025, Governor Kelly Ayotte announced she would nominate Caitlin Davis, then Director of Education Analytics and Resources at the New Hampshire Department of Education, as the state's next education commissioner.
   - citation: Office of the Governor, "Governor Ayotte Announces Caitlin Davis as Choice for Next Education Commissioner" (June 10, 2025)
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-announces-caitlin-davis-choice-next-education-commissioner
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: governor-davis-nomination-2025-06-10.html (fetched through a headless browser; governor.nh.gov blocks plain downloads).
2. On June 12, 2025, the New Hampshire State Board of Education held the consultation on the new commissioner that RSA 21-N:3 requires, at which the governor's chief of staff, Chris Connelly, presented Caitlin Davis's nomination and qualifications.
   - citation: NH State Board of Education, minutes of June 12, 2025, item III
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/nh-sboe-mtg-minutes-6-12-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Agenda item: "CONSULTATION with the GOVERNOR - per RSA 21-N:3 (1) - Appointment of a new commissioner of education." Raw: nh-sboe-mtg-minutes-6-12-2025.pdf.
3. The Governor and Executive Council confirmed Caitlin Davis as New Hampshire's Commissioner of Education on July 30, 2025.
   - citation: Office of the Governor, "Governor Ayotte, School Leaders Praise Confirmation of Caitlin Davis as Commissioner of Education" (July 30, 2025)
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-school-leaders-praise-confirmation-caitlin-davis-commissioner-education
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The July 30, 2025 Governor and Council minutes list "Caitlin D. Davis, Concord, Effective: July 30, 2025, Succeeding: Frank Edelblut, Wilton, Term: March 23, 2029"; the copy read was on a non-government mirror (raw: gc-minutes-2025-07-30-mirror.pdf), so the term date is not stated as a fact. Raw: governor-davis-confirmation-2025-07-30.html.
4. According to the New Hampshire Department of Education, Commissioner Caitlin D. Davis was sworn in in August 2025, after 15 years at the department, including eight years as Director of Education Analytics and Resources.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, "About the Commissioner"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/who-we-are/commissioner/about
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The exact swearing-in date (reported elsewhere as August 5, 2025) was not found in a primary source. Raw: doe-commissioner-about.html.
5. The chair of the New Hampshire State Board of Education is Andrew (Drew) Cline of Bedford, who represents Executive Council District 4 and has served since May 3, 2017, with his current term ending January 31, 2029.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, "State Board of Education Members"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/who-we-are/state-board-of-education/state-board-education-members
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: doe-state-board-members.html.
6. The other district members of the New Hampshire State Board of Education are Kenneth (Ken) Garry of Middleton (District 1, term to January 31, 2028), James (Jim) Laboe of Hopkinton (District 2, to January 31, 2029), Philip Nazzaro of Newmarket (District 3, to January 31, 2030) and Rajesh Nair of Nashua (District 5, to January 31, 2028).
   - citation: NH Department of Education, "State Board of Education Members"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/who-we-are/state-board-of-education/state-board-education-members

7. The two at-large members of the New Hampshire State Board of Education are James (Jim) Fricchione of Windham, whose term ends January 31, 2027, and Ann Lane of Dover, whose term ends January 31, 2028.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, "State Board of Education Members"
