8. According to the New Hampshire Department of Education, State Board of Education members may serve up to three consecutive four-year terms, the board must hold at least six regular meetings a year, and it usually meets on the second Thursday of each month at the department's offices at 25 Hall Street in Concord.

# Special Education Aid, School Buildings, Medicaid and Federal Funding

**Sources and notes for this section**

- Sources: the General Court's bill status and bill text pages for HB 1563 (2026), the online RSA, HB 1 (2025, chapter 140) and HB 2 (2025, chapter 141) as chaptered; NH DOE special education aid files, child counts, state revenue and expenditure summaries, the school building aid ranked list and payment schedule, and the FY2027 adequacy aid file; Governor and Council minutes and DHHS's item of June 3, 2026; the Governor's Office; U.S. Department of Education budget documents, state tables, press releases and fact sheets; congress.gov (H.R. 7148 and CRS report R48970); and the U.S. Supreme Court docket.
- 7 topics, 66 facts: money-hb-1563-special-ed-aid-formula (10), money-special-education-costs-trends (11), money-school-building-aid-2026-27 (7), money-fcda-fy2027-amounts (6), federal-medicaid-to-schools-nh (9), federal-k12-funding-nh (11), federal-education-department-changes (12).
- Review dates: enacted NH law 2027-08-15; federal law 2027-11-11; proposals, the ranked waitlist and the interagency agreements still being carried out 2027-05-11; data and everything else 2027-11-11.
- Raw copies: `/Users/michaelkorthuis/workspace/education/additional-data/raw/state-issues/<slug>/`. gc.nh.gov stopped answering this computer partway through (empty replies from about 00:06 on 2026-11-11), so the 2025 HB 1 chaptered text and the NHED budget presentation were read from Wayback copies of their gc.nh.gov addresses, and three 2026 building aid bills are listed under Not retrieved.

## money-hb-1563-special-ed-aid-formula: HB 1563 (2026): How the Special Education Aid Bill Passed and What Else It Requires

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1563 (2026): the change to NH's special education (catastrophic) aid formula from July 2028, with its votes, cost estimates and new audits.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1563 (2026), "relative to the special education aid formula and the administration and monitoring of state special education aid," was signed by Governor Ayotte on July 2, 2026, became 2026 chapter 232, and takes effect July 1, 2028.
   - citation: HB 1563 (2026), chapter 232; docket entry of 07/02/2026
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1610&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: raw/state-issues/money-hb-1563-special-ed-aid-formula/hb1563-billinfo.html. The chaptered text says "Approved: July 02, 2026" and "Effective Date: July 01, 2028".
2. HB 1563 (2026) was sponsored by Representatives Ladd (prime), Erf, Peeples, D. McGuire, Popovici-Muller and S. Smith and Senators Sullivan, Murphy and Innis.
   - citation: HB 1563 (2026), chaptered final version, sponsors line
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1610&txtFormat=html&v=current
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
3. HB 1563 (2026) passed both chambers without a roll call: the House Education Funding Committee (18-0) and House Finance Committee (25-0) recommended it with amendments, the House passed it by voice vote on February 19 and March 26, 2026, and after the Senate Education Finance (4-0) and Finance (7-0) committees recommended it, the Senate passed it by voice vote on May 14, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1563 (2026) bill docket (HJ 5, HJ 9, SJ 10, SJ 12)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1610&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: House committee votes 02/10/2026 and 03/17/2026; Senate committee votes 04/23/2026 and 05/14/2026. An enrolled bill amendment (2026-2140e) was adopted by both chambers on 06/04/2026. The status page lists no House or Senate roll calls.
4. As introduced, HB 1563 (2026) used fixed dollar amounts instead of multiples of the state average cost per pupil: aid would have started at $60,000 per student, the district's 20% share would have run to $200,000, and state reimbursement would have been capped at $160,000 per pupil. The final law uses 2.5, 3.5 and 10 times the state average cost per pupil instead.
   - citation: HB 1563-FN-LOCAL as introduced, section 1 (RSA 186-C:18, III(a)-(c)); chaptered version, 232:2
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1610&txtFormat=html&v=HP
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: raw/state-issues/money-hb-1563-special-ed-aid-formula/hb1563-introduced.html. The final formula itself is already in the library (money-special-education-aid, fact 3).
5. HB 1563 (2026) states the legislature's intent "to reduce administrative burden on school districts and the department, expedite reimbursement of state special education aid, and ensure appropriate stewardship of state funds through a risk-based monitoring methodology."
   - citation: 2026, 232:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1610&txtFormat=html&v=current
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
6. From July 1, 2028, a New Hampshire school district seeking special education aid must submit a certified report for each student, giving a student identifier, the services provided under the student's IEP, the eligible spending itemized by category, the total reimbursement requested, and the superintendent's certification that the information is accurate and supporting records are kept. NH DOE pays the aid based on these reports.
   - citation: RSA 186-C:18-a, I-III (2026, 232:4, eff. July 1, 2028)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186-C/186-C-18-a.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
7. From July 1, 2028, NH DOE must run a risk-based monitoring program for special education aid claims that reviews at least 20% of claiming districts each year, so every district is reviewed at least once every 5 years, plus random and targeted reviews. If it finds systemic errors it may review past years, require an independent audit or school board certification, and it must adjust payments, recoup overpayments and may require corrective action plans.
   - citation: RSA 186-C:18-a, IV-VII (2026, 232:4, eff. July 1, 2028)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186-C/186-C-18-a.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
8. The Legislative Budget Assistant's fiscal note on HB 1563 (2026) gives the 2024-25 state average cost per pupil as $21,520, so the current 3.5 times threshold was $75,320 (aid paid in FY2026) and the new 2.5 times threshold would have been $53,800.
   - citation: HB 1563-FN-LOCAL fiscal note, as amended by the House (amendment 2026-1086h), methodology
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1610&txtFormat=html&v=SP
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/money-hb-1563-special-ed-aid-formula/hb1563-adopted-both-bodies.html (the "Version adopted by both bodies" page carries the fiscal note).
9. Using 2024-25 claims, NH DOE estimated in the HB 1563 (2026) fiscal note that 798 currently eligible students would gain about $2,575,933 in aid (about $3,228 each) from the new 15% state share between 2.5 and 3.5 times, while lowering the state share above 10 times to 90% would affect 64 students in 45 districts, saving the state $708,528 and costing those districts $459 to $49,000 per student, for a net increase of $1,934,153.
   - citation: HB 1563-FN-LOCAL fiscal note, methodology
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1610&txtFormat=html&v=SP
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: these figures cover only students already above 3.5 times; NH DOE does not collect data on students below that threshold.
10. The HB 1563 (2026) fiscal note says the bill's cost is "indeterminable" but, counting an estimated 1,000 to 3,000 more students between 2.5 and 3.5 times the average cost, it could raise state aid to districts by about $5 million to $12 million a year beginning in FY2029. It also notes NH DOE's estimate of $208,000 for two more staff positions, which the bill does not fund.
   - citation: HB 1563-FN-LOCAL fiscal note, fiscal impact table and methodology
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1610&txtFormat=html&v=SP
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

## money-special-education-costs-trends: Special Education Costs, Student Counts and Catastrophic Aid Payments in New Hampshire

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH students with IEPs, special program spending, and special education (catastrophic) aid claimed, paid and prorated each year, with the 2025 budget's changes.

1. As of October 1, 2025, New Hampshire counted 32,181 students with disabilities ages 3 to 21, down from 32,324 in 2024 and up from 30,309 in 2021; district enrollment in the same counts was 160,322 in 2025.
   - citation: NH DOE, Total Student Count Trends 2000-2025 (updated 5/1/26)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/2026/student-count-trends-10.1.25.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the 2025 count is 3,723 ages 3-5 and 28,458 ages 6-21. raw/state-issues/money-special-education-costs-trends/student-count-trends-10.1.25.pdf
2. In New Hampshire's October 1, 2025 count of students with disabilities, the largest primary disability categories were specific learning disability (9,952), other health impairment (6,340), developmental delay (4,993), autism (4,237) and speech or language impairment (3,418). The autism count has grown from 397 in 2000.
   - citation: NH DOE, Statewide Census by Disability, ages 3-21, 2000-2025 (updated 5/1/26)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/2026/student_census_by_disability_10.1.25.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: counts are by primary disability. The single-year table (state-census_10.1.25-hk.pdf) shows the same totals and 20,347 male and 11,834 female students.
3. NH DOE's statewide summary of school district finances shows spending on "Special Programs" of $802,759,963 in 2024-25 (20.4% of recurring expenditures), up from $636,480,150 (19.3%) in 2020-21, with $666,188,820 in 2021-22, $701,283,158 in 2022-23 and $749,808,675 in 2023-24.
   - citation: NH DOE, State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts, 2020-21 through 2024-25 (Distribution of Expenditures)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/summary-of-rev-exp-fy2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: earlier years from the fy2024, fy2023, fy2022 and fy2021 CSVs on the same NH DOE Financial Reports page. "Special Programs" is a DOE-25 instruction category, not total special education cost: a Dover public meeting document (https://publicrecords.dover.nh.gov/FileDownload/118700/Public_Meetings/0, saved as dover-public-meeting-sped-costs.pdf) quotes the DOE accounting handbook that it includes some non-special-education programs and excludes psychological, speech, occupational therapy and transportation services, which are reported elsewhere. Do not call it total special education spending.
4. For special education aid paid in FY2026 (on 2024-25 costs), New Hampshire districts reported 885 students with total costs of $132,761,168.26, of which $54,768,513.81 was eligible for state aid; the $49,917,000 appropriation was prorated at about 91.14%. 96 of the students had costs over 10 times the state average.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY '26 Sped Aid Disbursement - School Year 2024-2025
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy26-special-education-aid-amounts-website.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: district liability per student ("CAP") was $75,319.68. The HB 1563 fiscal note gives the same totals ($54.769M eligible, $49.917M appropriation, 91.14%). The fiscal note counts 907 students over 3.5 times the average for 2024-25; the DOE disbursement file reports 885.
5. For special education aid paid in FY2025 (on 2023-24 costs), New Hampshire districts reported 857 students with total costs of $119,129,962.37 and an entitlement of $49,303,241.19; the state paid $33,917,000, or 68.79% of the entitlement.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY25 special education aid (2023-2024 school year), state totals row
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-25-2023-2024-sped-aid-csv.csv
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
6. For special education aid paid in FY2024 (on 2022-23 costs), New Hampshire districts reported 762.5 students with total costs of $97,798,488.52 and an entitlement of $38,967,952.85; the state paid $33,917,000, prorated at 87.038%.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY24 special education aid (school year 2022-2023), grand total row
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-24-school-year-2022-2023-sped-aid-final.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: NH DOE's page lists this file under 2023-2024; the student count is shown as 762.5 in the source.
7. For special education aid paid in FY2023 (on 2021-22 costs), New Hampshire districts reported 760 students with total costs of $89,546,192.07 and an entitlement of $34,511,871.10, prorated at 98.2763% to $33,916,999.99.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY 23 Special Education, School Year 2021-2022, state totals row
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-23-school-year-2022-2023-sped-aid-final-no-students.csv
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the file name says 2022-2023 but its title row says "FY 23 Special Education School Year 2021-2022". Derived: total reported costs rose 48% from FY2023 to FY2026 ($89.5M to $132.8M).
8. New Hampshire's 2026-27 state budget (HB 1, 2025 chapter 140) appropriated $49,917,000 from the education trust fund for special education aid in each of FY2026 and FY2027, and directs any unspent balance at the end of a year to court-ordered placements. NH DOE's aid files show about $33.9 million distributed in each of FY2023, FY2024 and FY2025.
   - citation: HB 1 (2025), chapter 140, account 4073 Special Education Aid and class note; NH DOE aid files FY2023-FY2025
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%201%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-07-28
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov was unreachable on 2026-11-11; read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260728120058/ of this address. raw/state-issues/money-special-education-costs-trends/hb1-2025-chaptered-wayback-20260728.pdf (page 713).
9. HB 2 (2025, chapter 141) added the rule that, when special education aid is prorated, each district gets at least 80% of its entitlement, effective September 1, 2025, and removed the earlier rule sending unspent special education aid to court-ordered placements and episodes of treatment.
   - citation: 2025, 141:276 and 141:277 (RSA 186-C:18, III(a))
   - source: https://www.gc.nh.gov/LBA/Budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%202%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: raw/state-issues/money-special-education-costs-trends/hb2-2025-chaptered.pdf, page 78-79. The 80% floor itself is already in the library (money-special-education-aid, fact 2); this fact adds when and how it came in.
10. Since July 1, 2025, New Hampshire law says the amount needed to fund special education aid "is hereby appropriated" from the education trust fund and lets the governor draw a warrant regardless of the fund's balance, with the general fund covering any deficit.
   - citation: RSA 186-C:18, IV (2025, 141:278, eff. July 1, 2025)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186-C/186-C-18.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: NH DOE's FY2026 distribution (paid by January 1, 2026) was still prorated to the $49,917,000 appropriation (fact 4); the HB 1563 fiscal note describes this as a "Statutory Open Warrant". The sources do not explain the difference.
11. HB 1 (2025, chapter 140) also appropriated $4,500,000 a year for court-ordered placements, which may not be used for any other purpose, and authorizes the governor to draw more from the education trust fund if required placements cost more.
   - citation: HB 1 (2025), chapter 140, account 7722 Court Ordered Placements, class note (RSA 186-C:19-b)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%201%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-07-28
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

## money-school-building-aid-2026-27: School Building Aid in the 2026-27 Budget and the Waiting List of Projects

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH school building aid in the 2026-27 budget against earlier years, what FY2027's money pays for, and the ranked waiting list of school projects.

1. New Hampshire's 2026-27 state budget (HB 1, 2025 chapter 140) appropriated $14,762,183 for school building aid in FY2026 and $12,039,788 in FY2027, from the education trust fund, and says the appropriation does not lapse until June 30, 2027.
   - citation: HB 1 (2025), chapter 140, account 3479 School Building Aid and class note
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%201%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-07-28
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov was unreachable on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy. raw/state-issues/money-school-building-aid-2026-27/hb1-2025-chaptered-wayback-20260728.pdf (page 712).
2. NH DOE's April 2025 budget presentation lists budgeted school building aid of $38,500,000 in FY2020, $30,500,000 in FY2021, $56,972,728 in FY2022, $24,960,075 in FY2023, $43,183,728 in FY2024 and $43,400,528 in FY2025. Derived: the FY2026-27 total of $26,801,971 is about 69% less than the FY2024-25 total of $86,584,256.
   - citation: NH DOE, Senate budget hearing, FY 2026/2027 budget request (April 22, 2025), Budgeted Appropriations table
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/Senate_Finance/NHED%2026_27%20Budget.pdf

   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the agency, the governor and the House all proposed $14,762,183 and $12,039,788 for FY2026-27 in that table (the House moved the line to the general fund); the enacted amounts are in fact 1. raw/state-issues/money-school-building-aid-2026-27/nhed-26-27-budget-senate-finance-wayback-20250605.pdf
3. NH DOE's building aid payment schedule (last revised May 13, 2026) shows FY2027 payments of $19,582,541: $12,000,109 in continuing payments for projects begun before FY2013 and $7,582,432 in final payments for two newer projects.
   - citation: NH DOE, Building Aid Distributions FY27-FY41, "Tail & New Projects" summary
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/building-aid-all-payments-7-1-25.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: amounts rounded from the file. DOE notes the FY2027 new-project amounts assume the final payment falls in that year. The schedule lists no payments for new projects after FY2027.
4. The two newer projects in NH DOE's FY2027 building aid schedule are the Barnstead Elementary School addition and renovation (total award $12,849,760, final payment $2,569,952) and the Monadnock Regional addition and renovation of Emerson, Gilsum STEAM Academy, Mt. Caesar, Cutler and Troy elementary schools (total award $19,385,850, final payment $5,012,480), both at a 55% aid rate.
   - citation: NH DOE, Building Aid Distributions FY27-FY41, New Project Payments
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/building-aid-all-payments-7-1-25.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the file spells it "Gillsum STEAM Academy". Two districts, so no place line.
5. NH DOE's approved descending rank order list for school building aid (dated October 9, 2025; titled "Proposed School Building Aid - Descending Ranked Order List for FY2026") has seven projects with estimated costs totaling $323,282,042 and estimated maximum state awards totaling $125,553,894 (derived sums). In rank order: Concord, Litchfield, Monroe, Kearsarge Regional, Littleton, Haverhill Cooperative and Windham.

DOE notes any award depends on the state appropriation available. The list shows no award decisions.
6. Concord's new Rundlett Middle School, ranked first on NH DOE's school building aid list, is a 900-student, 214,105-square-foot school to replace the building constructed in 1957, with an estimated cost of $164,623,927 and an estimated maximum state award of $65,849,571 at Concord's 40% rate.

## money-fcda-fy2027-amounts: Fiscal Capacity Disparity Aid in 2026-27: Amounts, Manchester and Nashua, and the $3,750 Limit

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: Fiscal capacity disparity aid by town in FY2027, why Manchester and Nashua get none, the $3,750 limit for large cities, and the one-year FY2021 version.

1. NH DOE's FY2027 adequacy calculation (October 1, 2026) shows $10,277,616.20 in fiscal capacity disparity aid statewide, paid to 34 municipalities (derived count of towns with an amount above zero).
   - citation: NH DOE, FY 2027 Adequacy Aid - October 1, 2026, municipal summary, State Total row
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the file is not labeled final. raw/state-issues/money-fcda-fy2027-amounts/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx (identical to raw/town/doe/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx, MD5 086462d6...).
2. The largest FY2027 fiscal capacity disparity aid amounts in NH DOE's October 1, 2026 calculation go to Claremont ($1,351,854.52), Derry ($1,017,217.31), Brookline ($880,770.65) and Berlin ($846,043.32).
   - citation: NH DOE, FY 2027 Adequacy Aid - October 1, 2026, municipal summary
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. Manchester and Nashua receive no fiscal capacity disparity aid in FY2027, because their equalized valuation per pupil ($1,738,045.50 and $1,945,185.79) is above the $1,600,000 level where the aid ends.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY 2027 Adequacy Aid - October 1, 2026, municipal summary (EV per ADM column); RSA 198:40-g, I(c)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
4. In NH DOE's FY2027 calculation, Manchester (11,266.88 resident pupils) and Nashua (9,252.43) are the only municipalities with 5,000 or more resident pupils, the group whose extraordinary need grant plus fiscal capacity disparity aid New Hampshire law limits to $3,750 per pupil starting July 1, 2027. The next largest is Derry, with 4,215.34.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY 2027 Adequacy Aid - October 1, 2026, municipal summary (ADM-R column); RSA 198:41, I(e)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the limit itself is already in the library (money-adequacy-grant-calculation, fact 2).
5. Manchester's FY2027 extraordinary need grant is $51,222,880.05, about $4,546 per resident pupil, above the $3,750 limit that applies from July 1, 2027; Nashua's is $24,393,369.90, about $2,636 per pupil (per-pupil amounts derived by dividing by resident pupils).
   - citation: NH DOE, FY 2027 Adequacy Aid - October 1, 2026, municipal summary; RSA 198:41, I(e)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/fy-27-adequacy-muni-summary.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: describes FY2027 amounts only; the limit applies to grants from FY2028, which will use new data. Derived: $51,222,880.05 / 11,266.8791 = $4,546.32; $24,393,369.90 / 9,252.4334 = $2,636.43.
6. New Hampshire also paid a one-year fiscal capacity disparity aid in FY2021: NH DOE's FY2021 adequacy file shows $47,505,896.81 statewide to 98 municipalities, including $4,112,485.84 to Manchester.
   - citation: NH DOE, FY2021 adequate education aid file, "New FY2021 only Fiscal Capacity Disparity Aid" column
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/2020-04/ad_ed_aid_fy2021.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the 98 count is derived from raw/town/adequacy-aid.csv (FY2021 rows above zero). The existing topic title "new July 1, 2026" describes the current RSA 198:40-g, not the first time such aid was paid.

## federal-medicaid-to-schools-nh: Medicaid to Schools: Federal Medicaid Money for School Health Services

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's Medicaid to Schools program: federal Medicaid money for school services such as speech therapy, its 2026-27 renewal and the 2019 compliance problem.

1. On June 3, 2026, the Governor and Executive Council approved (item #124) DHHS interagency agreements with local education agencies statewide to continue Medicaid to Schools federal claiming from July 1, 2026 through June 30, 2027, with federal payments to schools of up to $10,000,000 in total, 100% federal funds.
   - citation: Governor and Executive Council minutes, June 3, 2026, item #124 (DHHS, Division of Medicaid Services)
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/GC-Minutes-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/federal-medicaid-to-schools-nh/GC-Minutes-060326.pdf, page 27. The request letter is dated May 15, 2026.
2. DHHS told the Governor and Council in May 2026 that the federal Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) was ending New Hampshire's existing Medicaid to Schools claiming method as of June 30, 2026, and that the new agreements use a new method consistent with CMS's 2023 school-based services guidance.
   - citation: DHHS letter to the Governor and Council, May 15, 2026, item #124, requested action 1 and explanation
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/124-GC-Agenda-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. Under New Hampshire's 2026-27 Medicaid to Schools agreements, school districts certify what they spent and DHHS claims the federal Medicaid match (the Certified Public Expenditure method); districts take part in a Random Moment Time Study, file administrative and cost reports, receive interim payments for direct services and go through an annual cost settlement.
   - citation: DHHS letter, May 15, 2026, item #124; template Interagency Agreement, sections 3.2 and 6
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/124-GC-Agenda-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
4. DHHS says about 174 New Hampshire school districts are served by Medicaid to Schools each year, and a district must be an enrolled Medicaid provider under RSA 186-C:25 to be reimbursed.
   - citation: DHHS letter, May 15, 2026, item #124, explanation
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/124-GC-Agenda-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
5. The 2026-27 Medicaid to Schools agreement lists physical, occupational and speech therapy, behavioral health, nursing and eligible EPSDT services as examples of direct medical services, covering services in a Medicaid-enrolled student's IEP, IFSP, Section 504 plan or individual health care plan, or otherwise medically necessary, delivered by qualified, enrolled providers.
   - citation: template Interagency Agreement, sections 3.3 and 3.4
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/124-GC-Agenda-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
6. Under the 2026-27 Medicaid to Schools agreement, eligible administrative costs are reimbursed at 50% or 75% depending on the activity and direct services at the federal Medicaid matching rate (FMAP), less DHHS's costs of running the program.
   - citation: template Interagency Agreement, sections 6.1.1.1 and 6.1.1.2
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/124-GC-Agenda-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
7. DHHS told the Governor and Council that without the 2026-27 agreements, districts could not claim federal Medicaid reimbursement, "resulting in the loss of federal funds that support the delivery of medically necessary school-based services to Medicaid-eligible students."
   - citation: DHHS letter, May 15, 2026, item #124, explanation
   - source: https://media.sos.nh.gov/govcouncil/2026/0603/124-GC-Agenda-060326.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
8. The Governor's Office says New Hampshire's Medicaid to Schools program began for services in a student's IEP, and in 2017 the state expanded it to all students enrolled in Medicaid.
   - citation: Governor's Office, "Medicaid to Schools Fact Sheet" (December 2019), Background
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt336/files/documents/medicaid-to-schools.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: undated; the text refers to a training "today, December 4" and a meeting on December 11, 2019.
9. After July 1, 2019 CMS guidance that school Medicaid services must come from providers meeting the same qualifications as community providers, DHHS filed an emergency rule on August 27, 2019 clarifying that only licensed medical providers may bill, and districts using providers credentialed by the Department of Education but not licensed by a medical board could no longer claim federal matching funds for those services.
   - citation: Governor's Office, "Medicaid to Schools Fact Sheet" (December 2019), July 1, 2019 Guidance
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt336/files/documents/medicaid-to-schools.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: the fact sheet gives no dollar figure for lost funds; see Not retrieved for the reported $29 million.

## federal-k12-funding-nh: Federal K-12 Formula Funding to New Hampshire, FY2025-FY2026

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: Federal Title I, IDEA and other school money NH receives, the FY2026 appropriations, the July 2025 funding hold, and the federal share of school revenue.

1. Congress enacted full-year FY2026 education appropriations on February 3, 2026 in H.R. 7148 (Public Law 119-75), after a funding lapse from October 1 to November 12, 2025, a continuing resolution through January 30, 2026 and a second lapse; CRS reports $79.0 billion in discretionary funding for the U.S. Department of Education, 0.3% more than FY2025.
   - citation: CRS Report R48970, Labor, Health and Human Services, and Education: FY2026 Appropriations (May 28, 2026), Overview; P.L. 119-75
   - source: https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/R48970
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: congress.gov lists H.R. 7148 as "Became Public Law No: 119-75" on 02/03/2026 (raw/state-issues/federal-k12-funding-nh/congress-hr7148.html). CRS calls it the Further Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026; congress.gov titles it the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026.
2. Nationally, FY2026 appropriations provided $18,426,802,000 for Title I-A grants to school districts and $14,233,704,000 for IDEA Part B grants to states, each $20 million more than in FY2025.
   - citation: U.S. Department of Education, State Tables by Program, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget (compiled March 16, 2026), totals
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-program-fy-2025-fy-2027-113553.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. New Hampshire's federal Title I-A grants to school districts were $50,823,340 in FY2025, and ED estimates $46,456,337 for FY2026, 8.6% less.
   - citation: ED, State Tables by State, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget, New Hampshire, Grants to Local Educational Agencies
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-state-fy-2025-fy-2027-113554.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: "2025 Actual" and "2026 Estimate" columns; final FY2026 allocations may differ.
4. New Hampshire's federal IDEA Part B special education grant to the state was $56,713,037 in FY2025, with ED estimating $56,784,857 for FY2026; New Hampshire also received $1,714,696 in IDEA preschool grants and $2,587,723 in IDEA infants and families (Part C) grants in each year.
   - citation: ED, State Tables by State, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget, New Hampshire, Special Education rows
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-state-fy-2025-fy-2027-113554.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
5. ED's state tables put New Hampshire's total federal K-12 formula grants at $149,496,566 in FY2025 and an estimated $145,001,006 in FY2026.
   - citation: ED, State Tables by State, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget, New Hampshire, "Subtotal, All Elementary/Secondary Level Programs"
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-state-fy-2025-fy-2027-113554.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: formula programs only; ED notes the tables do not include competitive grants.
6. ED's FY2026 estimates for New Hampshire's other federal K-12 formula grants include $10,837,184 for Title II-A (teachers and leaders), $6,693,000 for Title IV-A (student support and enrichment), $6,515,398 for 21st Century Community Learning Centers, $7,020,601 for career and technical education, $3,714,047 for state assessments and $922,814 for Title III (English learners).
   - citation: ED, State Tables by State, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget, New Hampshire
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-state-fy-2025-fy-2027-113554.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
7. In 2025, ED did not make FY2025 formula funds for Title I-C (migrant), Title II-A, Title III-A, Title IV-A and Title IV-B (21st Century Community Learning Centers) available to states on July 1 as it usually does; on July 25, 2025, reports indicated OMB had finished its review and ED would release the remaining funds.
   - citation: CRS Report R48970, "Forward Funding for Formula Grant Programs"
   - source: https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/R48970
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: CRS cites news reports for the July 25 release. Adult education (WIOA Title II) state grants were also held.
8. New Hampshire's FY2025 allocations for the four held programs it receives (Title II-A $10,837,184, Title III-A $979,091, Title IV-A $6,693,000 and 21st Century Community Learning Centers $6,548,640) total $25,057,915 (derived sum; New Hampshire receives no Title I-C migrant funds).
   - citation: ED, State Tables by State, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget, New Hampshire, 2025 Actual
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-state-fy-2025-fy-2027-113554.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: derived to check the reported "about $25 million"; no NH DOE or ED document giving the NH total held was found.
9. On July 25, 2025, Governor Ayotte announced the release of federal education funding for New Hampshire schools for "academic enrichment, English language learning, and other key programs," saying she had met several times with Secretary of Education Linda McMahon about it.
   - citation: Office of the Governor, press release, July 25, 2025
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-secures-release-federal-education-funding-new-hampshire
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
10. The FY2026 appropriations law added a requirement (section 312) that ED award formula grant funds under ESEA, IDEA, McKinney-Vento, Perkins CTE and adult education "on the date such funds become available for obligation."
   - citation: CRS Report R48970, "Forward Funding for Formula Grant Programs" (P.L. 119-75, Division B, sec. 312)
   - source: https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/R48970
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
11. NH DOE's statewide summary of school district revenue shows federal sources of $250,486,088 (6.0% of net revenue) in 2024-25, down from $339,939,204 (8.4%) in 2023-24, $330,981,901 (8.7%) in 2022-23 and $323,491,486 (8.9%) in 2021-22; it was $252,513,392 (7.1%) in 2020-21.
   - citation: NH DOE, State Summary Revenue and Expenditures of School Districts, 2020-21 through 2024-25 (Revenue Sources, Federal Sources)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/summary-of-rev-exp-fy2025.csv
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: earlier years from the fy2024, fy2023, fy2022 and fy2021 CSVs. The library's Census table (national-comparisons, census_revenue_share_by_source) gives a federal share of 8.3% for FY2024 on Census's definitions; the two sources count revenue differently.

## federal-education-department-changes: The U.S. Department of Education in 2025-26: Staff Cuts, Program Transfers and the FY2027 Budget Request

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: 2025-26 changes at the U.S. Department of Education that affect NH: staff cuts, moving K-12 and IDEA work to Labor and HHS, and the FY2027 budget request.

1. On March 11, 2025, the U.S. Department of Education announced a reduction in force affecting nearly half its workforce: it said staff had numbered 4,133 at the President's inauguration and would total roughly 2,183 after the action, including nearly 600 who took voluntary resignation or retirement.
   - citation: ED press release, "U.S. Department of Education Initiates Reduction in Force," March 11, 2025
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/about/news/press-release/us-department-of-education-initiates-reduction-force
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
2. On July 14, 2025, the U.S. Supreme Court stayed a federal district court's May 22, 2025 preliminary injunction against the Education Department's reduction in force, in McMahon v. New York, No. 24A1203, pending appeal; Justices Sotomayor, Kagan and Jackson dissented.
   - citation: McMahon v. New York, No. 24A1203 (U.S. July 14, 2025) (order granting stay)
   - source: https://www.supremecourt.gov/docket/docketfiles/html/public/24A1203.html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: the injunction was from the District of Massachusetts, case No. 1:25-cv-10601; the appeal was in the First Circuit (25-1495, 25-1500). Later proceedings were not checked.
3. The President's FY2027 budget request supports 1,909 full-time-equivalent staff at the U.S. Department of Education, 1,635 fewer than the 3,544 used in FY2025.
   - citation: ED, Fiscal Year 2027 Budget Summary, Departmental Management, Full-Time Equivalent Employment table
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/fy-2027-budget-summary-113552.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
4. On November 18, 2025, the U.S. Department of Education announced six interagency agreements with the Departments of Labor, Interior, Health and Human Services and State, including an "Elementary and Secondary Education Partnership" under which Labor "will take on a greater role in administering federal K-12 programs," managing competitions and technical assistance "with proper oversight by ED."
   - citation: ED press release, "U.S. Department of Education Announces Six New Agency Partnerships to Break Up Federal Bureaucracy," November 18, 2025
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/about/news/press-release/us-department-of-education-announces-six-new-agency-partnerships-break-federal-bureaucracy
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: CRS R48970 footnote 112 dates this release October 18, 2025; the ED page says November 18, 2025.
5. On June 16, 2026, the U.S. Department of Education announced four more interagency agreements, following 10 signed over the past year: one with HHS on special education and rehabilitative services, and three with the Department of Justice on civil rights enforcement, student privacy and training and advisory services.
   - citation: ED press release, "U.S. Department of Education Announces Additional Partnerships to Strengthen Coordination for Individuals with Disabilities Programs, Bolster Civil Rights Enforcement," June 16, 2026
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/about/news/press-release/us-department-of-education-announces-additional-partnerships-strengthen-coordination-individuals-disabilities-programs-bolster-civil-rights
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
6. Under the June 2026 ED-HHS agreement, HHS supports administration of IDEA Parts B, C and D, including grant administration, enforcement, compliance and monitoring, annual state performance determinations, data collection and drawdowns of federal funds; FY2026 IDEA awards are made through ED's G5 system and later awards through HHS's GrantSolutions and Payment Management System.
   - citation: ED, "Fact Sheet: Department of Education (ED) and Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) Special Education and Rehabilitative Services Partnership" (June 2026)
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/fact-sheet-department-of-education-and-department-of-health-and-human-services-special-education-and-rehabilitative-services-partnership-114238.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
7. ED's fact sheet says the HHS partnership is made under the Economy Act (31 U.S.C. 1535), that ED "will maintain all statutory responsibilities," that the Office of Special Education and Rehabilitative Services keeps its independent statutory functions, and that states "should not expect to experience programmatic disruptions."
   - citation: ED, ED-HHS Special Education and Rehabilitative Services Partnership fact sheet (June 2026)
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/fact-sheet-department-of-education-and-department-of-health-and-human-services-special-education-and-rehabilitative-services-partnership-114238.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
8. In the explanatory statement for the FY2026 appropriations law, Congress said "no authorities exist" for the Education Department to transfer its fundamental responsibilities to other agencies, kept a provision barring transfers of its funding without appropriations authority, and directed ED to give the appropriations committees biweekly briefings on the interagency agreements.
   - citation: CRS Report R48970, "Interagency Agreements" (citing Congressional Record, vol. 172, no. 15, January 22, 2026, p. H1598)
   - source: https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/R48970
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
9. The FY2026 appropriations law gave ED $400 million for salaries and expenses, $140 million for the Office for Civil Rights and $68 million for the Inspector General, and added language that ED "shall support staffing levels necessary to fulfill its statutory responsibilities."
   - citation: CRS Report R48970, "Departmental Management"
   - source: https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/R48970
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
10. The President's FY2027 budget request asks for $75.7 billion in discretionary funding for the U.S. Department of Education, $3.2 billion (4.1%) less than the FY2026 appropriation, with Title I-A held at $18.4 billion and a new $2 billion "Make Education Great Again" grant replacing 17 separate K-12 programs.
   - citation: ED, Fiscal Year 2027 Budget Summary, Summary of the 2027 Budget Request
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/fy-2027-budget-summary-113552.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: a request, not law. The programs that would be folded in include Title II-A, 21st Century Community Learning Centers, state assessments, rural education, Title IV-A and homeless education (appendix note).
11. The President's FY2027 budget request asks for $15.4 billion for IDEA Grants to States, folding in six other IDEA programs, including preschool grants, which ED calls a $538.6 million increase over the FY2026 level of those programs, plus $590 million for IDEA infants and families grants.
   - citation: ED, Fiscal Year 2027 Budget Summary, "Supporting Students with Disabilities"
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/fy-2027-budget-summary-113552.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
12. ED's state tables for the FY2027 request show New Hampshire's federal K-12 formula grants falling from an estimated $145,001,006 in FY2026 to $110,019,464 in FY2027, with IDEA Grants to States rising to $60,975,404 and the programs proposed for consolidation shown as $0.
   - citation: ED, State Tables by State, FY 2025-2027 President's Budget, New Hampshire, "2027 Estimate" column
   - source: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/state-tables-state-fy-2025-fy-2027-113554.xlsx
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: the tables do not show a state amount for the proposed $2 billion MEGA grant, so the FY2027 total leaves it out. These are estimates for a proposal.

---

# School Choice

**Sources and notes for this section**

- 8 topics, 62 facts, researched 2026-11-11 (one fact, the repealed charter MOU text, is from a Wayback copy).
- Sources: NH General Court bill status pages, chaptered and introduced bill texts, amendments and LBA fiscal notes; the Governor's signing and veto releases; NH Department of Education EFA fact sheet, EFA payment reports, enrollment report, tax credit page and press release; the IRS, the U.S. Code (Cornell LII) and the Federal Register; Children's Scholarship Fund NH for its own program figures. One news source (Concord Monitor) is cited, for the Governor's stated position on open enrollment, because no primary statement was found.
- Raw copies: `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/` (governor.nh.gov, education.nh.gov and my.doe.nh.gov pages were saved through a Chrome window because those sites refuse scripted requests; gc.nh.gov refused connections for about an hour on 2026-11-11 and was downloaded after it recovered).
- review_by: enacted NH bills 2027-08-15; open items (FY2027 EFA payments still to come, the 2026-27 EFA count, the IRS state list and New Hampshire's SGO list) 2027-05-11; everything else, including federal law, 2027-11-11.

## choice-efa-enrollment-growth: Education Freedom Account Enrollment Since SB 295 (2025)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How many students have used NH's Education Freedom Accounts (EFAs) each year, the growth after SB 295 (2025) removed the income limit, priority students and the waitlist.

1. New Hampshire's SB 295 (2025), which removed the household income limit for Education Freedom Accounts, passed the Senate 16-8 on March 27, 2025. The House passed it with amendments 190-178 on June 5, 2025, and the Senate agreed to the House's changes by voice vote the same day.
   - citation: SB 295 (2025 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=965&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

2. Governor Kelly Ayotte signed SB 295 on June 10, 2025, the same day she signed HB 10, the Parental Bill of Rights. SB 295 became 2025 chapter 75.
   - citation: Governor's release, June 10, 2025; SB 295 (2025), chapter 75
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-laws-establishing-parental-bill-rights-and-expanding-education
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The chapter number is from the bill status page (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=965&inflect=2). governor.nh.gov blocks scripted requests; the release was read and saved in a Chrome window.

3. Children's Scholarship Fund New Hampshire, which runs the EFA program, reported on August 1, 2025 that 10,000 students had enrolled for 2025-26, the first year without an income limit, and that 295 students were on a waitlist. It said more than 11,000 students applied in the month after the Governor signed SB 295.
   - citation: Children's Scholarship Fund NH, "10,000 New Hampshire Students Enrolled in Education Freedom Accounts," August 1, 2025
   - source: https://nh.scholarshipfund.org/10000-new-hampshire-students-enrolled-in-education-freedom-accounts/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Program operator's own figures. Once the cap is reached, only students in the priority groups may still enroll (RSA 194-F:1, XIV), and they kept enrolling after this date (see facts 4 and 6).

4. According to the NH Department of Education, Education Freedom Account enrollment on October 1 of each school year was 1,635 in 2021-22, 3,110 in 2022-23, 4,552 in 2023-24, 5,765 in 2024-25 and 10,510 in 2025-26.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Table 1
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The table labels school years by the year they end (2022 = 2021-22). Counts change during the year because students enter and leave on a rolling basis. education.nh.gov blocks scripted requests; the PDF was saved through a Chrome window.

5. New Hampshire's Education Freedom Account enrollment grew by 4,745 students, or 82.31 percent, from fall 2024 to fall 2025, the first fall after SB 295 removed the income limit. The year before, it had grown by 1,213 students (26.65 percent).
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Table 1
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

6. Of the 10,510 Education Freedom Account students on October 1, 2025, 8,757 (83.32 percent) were in a priority group and 1,753 (16.68 percent) were not, according to the NH Department of Education. The department notes that priority students can keep enrolling during the year even after the cap is met.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Table 7
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The priority groups (current EFA students, siblings, children with disabilities, and families at or below 350% of the federal poverty guidelines) are listed in efa-eligibility-and-cap. RSA 194-F:1, XIV defines the cap as the total number of students who may be enrolled in a year before enrollment closes to all but priority students.

7. The NH Department of Education counted 343 students who left a district public school to join the EFA program in 2025-26, compared with 297 in 2024-25, 309 in 2023-24, 213 in 2022-23 and 275 in 2021-22. The 2024-25 and 2025-26 counts are preliminary.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Table 12
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The department uses these counts for phase-out grants to districts under RSA 194-F:10. They cover only students enrolled in a district school on the last day of the prior year; most EFA students came from elsewhere (for example, private or home education, or entering kindergarten).

8. In fall 2025, 2,017 of New Hampshire's 10,510 Education Freedom Account students (19.19 percent) qualified for the extra free or reduced-price meal aid and 890 (8.47 percent) for special education aid, compared with 23.46 percent and 19.07 percent of public school students. In fall 2021, 53.76 percent of EFA students had qualified for the free or reduced-price meal aid.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Tables 2 and 11
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Public school figures cover NH resident students in district schools, academies and charter schools (156,605). Children's Scholarship Fund NH said on August 1, 2025 that some returning low-income families had not completed the income section of the application, so the meal-aid count was understated, and that it was asking those families to re-certify.

9. The NH Department of Education counted 13,177 Education Freedom Account students on October 2, 2026, for the 2026-27 school year. Of them, 3,020 qualified for free or reduced-price meal aid, 1,517 for special education aid and 27 for English learner aid.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, Education Freedom Accounts by Residence of Student, Cost of an Adequate Education Calculation, SFY 2027 (November 1, 2026)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/media/media_document/efa-by-muni-as-of-10-02-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Statewide total row. The 2026-27 cap is 12,500 (efa-eligibility-and-cap); priority students may enroll beyond it. The program operator's dashboard showed 13,159 "Enrolled" on November 8, 2026 (efa-how-to-apply); the two counts are on different dates.

## choice-efa-state-cost: What Education Freedom Accounts Cost the State, and the 2026 Cap Bills

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: What NH spends on Education Freedom Accounts (EFAs) in FY2026 and FY2027, the average grant, per-pupil amounts, and the 2026 EFA cap bills (HB 1834, SB 581).

1. The NH Department of Education's projected Education Freedom Account spending, based on fall enrollment, was $8,041,351 in 2021-22, $15,220,715 in 2022-23, $23,829,800 in 2023-24, $30,354,282 in 2024-25 and $51,619,550 in 2025-26.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Table 3
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: "Projected expenditures based on beginning of school year enrollment and differential aid eligibility." Final spending differs as students enter and leave during the year (see fact 4).

2. The NH Department of Education put the average cost per Education Freedom Account student at $4,911 in 2025-26, compared with $5,265 in 2024-25, $5,235 in 2023-24, $4,894 in 2022-23 and $4,918 in 2021-22.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, November 2025 Education Freedom Account Data Fact Sheet, Table 3
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-factsheet-november-2025.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The average includes base aid plus any differentiated aid. In the same table, the share of EFA students with free or reduced-price meal aid fell from 39.22% in 2024-25 to 19.19% in 2025-26 (see choice-efa-enrollment-growth, fact 8).

3. For 2025-26 (FY2026), each Education Freedom Account carried base aid of $4,265.64, plus $2,392.92 for a student eligible for free or reduced-price meals, $2,184.84 for special education and $832.32 for an English learner. For 2026-27 (FY2027) the amounts are $4,351, $2,441, $2,229 and $849.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, EFA by Residence of Student payment reports for SFY 2026 (April 1, 2026) and SFY 2027 (November 1, 2026)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/media/media_document/efa-by-muni-as-of-10-02-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: FY2026 figures from https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-by-muni-as-of-3-02-2026_0.pdf. These are the adequacy aid amounts under RSA 198:40-a, which EFAs carry under RSA 194-F:2, I.

4. For FY2026 (2025-26), the NH Department of Education's EFA payment report as of April 1, 2026 counted 10,708 students on March 2, 2026 and a total calculated cost of $53,141,031. The four scheduled payments to the scholarship organization (September, November, January and April) add up to $51,626,095.14 (derived).
   - citation: NH Department of Education, Education Freedom Accounts by Residence of Student, Cost of an Adequate Education Calculation, Payment Schedule for SFY 2026 (April 1, 2026), state total
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/efa-by-muni-as-of-3-02-2026_0.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Derived by adding the state-total payments: $9,589,403.13 (Sept. 1, 20% of Aug. 2 count) + $10,323,924.66 (Nov. 1, 20% of Oct. 2 count) + $15,770,472.62 (Jan. 1, 30% of Dec. 2 count) + $15,942,294.73 (Apr. 1, 30% of Mar. 2 count). Payments track head counts at four dates, so they differ from the full-year calculated cost. Any later adjustment was not found.

5. For FY2027 (2026-27), the NH Department of Education's EFA report as of November 1, 2026 counted 13,177 students on October 2, 2026 and a total calculated cost of $68,109,263, an average of about $5,169 per student (derived). The first two payments were $12,830,584 on September 1 and $13,621,852.60 on November 1, 2026; the January and April payments were still to come.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, Education Freedom Accounts by Residence of Student, Cost of an Adequate Education Calculation, Payment Schedule for SFY 2027 (November 1, 2026), state total
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/media/media_document/efa-by-muni-as-of-10-02-2026.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Average derived as $68,109,263 / 13,177 = $5,168.80. The total will change as later head counts are taken.

6. In January 2026, the Legislative Budget Assistant's fiscal note for HB 1834 used Department of Education estimates that the EFA program would cost $62,625,000 in FY2027 under the law as it stood (12,500 students at an average of $5,010), or $56,362,500 if the cap stayed at 10,000 (an estimated 11,250 students, counting priority students). It said 10,510 students were enrolled for the November 2025 payment.
   - citation: HB 1834-FN (2026), fiscal note as introduced (revised 1/12/26)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=24647&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

7. The same HB 1834 fiscal note said the net state cost of a new EFA student depends on where the student comes from: a student leaving home education or a private school adds the full average EFA grant; a student leaving an in-person charter school saves the state about $5,407 (FY2027 charter aid of $10,417 minus the average EFA grant); and a student leaving a district school in a community without excess statewide education property tax lowers that district's adequacy grant, which the note puts at a difference of $2,040 per student (an average FY2027 grant of $7,050 minus the average EFA grant).
   - citation: HB 1834-FN (2026), fiscal note as introduced (revised 1/12/26), methodology
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=24647&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: For a district school student in a town whose statewide education property tax exceeds its adequacy cost ("excess SWEPT"), the note says the net state cost equals the average EFA grant.

8. HB 1834 (2026) would have kept the Education Freedom Account cap at 10,000 for 2026-27 instead of letting it rise to 12,500. The House Education Policy and Administration Committee recommended killing it 10-8, the House voted 180-157 on March 5, 2026 to lay it on the table, and it died on the table when the session ended on August 19, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1834-FN (2026 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2394&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

9. SB 581 (2026) would have repealed the Education Freedom Account priority guidelines and the enrollment cap. The Senate Education Finance Committee voted 6-0 to send it to interim study, and the Senate agreed by voice vote on March 5, 2026, so it did not pass in 2026.
   - citation: SB 581-FN (2026 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1207&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Its fiscal note was never completed: the LBA wrote that it was awaiting information from the Department of Education. Because neither bill passed, the 2026-27 cap rose to 12,500 under the existing law.

## choice-hb-1817-efa-public-school-courses: HB 1817 (2026): EFA Students' Access to Their Local District's Courses and Activities

- kind: rule
- question_type: rules
- description: HB 1817 (2026): EFA students may take courses and join activities, such as sports, at their resident district's public schools.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1817 (2026) was signed by Governor Ayotte on June 19, 2026, became 2026 chapter 183 and took effect August 18, 2026, 60 days after passage.
   - citation: HB 1817-FN (2026), chapter 183
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2071&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

2. HB 1817 (2026) added Education Freedom Account students to the groups guaranteed access to the curricular courses and cocurricular programs of the school district where they live, on terms no more restrictive than for the district's own students. Before it, RSA 193:1-c named only nonpublic, public charter school and home-educated students.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 183:1 (HB 1817), amending RSA 193:1-c, I
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=33970&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Prior text: Wayback snapshot of https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-1-c.htm, 2026-01-30 (raw copy saved). The current text is quoted in home-education-rules, fact 8.

3. HB 1817 (2026) also added that the access in RSA 193:1-c includes the statewide assessment and the PSAT (preliminary scholastic aptitude test), for all the listed groups of students.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 183:1 (HB 1817), amending RSA 193:1-c, I
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=33970&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Confirmed by comparing the chaptered text with the Wayback copy of RSA 193:1-c from January 30, 2026.

4. As introduced, HB 1817 (2026) would have given access to district courses and programs to every New Hampshire resident eligible to enroll in a public school. The House Education Policy and Administration Committee rewrote it, 10-8, to cover Education Freedom Account students, and the House passed that version 179-156 on March 11, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1817-FN (2026), as introduced; bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2071&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Introduced title: "permitting all students the choice to attend the school district in which they reside." The House adopted the committee amendment (#2026-0911h) on a division vote, 177-150.

5. In the Senate, the Education Committee recommended 3-2 an amendment to HB 1817 (2026) that would have dropped the new reference to the statewide assessment and PSAT. The Senate rejected that amendment by voice vote and passed the House version 15-8 on May 14, 2026, so the House did not have to vote again.
   - citation: HB 1817-FN (2026), bill docket; Senate amendment #2026-1733s
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2071&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The bill was laid on the table on May 7 and taken off the table on May 14, 2026.

6. The fiscal note for HB 1817 (2026) as passed by the House called the state and local cost indeterminable. The Department of Education said it would likely cause a significant cost to districts, the state or both, depending on interpretation, because districts are not paid state aid for EFA students who have not left the EFA program.
   - citation: HB 1817-FN (2026), fiscal note as amended by the House (#2026-0911h), March 20, 2026
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=28499&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The department also noted that an EFA student enrolled in a district school more than 50 percent of the time must leave the EFA program (RSA 194-F:1, VIII; RSA 194-F:3, III(d)(2)), and that taking many district courses could lead to that.

## choice-federal-tax-credit-scholarships: The Federal Scholarship Tax Credit (Section 25F) and New Hampshire's HB 1774 (2026)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: The federal scholarship tax credit (IRC 25F, Educational Choice for Children Act): NH's opt-in, HB 1774 (2026), the $1,700 credit from 2027, participating states.

1. Section 25F of the federal Internal Revenue Code, added by Public Law 119-21 (the 2025 federal tax and budget law, signed July 4, 2025), gives a federal income tax credit for cash donations to scholarship granting organizations. It applies to taxable years ending after December 31, 2026, and the credit for any taxpayer for a year may not exceed $1,700.
   - citation: 26 U.S.C. § 25F(a), (b); Pub. L. 119-21, title VII, § 70411
   - source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/26/25F
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Cornell LII reproduces the U.S. Code; uscode.house.gov returned an error for this section on 2026-11-11. The effective date is in the note to section 25 (section 70411(c)(1)).

2. Under the IRS's explanation of the Federal Scholarship Tax Credit, a married couple filing jointly may each claim up to $1,700, for $3,400 in all, and the credit is nonrefundable. It is available only in states that elect to take part and send the IRS a list of approved scholarship granting organizations.
   - citation: IRS, Federal Scholarship Tax Credit (FSTC)
   - source: https://www.irs.gov/charities-non-profits/federal-scholarship-tax-credit-fstc
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

3. Under section 25F, scholarships may go only to students in households with income no greater than 300 percent of the area median gross income, and a scholarship granting organization must spend at least 90 percent of its income on scholarships for qualified elementary and secondary education expenses. Taking part is voluntary for each state; the election is made by the governor or another person or agency designated under state law.
   - citation: 26 U.S.C. § 25F(c)(1)-(2), (d)(1), (g)(1)
   - source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/26/25F
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Qualified expenses are those described in 26 U.S.C. § 530(b)(3)(A). Contributions must fund scholarships only in the state where the organization is listed, and the federal credit is reduced by any state tax credit for the same contribution (§ 25F(b)(2), (c)(3)).

4. On January 29, 2026, during National School Choice Week, the NH Department of Education announced that New Hampshire would opt in to the Federal Education Tax Credit program for scholarship granting organizations. Its release quoted Governor Ayotte and Commissioner Caitlin Davis and said taxpayers may receive a federal credit of up to $1,700.
   - citation: NH Department of Education press release, January 29, 2026 (updated January 30, 2026)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/new-hampshire-opts-federal-education-tax-credit-program-scholarship-granting-organizations
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: No separate release was found on governor.nh.gov. education.nh.gov blocks scripted requests; read and saved in a Chrome window.

5. New Hampshire's HB 1774 (2026) directs the commissioner of the Department of Revenue Administration to make the annual election under section 25F so New Hampshire residents can claim the credit, beginning in taxable years ending after December 31, 2026. It directs the Department of Education to create a list of qualified scholarship granting organizations, send it to the U.S. Treasury each year and publish it on its website.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 242:2-4 (HB 1774), RSA 21-J:3, XXXIV
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=34075&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Section 242:5-6 separately has the Governor, with the State Workforce Innovation Board, approve workforce training programs for federal Workforce Pell Grants. Section 242:7 bars any state agency from adopting rules to carry out the act.

6. Governor Ayotte signed HB 1774 (2026) on July 2, 2026, and it became 2026 chapter 242, effective July 1, 2026. The House passed it by voice vote on March 11, 2026, the Senate by voice vote on May 14, 2026, and the House agreed to the Senate's amendment by voice vote on May 21, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1774-FN (2026), chapter 242; bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2059&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The Governor's July 2, 2026 release lists HB 1774 among the bills signed (https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-74-bills-law). The chaptered effective date (July 1) is the day before signing.

7. As of October 22, 2026, the IRS listed 30 states, including New Hampshire, that had elected to take part in the Federal Scholarship Tax Credit for 2027.
   - citation: IRS, Federal Scholarship Tax Credit (FSTC) - Participating states (as of October 22, 2026)
   - source: https://www.irs.gov/government-entities/federal-state-local-governments/federal-scholarship-tax-credit-fstc-participating-states
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Count derived from the list: Alabama, Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Mississippi, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, Nevada, New Hampshire, North Dakota, North Carolina, Ohio, Oklahoma, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Virginia, West Virginia, Wyoming. States elect on IRS Form 15714.

8. The U.S. Treasury and IRS published temporary regulations and proposed regulations for the Federal Scholarship Tax Credit in the Federal Register on October 2, 2026. The temporary regulations, which set requirements and procedures for participating states and certified scholarship granting organizations, take effect December 1, 2026.
   - citation: Federal Register documents 2026-20264 (temporary rule) and 2026-20277 (proposed rule), October 2, 2026
   - source: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/02/2026-20264/federal-scholarship-tax-credit
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Read through the Federal Register API (raw copies saved). The proposed rule is at https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/02/2026-20277/federal-scholarship-tax-credit.

9. As of November 11, 2026, the NH Department of Education's tax credit page said New Hampshire was still finalizing its application process for scholarship granting organizations. It asked organizations to register with the Secretary of State and the Department of Justice's Charitable Trust Unit, and it did not yet show a list of approved organizations.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, Education Tax Credit Scholarship Programs page
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/pathways-education/education-tax-credit-scholarship-programs
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Children's Scholarship Fund NH said in July 2026 that it was preparing to take part as a scholarship granting organization. Questions go to sgo@doe.nh.gov.

10. The federal credit is separate from New Hampshire's own Education Tax Credit scholarship program, which has run since 2012. In the state program, businesses donate up to $600,000 to an approved scholarship organization and receive a credit of 85 percent of the donation against the Business Profits Tax or Business Enterprise Tax, and scholarships go to families at or below 300 percent of the federal poverty level.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, Education Tax Credit Scholarship Programs page
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/pathways-education/education-tax-credit-scholarship-programs
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The federal credit uses 300 percent of area median income, not of the federal poverty level.

## choice-open-enrollment-2026-bills: How the 2026 Statewide Open Enrollment Bills (HB 751 and SB 101) Failed

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's 2026 statewide open enrollment bills (HB 751, SB 101): how open enrollment was added to HB 751, the conference versions, the Governor's view and how they died.

1. New Hampshire's HB 751 began in 2025 as a House bill on licensing outpatient substance use disorder treatment facilities. The House passed amended versions 216-152 on March 6, 2025 and 350-3 on January 8, 2026; the version sent to the Senate would have set up a committee to study licensing those facilities.
   - citation: HB 751-FN (2025-26 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=275&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

2. On January 29, 2026, the Senate added statewide open enrollment to HB 751 through a floor amendment by Senator Lang (#2026-0297s). It would have required every school district to establish an open enrollment policy allowing pupils to transfer among its schools and from other New Hampshire districts, and let a parent apply to any school or district in the state. The Senate split the vote: the two sections on the Parental Bill of Rights and the effective date passed 24-0, and the remainder, including open enrollment, passed 16-8.
   - citation: HB 751-FN (2025-26 session), bill docket; Senate floor amendment #2026-0297s
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=275&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The bill's second new title added "authorizing parents to enroll their children in any public school in the state" and a limited exemption to the Parental Bill of Rights consent rule. The amended fiscal note said it was not possible to predict how many students would transfer or the effect on state aid and local budgets.

3. The House voted by voice vote on February 12, 2026 not to accept the Senate's changes to HB 751 and asked for a committee of conference, which the Senate agreed to. Conference meetings were scheduled from May 18 to May 28, 2026.
   - citation: HB 751-FN (2025-26 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=275&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

4. A committee of conference amendment to HB 751 dated May 28, 2026 (#2026-2116CofC) would have rewritten New Hampshire's open enrollment law (RSA 194-D) with a statewide cap of 500 open enrollment students for 2027-28, raised by 25 percent in any later year when more than 90 percent of the cap was used. The state would have paid receiving schools per-pupil aid equal to adequacy aid plus the charter school grant, with no tuition charged.
   - citation: HB 751-FN (2025-26 session), committee of conference amendment #2026-2116CofC, proposed RSA 194-D:2, I and 194-D:4, I
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?txtFormat=amend&sy=2026&id=2026-2116CofC
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The version also let districts set their own capacity, limited the reasons for denying an application, required lotteries when applications exceed seats, and kept special education with the resident district.

5. The committee of conference report filed for HB 751 (#2026-2118CofC) did not include the 500-student plan. Instead, it would have amended the existing optional open enrollment law so that a district adopting open enrollment could not limit its resident students' participation to less than 10 percent of its enrollment, with 10 percent as the default in districts with annual meetings where no limit passed.
   - citation: HB 751-FN (2025-26 session), committee of conference report #2026-2118CofC, amending RSA 194-D:3, I(c)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?txtFormat=amend&sy=2026&id=2026-2118CofC
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The bill status page records the report as filed on June 4, 2026 (#2026-2118c).

6. On June 4, 2026, the Senate voted by voice vote, on a motion by Senator Pearl, to lay the HB 751 committee of conference report on the table, so neither chamber voted on the report itself. It was not taken up again.
   - citation: HB 751-FN (2025-26 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=275&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

7. Governor Ayotte said in a statement on May 28, 2026 that she did not plan to support the universal open enrollment bill, according to the Concord Monitor, which quoted her: "That bill is not ready for primetime."
   - citation: Concord Monitor, "Governor does not plan to support universal open enrollment bill in current form," May 28, 2026
   - source: https://www.concordmonitor.com/2026/05/28/governor-does-not-plan-to-support-universal-open-enrollment-bill-in-current-form/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: News source: no statement on open enrollment was found on governor.nh.gov (keyword searches of its news feed for "open enrollment", "enrollment" and "primetime"). InDepthNH (June 4, 2026) reported that the Governor's statement came about an hour before the conference agreement had to be signed, and that the 10 percent version followed it.

8. New Hampshire's other 2026 statewide open enrollment bill, SB 101, passed the Senate 14-10 on January 29, 2026. On April 23, 2026, the House adopted a committee amendment 182-172 but then rejected passing the bill 168-184, before tabling it.
   - citation: SB 101-FN (2025-26 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1052&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
