   - note: The 320-32 vote to table and the death on the table are in open-enrollment-status, fact 6.

## choice-hb-1358-charter-study-veto: HB 1358 (2026): The Vetoed Study of Converting Public Schools to Charter Schools

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1358 (2026), a study of turning all NH public schools into charter schools: the votes, the Governor's veto (not HB 1385) and the failed override.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1358 (2026), sponsored by Representative Osborne, would have set up a commission to study the feasibility and potential impact of transitioning all New Hampshire public schools to operate as public charter schools. The commission was to look at whether that could relieve local property taxpayers, restore local control and use state education dollars more efficiently.
   - citation: HB 1358 (2026), as passed, proposed RSA 194-B:21-a, I
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=33049&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

2. The HB 1358 commission would have had three House members, one senator, the education commissioner or a designee, one charter school representative and one traditional school district representative named by the commissioner, and one special education member named by the Governor. It was to report by November 1, 2026, and the section would then have been repealed.
   - citation: HB 1358 (2026), as passed, proposed RSA 194-B:21-a, II and VI; sections 2-3
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=33049&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

3. The House passed HB 1358 (2026) 169-163 on March 11, 2026, after its Education Policy and Administration Committee recommended it 10-8. The Senate passed an amended version by voice vote on May 7, 2026, and the House agreed to the Senate's changes 184-164 on May 21, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1358 (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2279&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

4. Governor Ayotte vetoed HB 1358 (2026) on July 2, 2026. Her veto statement said: "As a proud product of New Hampshire public schools, I believe providing every child the opportunity and choice to attend a public school is important and necessary. While I fully support education freedom allowing every child to be in the learning environment best for them, including innovative public charter schools, I cannot envision a future that does not include public schools. Therefore, I believe this study committee is unnecessary."
   - citation: Governor's release "Governor Signs 74 Bills into Law," July 2, 2026, veto statement for HB 1358
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-74-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: governor.nh.gov blocks scripted requests; the release was read and saved in a Chrome window.

5. The bill vetoed was HB 1358. The Governor's July 2, 2026 release lists "HB 1358 – Establishing a commission to study transitioning all public schools to public charter schools" among the vetoed bills and heads the statement "The Governor's veto statement for HB 1358," but the statement's last sentence reads "I have vetoed House Bill 1385." HB 1385 (2026) was a different bill, on negative property tax rates, which the House had found inexpedient to legislate.
   - citation: Governor's release, July 2, 2026; NH General Court bill status search for HB 1358 and HB 1385 (2026)
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-74-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: HB 1385's title, "prohibiting the use of negative property tax rates in certain municipalities," and status are from the General Court's bill status search results, saved 2026-11-11. The bill status page for HB 1358 records the veto.

6. On August 19, 2026, the House vote to override the veto of HB 1358 (2026) was 150 in favor and 166 against, short of the two-thirds needed, so the veto was sustained and the bill did not become law.
   - citation: HB 1358 (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2279&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

## choice-charter-district-special-education: Charter Schools and Districts: HB 222 and HB 1586 (2026), Enrollment and State Aid

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 222 (2026) ending the charter-district agreement for students with disabilities, HB 1586 (2026), and NH charter enrollment and per-pupil aid.

1. Until June 27, 2026, New Hampshire law (RSA 194-B:5, VIII) required a chartered public school and the resident district of a student with disabilities to enter into a memorandum of understanding on how the district would provide special education services in the charter school, to be reviewed and updated before each school year. The Department of Education was to provide a model MOU as a guide.
   - citation: RSA 194-B:5, VIII (repealed by 2026 ch. 41:1)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/194-B/194-B-5.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-04-15
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Read from the Wayback Machine copy of April 15, 2026 (raw copy saved), before the repeal took effect.

2. New Hampshire's HB 222 repealed that MOU requirement. Governor Ayotte signed it on April 28, 2026 as 2026 chapter 41; the repeal (section 1) took effect June 27, 2026. The Senate had added unrelated changes to the Department of Corrections' organization, which took effect on signing.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 41:1 and 41:10 (HB 222)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=31788&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Repeal confirmed in the RSA 194-B:5 source note ("2026, 41:1 eff. June 27, 2026") in raw/rules/rsa-194-B-mrg.txt. The resident district's duty to provide and pay for special education in a charter school (RSA 194-B:11, III) is unchanged; see charter-schools, fact 6.

3. HB 222 was filed in 2025. The House passed it by voice vote on March 6, 2025, after its Education Policy and Administration Committee recommended it 18-0. The Senate passed it with a floor amendment by voice vote on January 7, 2026, and the House agreed to the amendment by voice vote on April 9, 2026.
   - citation: HB 222 (2025-26 session), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=213&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

4. HB 1586 (2026) would have required the education commissioner and the state treasurer to halt all public funding to a public school that did not follow RSA 194-B:3, VI(c), on a district's duty to provide special education to all qualifying students who live in it, or the MOU requirement in RSA 194-B:5, VIII. In its fiscal note, the Department of Education said it read the bill as withholding a school district's state and federal funds if it did not complete an MOU with a charter school, and estimated $80,000 in FY2027 for a staff member if it had to monitor compliance.
   - citation: HB 1586-FN (2026), as introduced, sections 1-2; fiscal note
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=23960&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

5. The House Education Funding Committee voted 16-0 that HB 1586 (2026) was inexpedient to legislate, and the House agreed by voice vote on February 5, 2026, so the bill died.
   - citation: HB 1586-FN (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2453&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

6. New Hampshire public charter schools enrolled 7,071 students in fall 2026 (2026-27), up from 6,336 in fall 2025 and 4,228 in fall 2019, according to the NH Department of Education. Charter students were about 4.5 percent of the 157,617 public school students in fall 2026 (derived).
   - citation: NH Department of Education, State Totals Ten Years Public and Private Fall Enrollments, 2017-18 through 2026-27 (Report ID 17, report date November 5, 2026)
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform/Report/Report?path=%2FBDMQ%2FiPlatform%20Reports%2FEnrollment%20Data%2FState%20Totals%2FState%20Totals%20Ten%20Years%20Public%20and%20Private%20Fall%20Enrollments&name=State%20Totals%20Ten%20Years%20Public%20and%20Private%20Fall%20Enrollments&categoryName=State%20Totals&categoryId=12
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Charter totals include preschool (15 in 2026-27); the Department's 2024 charter report says its charter counts include full-time Virtual Learning Academy students. Share derived as 7,071 / 157,617. The Excel export (report date 11/5/2026, "Type of Data: Final") was saved by another research group and copied to this topic's raw folder. EFA students are not included.

7. Under New Hampshire law, the state pays a state-authorized charter school, for each New Hampshire pupil, the adequacy aid amounts plus an additional grant that was set at $4,900 for FY2024 and is adjusted each year; the Virtual Learning Academy Charter School receives a smaller additional grant, set at $2,036 and also adjusted. The state pays the special education differentiated aid to the student's resident district.
   - citation: RSA 194-B:11, I(b)(1)(A)-(B)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/194-B/194-B-11.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Adjustments are made under RSA 198:40-d. Online charter schools first authorized on or after July 1, 2013 receive only the adequacy amounts (RSA 194-B:11, I(b)(2)).

8. The Legislative Budget Assistant's January 2026 fiscal note for HB 1834 put FY2027 state adequacy aid for an in-person charter school student at $10,417. The NH Department of Education's 2024 charter report gave $9,180 in base adequacy plus the additional charter grant for 2024-25.
   - citation: HB 1834-FN (2026), fiscal note (revised 1/12/26); NH Department of Education, 2024 Annual Charter Report, p. 5
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=24647&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The 2024 report is at https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/2024-final-charter-report.pdf (raw copy saved). Differentiated aid (free or reduced-price meals, English learners) is added for eligible students.

## choice-hb-1268-home-education-history: HB 1268 (2026): How the Home Education Law Was Passed

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: The legislative history of HB 1268 (2026), the "Home Education Freedom Act" rewriting NH's homeschool law (RSA 193-A): votes, signing, chapter and dates.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1268 (2026) was introduced as the "Home Education Freedom Act" by Representative Noble and other sponsors. As introduced, it added definitions to the home education law and removed certain notice and assessment requirements for home-educating parents.
   - citation: HB 1268 (2026), as introduced, section 1 and analysis
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=22924&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

2. The House passed HB 1268 (2026) 174-166 on March 11, 2026, after adopting the Education Policy and Administration Committee's amendment 178-160 (the committee had voted 10-8) and a floor amendment by Representative Noble 181-158.
   - citation: HB 1268 (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1715&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

3. In the Senate, a floor amendment by Senator Rochefort added pharmacy benefit manager and managed care provisions to HB 1268 (2026) as sections 11-20. On May 7, 2026, the Senate voted on the two parts separately: the home education sections passed 14-8 and the pharmacy sections passed by voice vote.
   - citation: HB 1268 (2026), bill docket; Senate amendments #2026-1737s and #2026-1872s
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1715&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The Senate Education Committee had recommended its amendment 3-2.

4. The House agreed to the Senate's version of HB 1268 (2026) on a division vote, 178-170, on May 21, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1268 (2026), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1715&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

5. Governor Ayotte signed HB 1268 (2026) on July 10, 2026, and it became 2026 chapter 297. The home education sections took effect July 1, 2026, and the pharmacy benefit sections (11-20) take effect January 1, 2027.
   - citation: HB 1268 (2026), chapter 297; Governor's release, July 10, 2026
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1715&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The bill status page's "Chapter Number" field and the RSA 193-A source notes say chapter 297, but its docket line reads "Signed by Governor Ayotte 07/10/2026; Chapter 290". Chapter 297 is used here. The Governor's release (https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-38-bills-law) lists HB 1268 among bills signed that week.

6. HB 1268 (2026) also directed the State Board of Education, immediately on passage, to undertake and complete an expedited repeal of its home education rules, Ed 315, under RSA 541-A:19-a.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 297:9 (HB 1268)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/pdf.aspx?id=34144&q=billVersion
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Relevant to home-education-rules, fact 10, which found Ed 315 still published on 2026-11-08. Whether the Board has completed the repeal was not checked.

---

# Rights and Classroom Rules

**Sources and notes for this section**

- **Topics:** 5 topics, 48 facts: the 2025 anti-DEI law and the NEA-NH lawsuit (13), the "divisive concepts" law and its appeal (10), the Parental Bill of Rights' history and 2026 changes (7), SB 430 and HB 1267 (10), and the CHARLIE Act, HB 1792 (8).
- **Sources:** General Court bill status pages, dockets and chaptered bill texts, and RSA pages (gc.nh.gov); court orders and opinions (the October 2, 2025 preliminary injunction and September 4, 2025 temporary restraining order in NEA-NH v. NH Attorney General, hosted by ACLU-NH; Judge Barbadoro's May 28, 2024 opinion from the District of New Hampshire's site; Doe v. Manchester School District, hosted by ACLU-NH); CourtListener dockets; and the First Circuit's opinion search. News (NHPR, InDepthNH) was used only to find records and is mentioned only in notes.
- **Access problems on 2026-11-11:** gc.nh.gov refused all connections from about 00:05 to 01:00. Pages needed in that window were captured through the Wayback Machine that morning and are marked "Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11". The bill status pages and RSA 189:13-e, 189-B:2 and 189-B:4 were then downloaded live and match. courts.nh.gov and governor.nh.gov returned 403.
- **Review dates:** NH statutes and bill histories 2027-08-15; open lawsuits and the pending appeal 2027-05-11; closed court rulings and orders 2027-11-11.
- **Raw copies:** `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/` (PDFs with extracted `.txt`, HTML pages, Wayback captures named `*-wayback-YYYYMMDD.*`).

## rights-anti-dei-law-lawsuit: New Hampshire's 2025 Anti-DEI Law (HB 2) and the Federal Lawsuit Against It

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's 2025 anti-DEI law in the state budget (HB 2) and the NEA-NH federal lawsuit, with the 2025 restraining order and preliminary injunction against it.

1. New Hampshire's ban on "diversity, equity, and inclusion" (DEI) programs was enacted in the 2025 state budget bill, HB 2 (2025 chapter 141), and took effect July 1, 2025. Section 321 of the bill added RSA 21-I:112 to 21-I:116 for state agencies and political subdivisions (including school districts), and section 322 added RSA 186:71 to 186:77 for public schools.
   - citation: 2025 ch. 141:321, :322 and :461; National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), Order of Oct. 2, 2025 (2025 DNH 123 P), pp. 3-5
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/10/PI-Order.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The order notes (p. 3, n. 2) that HB 2 inserted the last section as "RSA 187:77"; it was renumbered RSA 186:77. The RSA source notes read "2025, 141:322, eff. July 1, 2025."

2. New Hampshire's 2025 budget bill, HB 2, passed in final form on June 26, 2025, when the House adopted the committee of conference report 184-183 and the Senate 16-8. Governor Kelly Ayotte signed it on June 27, 2025, as chapter 141.
   - citation: HB 2 (2025), bill docket, HJ 18 p. 42; SJ 17
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=1170&sy=2025&txtsessionyear=2025
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: These votes were on the whole budget bill, not on the DEI sections alone. Read from a Wayback capture made 2026-11-11 (raw: hb2-2025-legacy-docket-wayback-20261111.html) while gc.nh.gov refused direct connections.

3. New Hampshire law defines "DEI" as any program, policy, training or initiative that classifies people by a characteristic listed in the state's anti-discrimination law (such as race, sex, gender identity, age or disability) "for the purpose of achieving demographic outcomes, rather than treating individuals equally under the law." A "public school" under this law is any school, academic institution or college in the state supported by public funds.
   - citation: RSA 186:71, I-II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186/186-71.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-186-71-wayback-20261111.htm. The characteristics are those in RSA 354-A:1: age, sex, gender identity, race, creed, color, marital status, familial status, physical or mental disability, and national origin (PI order p. 4, n. 4).

4. New Hampshire's anti-DEI law bars public schools from implementing, promoting or engaging in "DEI-related" initiatives, programs, training or policies, and bars state funds for DEI-related activities, "including but not limited to implicit bias training, DEI assessments, critical race theory, or race-based hiring, promotion, or contracting preferences." Under a companion section, public schools also may not enter into, renew or amend contracts with DEI-related provisions.
   - citation: RSA 186:72; RSA 186:73
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186/186-72.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-186-72-wayback-20261111.htm and rsa-186-73-wayback-20261111.htm (RSA 186:73 is at https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186/186-73.htm). "DEI-related" is not defined in the law (PI order p. 32).

5. Under New Hampshire's anti-DEI law, each public school had to send the Commissioner of Education a signed, certified report of any contracts with DEI-related provisions by September 30, 2025. If a public school fails to follow the law, "either knowingly or unknowingly," the commissioner must immediately halt all sources of public funding to it until it complies.
   - citation: RSA 186:75, II; RSA 186:77, I
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186/186-77.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-186-77-wayback-20261111.htm and rsa-186-75-wayback-20261111.htm (RSA 186:75 is at https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/186/186-75.htm).

6. On August 7, 2025, the National Education Association-New Hampshire (NEA-NH), four school districts (Oyster River Cooperative, Dover, Somersworth and Grantham), two individuals who work on diversity programs, and New Hampshire Outright sued in federal court to block the anti-DEI law. The defendants are the Attorney General, the Commissioner of Education, the Commissioner of Administrative Services and the State Treasurer.
   - citation: National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), Order of Oct. 2, 2025, pp. 11-16
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/10/PI-Order.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: ACLU-NH, one of the plaintiffs' counsel, calls the case "NEA-NH v. New Hampshire Department of Justice"; the court caption is National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General. The claims: vagueness (Fourteenth Amendment), free speech (First Amendment), conflict with federal disability laws (ADA, Section 504, IDEA), and two state constitutional claims.

7. On September 4, 2025, Judge Landya McCafferty issued a temporary restraining order in the NEA-NH case. It barred the state from using the law's funding cutoff against covered public schools for not complying with RSA 186:72, 186:73 or 186:75, or with the Department of Education's July 11, 2025 letter, which asked K-12 schools to certify compliance by September 5, earlier than the law's September 30 deadline. On September 17 the order was extended to October 2, 2025.
   - citation: National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), Order Issuing Temporary Restraining Order, Sept. 4, 2025 (Doc. 38, 2025 DNH 105 P)
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/08/TRO-Decision.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Extension: endorsed order of Sept. 17, 2025 on the CourtListener docket (https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/71052463/national-education-association-new-hampshire-v-nh-attorney-general/). "Covered public school" meant one that employs, contracts or works with a plaintiff or a plaintiff's members or schools, or serves a plaintiff district's student under the ADA, Section 504 or IDEA.

8. On October 2, 2025, Judge McCafferty granted a preliminary injunction in the NEA-NH case. She found the plaintiffs likely to succeed on their claims that the anti-DEI laws are unconstitutionally vague and that they conflict with, and so are overridden by, three federal disability laws: the Americans with Disabilities Act, Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act and the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act. She did not decide the free speech claim.
   - citation: National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), Order of Oct. 2, 2025 (Doc. 40, 2025 DNH 123 P), pp. 25, 46-54
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/10/PI-Order.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11

9. In the October 2025 order, the court said the federal special education law (IDEA) "requires conduct that the anti-DEI laws forbid: classifying students based on disability in order to improve outcomes for students with disabilities." The state had argued that accommodations for disabled students serve to treat people "equally under the law" and so are allowed; the court said the state "conflate[s] equality of treatment with equality of opportunity."
   - citation: National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), Order of Oct. 2, 2025, pp. 49-50, 53-54
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/10/PI-Order.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11

10. The October 2025 preliminary injunction bars the four state officials from enforcing RSA 21-I:112 to 21-I:116 and RSA 186:71 to 186:77, including their reporting and certification requirements, against the plaintiffs and any "covered entity." A covered entity is a public school, state agency or political subdivision that employs, contracts with or works with a plaintiff or a plaintiff's members or schools, or that serves a student of a plaintiff school district under the federal disability laws. The court declined to block the law for everyone statewide.
    - citation: National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), Order of Oct. 2, 2025, pp. 61-67
    - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/10/PI-Order.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-05-11
    - note: The order does not count how many districts are covered. It describes NEA-NH as representing "the majority of public-school employees in New Hampshire" (p. 11), which is why news reports describe the injunction as reaching almost all districts; that is an inference, not the order's words. The injunction "shall remain in effect pending further order of this court" (p. 67).

11. As of November 10, 2026, the NEA-NH case had not been decided on the merits, and the preliminary injunction remained in place. The plaintiffs moved for summary judgment and a permanent injunction on May 20, 2026, and the state filed a cross-motion for summary judgment on October 2, 2026. The docket showed no ruling on either motion and no appeal of the preliminary injunction.
    - citation: National Education Association-New Hampshire v. NH Attorney General, No. 1:25-cv-00293-LM (D.N.H.), docket entries 51, 61-63
    - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/71052463/national-education-association-new-hampshire-v-nh-attorney-general/
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-05-11
    - note: CourtListener (PACER/RECAP) docket, last filing Nov. 10, 2026 (an order on a Nov. 9 motion to extend time to respond; the filer is not shown). Six districts (Brentwood, Exeter, Exeter Region Cooperative, Kensington, Newfields and Stratham) and School Administrative Unit 16 filed an amicus brief on June 5, 2026 (Doc. 56).

12. A 2026 bill to change the anti-DEI law, HB 1788, did not become law. Titled "holding state contracts with DEI provisions to be void as a matter of law and establishing a right of action for citizens," it passed the House 179-148 (division vote) on March 11, 2026, and the Senate referred it to interim study on April 16, 2026.
    - citation: HB 1788 (2026), bill docket, HJ 7 p. 171; SJ 9
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=2401&sy=2026&txtsessionyear=2026
    - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: Raw: hb1788-2026-legacy-docket-wayback-20261111.html. The legacy search results (Wayback 2026-11-11; raw: legacy-search-hb2-hb1788-hb50-wayback-20261111.html) show general status "SENATE" and Senate status "INTERIM STUDY", with no chapter number. The amended text was not read from gc.nh.gov.

13. A separate federal case, National Education Association v. U.S. Department of Education (No. 1:25-cv-00091-LM, D.N.H.), challenged the U.S. Department of Education's February 14, 2025 "Dear Colleague" letter on DEI. The court granted a preliminary injunction on April 24, 2025. On February 18, 2026, it granted the parties' joint motion to dismiss the case without prejudice under their stipulation, and judgment entered February 23, 2026.
    - citation: National Education Association v. US Department of Education, No. 1:25-cv-00091-LM (D.N.H.), docket entries 74, 97, 99, 100
    - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/69706414/national-education-association-v-us-department-of-education/
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11
    - note: Plaintiffs included NEA, NEA-NH, the Center for Black Educator Development and the Dover, Oyster River, Somersworth and Hanover/Dresden districts. Before the dismissal, the court (Jan. 27, 2026) asked the parties whether proceedings in American Federation of Teachers v. U.S. Department of Education, another case, had made the claims moot. The stipulation's terms were not retrieved.

## rights-divisive-concepts-law: New Hampshire's "Divisive Concepts" Law (RSA 193:40), the 2024 Ruling Against It and the Appeal

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's 2021 "divisive concepts" (banned concepts) law, RSA 193:40, the 2024 ruling that it is too vague, the First Circuit appeal and attempts to revise it.

1. New Hampshire's "divisive concepts" law, RSA 193:40, was enacted in the 2021 state budget bill, HB 2 (2021 chapter 91, section 298), effective June 25, 2021. It says no public school pupil may be "taught, instructed, inculcated or compelled to express belief in, or support for" four concepts about age, sex, gender identity, sexual orientation, race, creed, color, marital status, familial status, disability, religion or national origin.
   - citation: RSA 193:40, I; 2021 ch. 91:298
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-40.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: rsa-193-40-wayback-20261111.htm. The online RSA still prints the 2021 text with no note about the 2024 ruling.

2. The four concepts in RSA 193:40 are: that one group is inherently superior to another; that a person, because of one of those characteristics, is inherently racist, sexist or oppressive; that a person should be discriminated against or treated adversely because of one; and that people of one group cannot and should not try to treat others without regard to those characteristics. The law allows "discussing, as part of a larger course of academic instruction, the historical existence" of these ideas.
   - citation: RSA 193:40, I(a)-(d) and II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-40.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

3. Under RSA 193:40, anyone claiming to be harmed by a violation, including the Attorney General, may sue the school or district in superior court or file with the New Hampshire Commission for Human Rights. A violation by a certified educator counts as a violation of the educator code of conduct that can lead to discipline by the State Board of Education.
   - citation: RSA 193:40, III-V
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-40.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

4. In December 2021, two groups of educators sued over the divisive concepts law in federal court: AFT-NH Local 8027 with five educators, and the National Education Association-New Hampshire with two school DEI administrators. The cases were combined as Local 8027, AFT-N.H., AFL-CIO v. Edelblut (No. 1:21-cv-1077-PB, D.N.H.).
   - citation: Local 8027, AFT-N.H., AFL-CIO v. Edelblut, No. 21-cv-1077-PB, 2024 DNH 040 (D.N.H. May 28, 2024), pp. 8-9
   - source: https://www.nhd.uscourts.gov/sites/default/files/Opinions/2024/24NH040.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Per the CourtListener dockets, the AFT case was filed Dec. 13, 2021 as No. 1:21-cv-01063 and consolidated on March 14, 2022 into No. 1:21-cv-01077 (filed Dec. 20, 2021, by NEA-NH, Andres Mejia and Christina Kim Philibotte).

5. On May 28, 2024, U.S. District Judge Paul Barbadoro ruled for the educators in Local 8027 v. Edelblut. He held that the 2021 amendments "are viewpoint-based restrictions on speech that do not provide either fair warning to educators of what they prohibit or sufficient standards for law enforcement to prevent arbitrary and discriminatory enforcement," and so violate the Fourteenth Amendment.
   - citation: Local 8027, AFT-N.H., AFL-CIO v. Edelblut, No. 21-cv-1077-PB, 2024 DNH 040 (D.N.H. May 28, 2024), p. 49
   - source: https://www.nhd.uscourts.gov/sites/default/files/Opinions/2024/24NH040.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

6. Judge Barbadoro's May 2024 ruling invalidated RSA 193:40 and two parallel sections of the state's anti-discrimination law, RSA 354-A:31 (public employers) and RSA 354-A:32 (government programs). It left RSA 354-A:29 and 354-A:33 in place. He granted a declaratory judgment but no injunction, saying he had no reason to believe the state would not respect the ruling.
   - citation: Local 8027, AFT-N.H., AFL-CIO v. Edelblut, No. 21-cv-1077-PB, 2024 DNH 040 (D.N.H. May 28, 2024), pp. 47-50
   - source: https://www.nhd.uscourts.gov/sites/default/files/Opinions/2024/24NH040.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The state had asked the court to strike only RSA 193:40, IV (the educator-discipline paragraph); the court declined (pp. 47-49).

7. Judgment in the Local 8027 case was entered on June 24, 2024. On July 24, 2024, the state defendants (the Attorney General, the Commissioner of Education, the Commissioner of Labor, and the Commission for Human Rights' chair and executive director) filed a notice of appeal to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the First Circuit, where the appeal is No. 24-1690.
   - citation: Local 8027, AFT-N.H., AFL-CIO v. NH Department of Education, Commissioner, No. 1:21-cv-01077 (D.N.H.), docket entries 111-114
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/61629085/local-8027-aft-new-hampshire-afl-cio-v-nh-department-of-education/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: The October 2, 2025 NEA-NH order (p. 1) cites the appeal as "No. 24-1690 (1st Cir. July 26, 2024)." The First Circuit heard argument on April 8, 2025 (NHPR, https://www.nhpr.org/education/2025-04-08/banned-concepts-divisive-dei-new-hampshire-ban-classroom-teaching-lawsuit-appeal); the appellate docket itself was not retrieved.

8. As of November 11, 2026, the First Circuit had not issued an opinion in the state's appeal of the divisive concepts ruling: the court's opinion search returned no result for No. 24-1690.
   - citation: U.S. Court of Appeals for the First Circuit, opinion search, No. 24-1690
   - source: https://www.ca1.uscourts.gov/opn/aci?OPINNUM=24-1690
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: The same search for another case number (25-1034) returned its October 26, 2026 opinion, so the search was working. The district court docket (CourtListener, last filing Jan. 5, 2026) shows no appellate judgment or mandate. InDepthNH reported on May 21, 2026 that the appeal was still pending. An order or judgment without an opinion would not show in the opinion search; the appellate docket (PACER) was not retrieved.

9. A 2025 bill to revise the divisive concepts law, HB 50, retitled "relative to intentional or knowing violation of the prohibition on teaching discrimination," passed the New Hampshire House 183-157 on March 26, 2025. The Senate killed it on May 15, 2025, voting it inexpedient to legislate on a voice vote.
   - citation: HB 50 (2025), bill docket, HJ 10 p. 115; SJ 13
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/bill_docket.aspx?lsr=2&sy=2025&txtsessionyear=2025
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: hb50-2025-legacy-docket-lsr2-wayback-20261111.html. The Senate Education Committee had recommended killing it 4-1.

10. In 2026, the Senate's version of HB 1792 would have re-enacted RSA 193:40 so that it applied only to "purposeful" teaching of the four concepts and without the right to sue. On May 21, 2026, the House voted 127-222 against agreeing to that version and then voted not to concur, and the bill did not become law. (See the topic on HB 1792, the CHARLIE Act.)
    - citation: HB 1792 (2026), bill docket, HJ 14 pp. 8, 10
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1642&inflect=2
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15

## rights-parental-bill-of-rights-history: HB 10 (2025), the Parental Bill of Rights: Votes, Signing and 2026 Changes

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How NH's Parental Bill of Rights (HB 10, 2025, RSA 189-B) passed, and the 2026 changes to its rule on recording students (HB 266, SB 429).

1. New Hampshire's Parental Bill of Rights began as HB 10 (2025), with Rep. Packard as prime sponsor. The House Children and Family Law Committee recommended passing it with an amendment on a 9-7 vote on March 4, 2025.
   - citation: HB 10 (2025), bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=49&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Sponsors listed: Packard (prime), Kofalt, Terry, DeSimone, Post, Osborne, all Republicans.

2. The New Hampshire House passed HB 10 with its committee amendment on March 20, 2025, on a 212-161 roll call. Earlier that day, a motion to table the bill failed 162-211.
   - citation: HB 10 (2025), bill docket, HJ 9 pp. 26, 28
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=49&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

3. The New Hampshire Senate passed HB 10 on June 5, 2025, on a voice vote, with two amendments (its Education Committee's amendment, recommended 3-2, and a floor amendment by Sen. Birdsell). The same day the House agreed to the Senate's changes on a 210-160 roll call.
   - citation: HB 10 (2025), bill docket, SJ 15; HJ 16 p. 71
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=49&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The House docket entry reads "House Concurs with Senate Amendment 2025-2150s (Rep. DeSimone): MA RC 210-160."

4. Governor Kelly Ayotte signed HB 10 on June 10, 2025. It became 2025 chapter 74, effective July 1, 2025.
   - citation: HB 10 (2025), chapter 74
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=49&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

5. New Hampshire changed the Parental Bill of Rights' recording rule twice in 2026. The first change was in HB 266 (2026 chapter 4), which Governor Ayotte signed on March 3, 2026; its parental-rights sections took effect that day. The recording sections were added by Senate floor amendments from Sen. Birdsell on February 19, 2026, and the House agreed the same day on a voice vote.
   - citation: HB 266 (2025-26 session), chapter 4; bill docket, SJ 4; HJ 5 p. 89
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=533&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: HB 266 began in 2025 as a Department of Energy bill (prime sponsor Rep. Harrington); chapter 4 also changed inheritance law for murder cases. The docket's effective-date entry: "Sec 5-8 eff 3/3/2026; Rem eff 5/2/2026."

6. HB 266 (2026 chapter 4) exempted from the Parental Bill of Rights' written-consent rule recordings made while giving a required assessment and recordings made at school-sponsored events open to the general public. It defined "open to the general public" as an event generally open to parents, guardians, family and community members, even if attendance needs registration, tickets or an invitation, and "required assessment" as any standardized assessment required by state or federal statute or regulation.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 4:5 and 4:6; RSA 189-B:2, VII-VIII; RSA 189-B:4, I(p)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?id=533&sy=2026&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Chaptered final text (raw: hb266-2026-billtext-wayback-20261111.html). The live RSA 189-B:2 page (read 2026-11-11) prints the same two definitions with the source note "2026, 4:5, eff. March 3, 2026."

7. The second change was in SB 429 (2026 chapter 336), which Governor Ayotte signed on July 15, 2026, and which also requires trauma kits in public schools. It repealed and rewrote the recording paragraph, RSA 189-B:4, I(p), effective July 15, 2026, keeping the earlier exemptions and adding recordings made as part of the school's regular instructional coursework and as part of an approved student journalism, media production or broadcasting course or school-sponsored extracurricular activity.
   - citation: SB 429 (2026), chapter 336:4 and 336:5; RSA 189-B:4, I(p)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?id=1410&sy=2026&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Chaptered final text (raw: sb429-2026-billtext-wayback-20261111.html). The live status page (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1410&inflect=2, read 2026-11-11) shows "Signed by the Governor on 07/15/2026; Chapter 336." SB 429 was introduced and passed by the Senate as a trauma-kit bill (introduced text, read live 2026-11-11: sb429-2026-introduced.html); the House amended it (2026-1351h, which retitled it), and both chambers adopted a committee of conference report on June 4, 2026. The online RSA 189-B:4, I(p) still prints chapter 4's "or such recording is made as a component of the administration of a required assessment or is made during a school-sponsored event that is open to the general public" alongside chapter 336's list, although chapter 336 repealed and reenacted the paragraph.

## rights-sb-430-parent-questions: SB 430 (2026), Teachers Answering Parents' Written Questions, and HB 1267 (2026) on Questioning Students

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: SB 430 (2026) on teachers answering parents' written questions (does it mention gender identity?), Doe v. Manchester, and HB 1267 on questioning students.

1. SB 430 (2026), named in its text the "Honesty and Transparency in Education Act," was sponsored by Sen. Tim Lang with 13 other Republican senators and two Republican House members. The New Hampshire Senate passed it with an amendment on February 19, 2026, on a 16-8 roll call.
   - citation: SB 430 (2026), bill docket, SJ 4; 2026 ch. 248:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1194&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The short title is in section 1 of the chaptered text (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?id=1194&sy=2026&txtFormat=html, Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11; raw: sb430-2026-billtext-wayback-20261111.html).

2. The New Hampshire House passed SB 430 with its own amendment on May 14, 2026, on a 193-163 roll call, after its Education Policy and Administration Committee recommended it 10-8. The Senate agreed to the House's changes on May 21, 2026, on a 14-8 roll call.
   - citation: SB 430 (2026), bill docket, HJ 13 p. 40; SJ 13
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1194&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

3. Governor Kelly Ayotte signed SB 430 on July 2, 2026. It became 2026 chapter 248 and takes effect January 1, 2027, adding RSA 189:13-e.
   - citation: SB 430 (2026), chapter 248; RSA 189:13-e
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1194&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

4. The text of New Hampshire's SB 430, as enacted in RSA 189:13-e, does not mention gender identity or list any particular subject. It covers parents' and legal guardians' written questions "regarding information relating to their child," which must be answered "completely and honestly, except where prohibited by state or federal law."
   - citation: RSA 189:13-e, I (2026 ch. 248:2, eff. Jan. 1, 2027)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-13-e.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The introduced bill (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&v=SI&id=1194&txtFormat=html, Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11) also did not mention gender identity; it covered "material information relating to their child" and would have had educators report to DHHS and withhold only the information creating the risk.

5. Under RSA 189:13-e, an educator who decides in good faith that a complete and honest answer would put the child at imminent risk of abuse or neglect must file a report with the superintendent, and the parent's request must be denied in writing.
   - citation: RSA 189:13-e, II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-13-e.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: "Abuse or neglect" is as defined in RSA 169-C:3.

6. Under RSA 189:13-e, the State Board of Education must adopt rules by June 30, 2027 adding the duty to answer parents to the educator code of ethics and code of conduct. A violation counts as a violation of the educator code of conduct and can be investigated against the educator's credential.
   - citation: RSA 189:13-e, III-IV
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-13-e.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

7. In Doe v. Manchester School District (2024 N.H. 48, decided August 30, 2024), the New Hampshire Supreme Court upheld the dismissal of a parent's lawsuit against a Manchester school policy on transgender and gender non-conforming students. The policy said staff "should not disclose information that may reveal a student's transgender status or gender nonconforming presentation to others including parents" unless legally required or the student agreed.
   - citation: Doe v. Manchester Sch. Dist., 2024 N.H. 48, No. 2022-0537 (Aug. 30, 2024), ¶¶ 1-2, 17
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2024/08/2024048manchester.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Copy of the slip opinion hosted by ACLU-NH (counsel for amici); courts.nh.gov refused downloads (403) on 2026-11-11.

8. In Doe v. Manchester, the court held 3-1 that the parent had not shown the policy infringes a fundamental parenting right under Part I, Article 2 of the New Hampshire Constitution, so the policy faced only rational-basis review. The majority wrote: "We cannot conclude that any interference with parental rights which may result from non-disclosure is of constitutional dimension." Justice Countway dissented, saying strict scrutiny should apply.
   - citation: Doe v. Manchester Sch. Dist., 2024 N.H. 48, ¶¶ 14-15, 17-18
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2024/08/2024048manchester.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Chief Justice MacDonald wrote the opinion; Justices Bassett and Donovan concurred; Justice Hicks heard argument but did not take part in the final vote. The court noted the policy "does not require non-disclosure" and "does permit non-disclosure in response to a parent's question" (¶ 14).

9. The Doe v. Manchester appeal decided only the parent's claim under the New Hampshire Constitution. Her other claims, that the policy was beyond the district's authority and violated the federal FERPA and PPRA student-privacy laws, were dismissed by the trial court and treated as waived on appeal because she did not brief them.
   - citation: Doe v. Manchester Sch. Dist., 2024 N.H. 48, ¶¶ 6-8
   - source: https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2024/08/2024048manchester.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: NHPR (July 7, 2026) described SB 430 as "effectively overriding" the Doe decision. SB 430's enacted text does not mention the case or the Manchester policy.

10. The other 2027 parent-rights change, the required school board policy on questioning students (RSA 189:1-a, VI), came from a separate bill, HB 1267 (2026). Governor Ayotte vetoed HB 1267 on May 28, 2026. On August 19, 2026, the House overrode the veto 311-6 and the Senate 24-0, and it became 2026 chapter 340 without her signature, effective January 1, 2027.
    - citation: HB 1267 (2026), chapter 340; RSA 189:1-a, VI
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1707&inflect=2
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: Earlier, the House passed HB 1267 on a voice vote (Feb. 12, 2026) and the Senate on a voice vote (Apr. 16, 2026). The governor's veto message was not retrieved (governor.nh.gov returned 403).

## rights-hb-1792-charlie-act: HB 1792 (2026), the "CHARLIE Act" on Classroom Teaching: What It Proposed and How It Died

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1792 (2026), the "CHARLIE Act" limiting what teachers present in class, the Senate's divisive-concepts rewrite, and how the bill died.

1. As introduced on January 7, 2026, New Hampshire's HB 1792 said it "may also be cited as the 'Countering Hate And Revolutionary Leftist Indoctrination in Education Act' or the 'CHARLIE Act.'" Its prime sponsor was Rep. Belcher, with Reps. Noble, Osborne, Corcoran and Sabourin dit Choinière, all Republicans.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), as introduced, section 1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&v=HI&id=1642&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: hb1792-2026-introduced-wayback-20261111.html. Party labels and the introduction date are from the live status page (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1642&inflect=2).

2. As introduced, HB 1792 would have added RSA 193:40-a, barring public schools, districts and public employees acting officially from "the pedagogy, praxis, or inculcation of critical theories or related practices that promote division, dialectical world-views, critical consciousness, or anti-constitutional indoctrination." Its listed examples included culturally relevant or responsive teaching that "prioritizes identity-based division" and "inculcating LGBTQ+ ideology, such as requiring affirmation of 'gender fluidity,' 'non-binary' or 'queer' identities." Factual, neutral teaching about historical events was allowed.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), as introduced, section 3 (proposed RSA 193:40-a, II-III)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&v=HI&id=1642&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

3. As introduced, HB 1792 would have let a student, parent or other aggrieved person sue the public school, district or agency within one year for an injunction, compensatory damages of up to $10,000 per violation, and attorney fees. Proven violations by school employees could have led to discipline up to revocation of a teaching certificate. It would not have applied to public colleges, private schools or home schools.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), as introduced, section 3 (proposed RSA 193:40-a, VI, VII and IX)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&v=HI&id=1642&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

4. The New Hampshire House passed HB 1792 without changes on February 19, 2026, on a 184-164 roll call. Its Education Policy and Administration Committee had recommended it 9-8.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), bill docket, HC 7 p. 30; HJ 5 p. 59
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1642&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

5. In the New Hampshire Senate, the Education Committee recommended an amendment to HB 1792 (2026-1744s) on a 3-2 vote. On May 14, 2026, the Senate rejected that committee amendment on a voice vote, adopted Sen. Abbas's floor amendment (2026-1779s) on a 15-8 roll call, and passed the bill as amended on a voice vote.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), bill docket, SC 17-18; SJ 12
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1642&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

6. The Senate's version of HB 1792 replaced the whole bill and its title, dropping the CHARLIE Act name and the proposed RSA 193:40-a. It would instead have repealed and re-enacted the 2021 "divisive concepts" law, RSA 193:40, with the same four concepts but covering only pupils "purposefully" taught them, making only a "purposeful violation" by an educator a code of conduct violation, and removing the right of aggrieved people to sue or to go to the Commission for Human Rights.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), as amended by the Senate (amendment 2026-1779s), section 1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?id=1642&sy=2026&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Raw: hb1792-2026-billtext-wayback-20261111.html and the floor amendment PDF, hb1792-2026-amendment-1779s-wayback-20261111.pdf. The new title: "relative to the prohibition on teaching discrimination."

7. On May 21, 2026, the New Hampshire House voted 127-222 against a motion to agree to the Senate's version of HB 1792. It then voted, on a voice vote, not to concur.
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), bill docket, HJ 14 pp. 8, 10
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1642&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

8. HB 1792 did not become law. As of November 11, 2026, its status page lists no action after the House's May 21, 2026 vote not to concur, no committee of conference and no chapter number, with general status "HOUSE" and House status "NONCONCURRED."
   - citation: HB 1792 (2026), bill status
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1642&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: InDepthNH (May 21, 2026) described the 127-222 vote as "killing the bill." With no new law, RSA 193:40 still reads as enacted in 2021 (see rights-divisive-concepts-law).

---

# School Climate, Students and School Standards

**Sources and notes for this section**

- **Topics:** school materials challenge bills (HB 324, SB 434); the girls' sports law (HB 1205) and the Tirrell lawsuit; the "bathroom bill" vetoes; the bell-to-bell phone law and NH DOE's 2026 educator survey; school vaccine requirements for 2026-27 and the 2025-26 vaccine bills; the 2024 rewrite of the Ed 306 school approval rules.
- **Sources:** General Court bill status pages, chaptered bill text and RSA pages (gc.nh.gov; some read from Wayback snapshots because gc.nh.gov refused connections from this machine for much of November 11, 2026); the Governor's press releases with veto statements (governor.nh.gov); NH DOE's survey memo, rule texts and rules pages; NH DHHS's 2026-27 school immunization requirements and exemptions page; the Tirrell v. Edelblut docket, orders and stipulation on CourtListener.
- **Dates:** sources read 2026-11-11 unless marked as a Wayback snapshot. Review dates: NH statutes, rules and enacted or vetoed bills 2027-08-15; court and survey facts 2027-11-11.
- **Size:** 6 topics, 65 facts: rights-school-materials-complaint-bills (9), rights-hb-1205-girls-sports-law (11), rights-bathroom-bill-vetoes (11), climate-phone-free-school-day (10), students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026 (12), gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite (12). Nine gaps are listed under Not retrieved.
- **Corrections to the leads:** NH DOE's phone survey covered 1,399 teachers plus 158 administrators (not 1,399 in all), was taken in spring 2026 and released July 28, 2026, and reports 79% of teachers and 87% of administrators (the Governor's release rounded to "nearly 80" and "about 88"). The Tirrell case ended by a joint stipulation of all parties, with prejudice, not by the students alone. The 2026 exemption-notice law is HB 1584 (2026 ch. 328), which adds a new section rather than amending RSA 141-C:20-c. HB 1815 (2026) defines an adequate education "as the school approval standards" in 11 learning areas; the rule that later Ed 306 changes do not count toward adequacy without legislative adoption is older (RSA 193-E:2-a, IV(a)).
- **Raw copies:** `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/`.

## rights-school-materials-complaint-bills: School Book and Materials Challenge Bills: HB 324 (2025) and SB 434 (2026) Vetoes

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's "harmful to minors" school library and materials bills (HB 324, 2025; SB 434, 2026): the vetoes, failed overrides and the parent objection law.

1. New Hampshire's HB 324 (2025) would have barred schools, school employees and contractors from making material "harmful to minors" available to any minor student, and required school boards to adopt, by November 1, 2026, a complaint process: a parent's written complaint to the principal, a decision within 10 school days, an appeal to the school board, and a further appeal to the State Board of Education. The attorney general, the Department of Education or an aggrieved person could have sued a school district in superior court, with damages of at least $1,000 per violation plus legal fees.
   - citation: HB 324 (2025), final version, section 2 (proposed RSA 189:79-189:81)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2025&id=418&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-02-22
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/rights-school-materials-complaint-bills/hb324-2025-text-wayback-20260222.html). The bill would also have made an educator's violation, after a final determination, a breach of the educator code of conduct (proposed RSA 189:81, III).
2. New Hampshire Governor Kelly Ayotte vetoed HB 324 (2025), titled "relative to prohibiting obscene or harmful sexual materials in schools," on July 15, 2025.
   - citation: Governor's press release "Governor Ayotte Signs 101 Bills into Law," July 15, 2025
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-101-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
3. In her HB 324 veto statement, Governor Ayotte said current law "appears to provide a mechanism for parents through their local school district" (citing RSA 186:11, IX-c), and that the state should not "engage in the role of addressing questions of literary value and appropriateness, particularly where the system created by House Bill 324 calls for monetary penalties based on subjective standards." She also wrote that the bill "could open the door to unnecessary litigation from out-of-state groups," and that if RSA 186:11, IX-c is not being carried out adequately, "the more suitable and narrow way" would be to change that statute.
   - citation: HB 324 (2025) veto statement, in the Governor's press release of July 15, 2025
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-101-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The existing topic opting-out-curriculum-surveys-tests (facts 1-3) states what RSA 186:11, IX-c requires; it is not repeated here.
4. New Hampshire's HB 324 (2025) passed the House 183-148 on March 26, 2025 and the Senate 15-8 on May 15, 2025. After the veto, the House voted 183-167 on December 17, 2025, short of the two-thirds needed to override, so HB 324 did not become law.
   - citation: HB 324 (2025) bill status and docket (HJ 10 P. 120; SJ 13; HJ 19)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=418&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-04-29
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/rights-school-materials-complaint-bills/hb324-2025-billinfo-wayback-20260429.html).
5. New Hampshire's SB 434 (2026), called the "Right To Challenge Act," would have required each local school board to adopt, by November 1, 2026, a policy for handling parents' complaints that school material is "obscene and harmful to minors," and to post it on the district website. "Material" would have included books, videos, web-based content, live performances, artwork and other items the district provides. Material "harmful to minors" would have been material that, taken as a whole, appeals to minors' prurient interest in sex, depicts sexual conduct in a patently offensive way under contemporary adult standards in the county, and lacks serious literary, artistic, political or scientific value.
   - citation: SB 434 (2026), final version, sections 1-2 (proposed RSA 189:74-a)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1219&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-07
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/rights-school-materials-complaint-bills/sb434-2026-text-wayback-20260907.html), which is marked "VETOED July 2, 2026 / Veto Sustained August 19, 2026."
6. Under New Hampshire's vetoed SB 434, by November 1, 2027 school boards would also have needed a complaint procedure for material a parent says is "harmful to minors, age-inappropriate, or otherwise offensive or inappropriate." A parent would complain in writing to the superintendent, who would look into it within 10 school days, decide within 25 school days whether the material stays, is removed, restricted or changed, and answer in writing within 5 calendar days after deciding. The parent could appeal to the school board, which would issue a signed written decision within 30 days showing how each member voted; all decisions would be public records.
   - citation: SB 434 (2026), final version, section 2 (proposed RSA 189:74-b)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1219&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-07
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Unlike HB 324 (2025), SB 434's final text had no provision for lawsuits, damages or State Board appeals (derived from reading both final texts).
7. New Hampshire's SB 434 (2026), "relative to regulation of public school materials," passed the Senate 16-8 on February 19, 2026 and the House, with an amendment, 188-161 on May 14, 2026; the Senate agreed to the House version 14-8 on May 21, 2026. Its prime sponsor was Sen. Lang.
   - citation: SB 434 (2026) bill status and docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1219&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
8. Governor Ayotte vetoed New Hampshire's SB 434 on July 2, 2026. Her veto statement said: "This bill is similar to the bill I vetoed last year," and "As current law provides a mechanism for parents through their local school district to exercise their rights to ensure their children are not exposed to inappropriate materials, I have vetoed Senate Bill 434." It again cited RSA 186:11, IX-c, the law requiring districts to allow an alternative to specific course material a parent objects to.
   - citation: SB 434 veto statement, in the Governor's press release "Governor Signs 74 Bills into Law," July 2, 2026
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-74-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The bill docket also records "Vetoed by Governor 07/02/2026."
9. On August 19, 2026, the New Hampshire Senate voted 16-8 to override the SB 434 veto, but the House vote was 165-140, short of the two-thirds needed, so the veto was sustained and SB 434 did not become law.
   - citation: SB 434 (2026) docket, August 19, 2026 entries (SJ 15; HJ 16 P. 56)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1219&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15

## rights-hb-1205-girls-sports-law: HB 1205 (2024): Girls' School Sports Limited by Sex at Birth, and the Tirrell Lawsuit

- kind: rule
- question_type: rules
- description: NH's 2024 girls' sports law (HB 1205), which sets school teams in grades 5-12 by sex at birth on the birth certificate, and the Tirrell v. Edelblut lawsuit and its dismissal.

1. New Hampshire enacted HB 1205 (2024), "an act relative to women's school sports," on July 19, 2024 as 2024 chapter 228. It created RSA 193:41 and 193:42 and took effect August 18, 2024.
   - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H. Sept. 10, 2024), 2024 DNH 073, "Overview of HB 1205," pp. 8-10
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10693724/parker-tirrell-et-al-v-p-frank-edelblut-et-al/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The RSA 193:41 source note reads "2024, 228:1, eff. Aug. 18, 2024" (https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-41.htm, Wayback snapshot 2026-04-19; gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; raw/state-issues/rights-hb-1205-girls-sports-law/rsa-193-41-wayback-20260419.htm).
2. Under New Hampshire's RSA 193:41, interscholastic and club teams of public middle schools (any of grades 5-8) and high schools (any of grades 9-12), and of private schools whose teams compete against public schools, must be designated for males, for females, or coed, "based on the biological sex at birth of intended participants." Teams for girls "shall not be open to students of the male sex." The law does not apply to kindergarten through grade 4, and it does not limit coed intramural activities.
   - citation: RSA 193:41, I, II and VI
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-41.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-04-19
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
3. Under New Hampshire's RSA 193:41, a student's sex for school sports is the sex on an official birth certificate issued at or near birth, or one changed only to fix a clerical error. If the certificate does not appear to be the original or does not show sex at birth, the student must provide other evidence, and the family pays any costs.
   - citation: RSA 193:41, III-IV
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-41.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-04-19
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. New Hampshire's RSA 193:41 requires the State Board of Education, every local school board and every charter school board to adopt and enforce policies to comply. It also bars any government body, licensing or accrediting group, or athletic association from investigating or taking other adverse action against a school for keeping separate teams for female students.
   - citation: RSA 193:41, V and VII
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-41.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-04-19
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. New Hampshire's RSA 193:42 lets a student who loses an athletic opportunity because a school knowingly violates the sports law, a student who faces retaliation for reporting a violation, and a school harmed by a violation sue for an injunction and damages, including for psychological, emotional and physical harm, plus attorneys' fees. The law places no limits on who may play on boys' teams.
   - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, 2024 DNH 073, pp. 8-10 (quoting RSA 193:42, I-IV)
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10693724/parker-tirrell-et-al-v-p-frank-edelblut-et-al/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The opinion says "HB 1205 places no restrictions on who may play on teams designated for males, men, or boys." The RSA 193:42 page itself was not retrieved (see Not retrieved).
6. Two transgender girls, Parker Tirrell and Iris Turmelle, sued New Hampshire's Education Commissioner, the State Board of Education members, and the Pemi-Baker Regional and Pembroke school districts and their boards in federal court on August 16, 2024 (Tirrell v. Edelblut). They claimed that enforcing HB 1205 against them violated the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment and Title IX.
   - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H.), order of Sept. 10, 2024 (2024 DNH 073), p. 1; docket (date filed)
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10693724/parker-tirrell-et-al-v-p-frank-edelblut-et-al/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Filing date from the CourtListener docket, https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/69050456/tirrell-v-edelblut/ ("Date Filed: Aug. 16, 2024").
7. In Tirrell v. Edelblut, Chief Judge Landya McCafferty granted Parker Tirrell a temporary restraining order on August 19, 2024, and on September 10, 2024 granted both students a preliminary injunction. The court found they were likely to succeed on both their Equal Protection and Title IX claims, and ordered the state and their school districts to let the two students try out for and play on girls' teams. Because the students asked only for relief for themselves, the injunction covered HB 1205 only as applied to them, not the whole law.
   - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H. Sept. 10, 2024), 2024 DNH 073, sections I, II and VI and Conclusion
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10693724/parker-tirrell-et-al-v-p-frank-edelblut-et-al/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The TRO date is from the docket entry for doc. 6 ("ORDER granting 6 Motion for Temporary Restraining Order ... (Entered: 08/19/2024)") and the written TRO order of Aug. 22, 2024 (2024 DNH 069).
8. On February 12, 2025, the Tirrell plaintiffs filed an amended complaint adding federal defendants: President Trump, Attorney General Pamela Bondi, Denise L. Carter, the U.S. Department of Justice and the U.S. Department of Education. On July 15, 2025, the court allowed a group called Female Athletes United to join the case as a defendant.
   - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H.), docket entries 95 (Feb. 12, 2025) and 133 (July 15, 2025)
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/69050456/tirrell-v-edelblut/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
9. In August 2025, the federal court put Tirrell v. Edelblut on hold, at the parties' joint request, until the U.S. Supreme Court decided two transgender-athlete cases, Little v. Hecox and West Virginia v. B.P.J. On June 30, 2026, the court lifted the hold, saying the Supreme Court's B.P.J. opinion "has significantly impacted the legal landscape applicable to plaintiffs' claims," and denied the pending motions to dismiss without prejudice to refiling.
   - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H.), endorsed orders of Aug. 15, 2025 (granting doc. 139) and June 30, 2026
   - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/69050456/tirrell-v-edelblut/
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
10. On July 8, 2026, all parties in Tirrell v. Edelblut, including the state, both school districts, the federal defendants and Female Athletes United, filed a stipulation dismissing the case with prejudice, with no attorneys' fees or costs to either side. The court docket shows the case closed on July 10, 2026, so the injunction no longer protects the two students and RSA 193:41-42 is not blocked by any order in this case.
    - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H.), Stipulation of Dismissal, doc. 142 (July 8, 2026), under Fed. R. Civ. P. 41(a)(1)(A)(ii)
    - source: https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.nhd.63968/gov.uscourts.nhd.63968.142.0.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11
    - note: Case closing date from the docket entry "Terminate Civil Case" dated Jul 10, 2026 (https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/69050456/tirrell-v-edelblut/?order_by=desc). The stipulation names Caitlin Davis as Education Commissioner in place of Frank Edelblut. "No longer protects" is derived: a stipulated dismissal with prejudice ends the case, and with it the preliminary injunction.
11. The Tirrell v. Edelblut district court docket lists no notice of appeal to the First Circuit from the 2024 injunction or any other order, and the case ended by agreement in the district court.
    - citation: Tirrell v. Edelblut, No. 1:24-cv-00251 (D.N.H.), full docket
    - source: https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/69050456/tirrell-v-edelblut/
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11
    - note: All three docket pages (Aug. 2024 to July 2026) were searched for "appeal" and "USCA" with no match, and a CourtListener search of First Circuit records for "Tirrell Edelblut" found no case. The related First Circuit cases found were Local 8027, AFT-NH v. Edelblut (No. 24-1690, the "divisive concepts" law) and Fellers v. Kelley (No. 25-1442, a Bow protest case).

## rights-bathroom-bill-vetoes: "Bathroom Bill" Vetoes, 2025-2026 (HB 148, SB 268, SB 552, HB 1442)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH "bathroom bills" letting restrooms, locker rooms and sports be separated by biological sex (HB 148, SB 268, SB 552, HB 1442), whether they cover schools, and the vetoes.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1442 (2026), "permitting classification of individuals based on biological sex under certain limited circumstances," would have added RSA 354-A:25-a to the state Law Against Discrimination. It said it is not unlawful sex or gender identity discrimination "for any person or organization, public or private," to classify by biological sex in multi-person restrooms and locker rooms; in sports in which strength, speed or endurance gives biological males an advantage; and in prisons, juvenile detention centers and similar facilities where people may be held involuntarily.
   - citation: HB 1442 (2026), final version, section 2 (proposed RSA 354-A:25-a, I)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1937&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-07
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/rights-bathroom-bill-vetoes/hb1442-2026-text-wayback-20260907.html).
2. New Hampshire's HB 1442 did not mention schools: its text applied to "any person or organization, public or private," with no exception for schools. It also said it "does not mean that any public or private entity is required by state statute to separate persons based upon biological sex," so it would have permitted separation, not required it.
   - citation: HB 1442 (2026), final version, proposed RSA 354-A:25-a, I-II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1937&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-07
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Whether public schools would be covered rests on the words "public or private"; the bill text does not name them either way. Girls' school sports teams are already governed by RSA 193:41 (see rights-hb-1205-girls-sports-law).
3. Governor Ayotte vetoed New Hampshire's HB 148 (2025), "permitting classification of individuals based on biological sex under certain circumstances," on July 15, 2025. Her veto statement said: "I believe there are important and legitimate privacy and safety concerns raised by biological males using places such as female locker rooms and being placed in female correctional facilities. At the same time, I see that House Bill 148 is overly broad and impractical to enforce, potentially creating an exclusionary environment for some of our citizens."
   - citation: HB 148 (2025) veto statement, in the Governor's press release "Governor Ayotte Signs 101 Bills into Law," July 15, 2025
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-101-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. In the same HB 148 veto statement, Governor Ayotte said that state law already bars "biological males from competing in girls' sports in K-12 schools" (citing RSA 193:41 and 193:42), that the bill's sports language was "largely redundant and, frankly, weaker than the current law," and that Governor Sununu had vetoed "this exact piece of legislation" the year before.
   - citation: HB 148 (2025) veto statement, July 15, 2025
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-101-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: RSA 193:41 itself covers grades 5-12 and excludes kindergarten through grade 4, although the statement says "K-12."
5. New Hampshire's HB 148 (2025) passed the House 201-166 on March 20, 2025 and the Senate 16-8 on May 22, 2025. After the veto, the House voted 188-165 on December 17, 2025, short of two-thirds, so the veto was sustained.
   - citation: HB 148 (2025) bill status and docket (HJ 9 P. 59; SJ 14; HJ 19)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=442&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-06-01
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from Wayback copies (raw/state-issues/rights-bathroom-bill-vetoes/hb148-2025-billinfo-wayback-20260601.html; bill text hb148-2025-text-wayback-20260909.html, from https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2025&id=442&txtFormat=html). HB 148's final text would have added RSA 354-A:25-a with the same three categories as HB 1442 (multi-person restrooms and locker rooms, sports, and places of involuntary confinement).
6. Governor Ayotte vetoed New Hampshire's SB 268 on February 6, 2026. On August 19, 2026, the Senate voted 0-24 on overriding the veto, so the veto was sustained.
   - citation: SB 268 (2025-2026) bill status and docket (SJ 15)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1139&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-02
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/rights-bathroom-bill-vetoes/sb268-2026-billinfo-wayback-20261002.html). SB 268 passed the Senate 16-8 on March 27, 2025 and the House 185-159 on January 8, 2026. The veto statement (https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-two-bills-law) said "there is minimal difference between Senate Bill 268 and the bill I vetoed last year."
7. Governor Ayotte vetoed New Hampshire's SB 552 on June 19, 2026. Her veto statement said: "I have continued to ask the Legislature to address this issue in a thoughtful, narrow way while protecting the privacy, safety, and rights of all Granite Staters," and "Trying the same thing again isn't going to get a different result."
   - citation: SB 552 veto statement, in the Governor's press release "Governor Signs 54 Bills into Law," June 19, 2026
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-54-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
8. On August 19, 2026, the New Hampshire Senate voted 16-8 to override the SB 552 veto, but the House vote of 161-142 fell short of two-thirds, so the veto was sustained and SB 552 did not become law.
   - citation: SB 552 (2026) bill status and docket (SJ 15; HJ 16)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1340&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-08-21
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/rights-bathroom-bill-vetoes/sb552-2026-billinfo-wayback-20260821.html). SB 552 passed the Senate 16-8 on March 5, 2026 and the House 179-159 on May 14, 2026.
9. New Hampshire's HB 1442 passed the House 181-164 on March 5, 2026 and the Senate, with an amendment, 15-8 on April 16, 2026; the House agreed to the Senate version 184-155 on May 21, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1442 (2026) bill status and docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1937&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-12
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
10. Governor Ayotte vetoed New Hampshire's HB 1442 on July 15, 2026. Her veto statement said: "This bill is nearly identical to the Senate bill I vetoed last month, as well as two other bills I have vetoed. These bills are overly broad, poorly drafted, and create the potential for substantial litigation."
    - citation: HB 1442 veto statement, in the Governor's press release "Governor Signs 20 Bills into Law," July 15, 2026
    - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-20-bills-law
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
11. On August 19, 2026, the New Hampshire House voted 165-149 on overriding the HB 1442 veto, short of the two-thirds needed, so the veto was sustained and HB 1442 did not become law.
    - citation: HB 1442 (2026) docket, August 19, 2026 (HJ 16 P. 36)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1937&inflect=2
    - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-12
    - review_by: 2027-08-15

## climate-phone-free-school-day: Bell-to-Bell School Phone Law (HB 2, 2025) and NH DOE's 2026 Educator Survey

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How NH's bell-to-bell school cell phone ban (RSA 189:1-a, V) was enacted in the 2025 budget, and NH DOE's 2026 survey of teachers on how it's working.
