1. New Hampshire's school cell phone policy law, RSA 189:1-a, V, was added by section 455 of HB 2 (2025), the bill that carries out the state budget for 2025-26 and 2026-27. Governor Ayotte signed HB 2 on June 27, 2025 as 2025 chapter 141, and the phone section took effect July 1, 2025.
   - citation: HB 2 (2025), chapter 141, sections 141:455 and 141:461
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%202%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-29
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from Wayback copies (raw/state-issues/climate-phone-free-school-day/hb2-2025-chaptered-lba-wayback-20261029.pdf, "Approved: June 27, 2025"; bill status hb2-2025-billinfo-wayback-20260430.html, "Signed by Governor Ayotte 06/27/2025; Chapter 141"). The RSA 189:1-a source note reads "2025, 141:455, eff. July 1, 2025" (Wayback 2026-10-31). The existing topic parent-communication-rights (fact 5) states what the policy must ban and the exceptions.
2. New Hampshire's RSA 189:1-a, V requires each school board and charter school board of trustees to develop its phone policy "in collaboration with school parents and teachers" and to review it every year.
   - citation: RSA 189:1-a, V
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-1-a.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-31
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
3. In July 2025, Governor Ayotte vetoed a separate phone bill, HB 781 (2025), "requiring school districts to adopt policies establishing a cell phone-free education." Her veto statement said a bell-to-bell ban "with stronger language was passed in House Bill 2."
   - citation: HB 781 (2025) veto statement, in the Governor's press release "Governor Ayotte Signs 16 Bills into Law," July 11, 2025
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-16-bills-law
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. NH DOE surveyed New Hampshire educators in spring 2026 about the phone policies, with help from the Regional Educational Laboratory (REL) Northeast & Islands. After removing elementary-only respondents, the results cover 1,399 secondary teachers and 158 secondary administrators (grades 6-12). NH DOE says the results "are not representative of all educators in New Hampshire": the survey went out through superintendents, so response rates could not be calculated.
   - citation: NH Department of Education, "Classrooms without Phones: Educators' Perspectives Nearly One Year After New Hampshire's Statewide 'Bell-to-bell' Cell Phone Ban," pp. 1, 11-12
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: There were 1,487 teacher responses and 198 administrator responses before 88 teachers and 40 administrators who work only in grades K-5 were excluded (p. 11). The memo was released with the Governor's press release of July 28, 2026 (https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/bell-bell-cell-phone-policy-delivers-powerful-results-first-year). The 1,399 figure is teachers only, not teachers and administrators combined.
5. In NH DOE's spring 2026 survey, 79% of teachers and 87% of administrators said their school's phone policy was very or somewhat effective at reducing distractions.
   - citation: NH DOE, "Classrooms without Phones," p. 2
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The chart on p. 2 shows teachers 42% very and 37% somewhat effective, and administrators 61% and 27% (88% when added; the memo's text says 87%, a rounding difference). The Governor's July 28, 2026 press release rounded these to "nearly 80 percent" and "about 88 percent."
6. In NH DOE's spring 2026 survey, 75% of teachers said they rarely or never saw phones used in class for non-instructional purposes, and 55% said phones did not interfere with learning at all. In a fall 2025 NH DOE survey about the year before the ban, 68% of teachers said they saw such use at least sometimes, and 62% said phones interfered with learning at least somewhat. NH DOE cautions that the two surveys had different respondents, so they are context, not a direct before-and-after comparison.
   - citation: NH DOE, "Classrooms without Phones," pp. 1-3
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The fall 2025 survey had 459 teachers and 77 administrators after exclusions (p. 1).
7. In NH DOE's spring 2026 survey, about three-quarters of teachers and administrators agreed that students seem more focused in class, but fewer reported other gains: 30% of teachers and 42% of administrators said there were fewer discipline problems, and 38% of teachers and 33% of administrators said academic outcomes had improved.
   - citation: NH DOE, "Classrooms without Phones," pp. 4-5
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
8. NH DOE notes that New Hampshire's law left "the specifics of how and where phones could be stored" up to each district, and educators in its spring 2026 survey described different storage rules: most commonly, students keep their phones but out of sight (63% of teachers, 56% of administrators), followed by lockers only (24%, 29%) and a bag or container at the start of the day (24%, 25%). Locked pouches such as Yondr were reported by 5% of teachers and 6% of administrators.
   - citation: NH DOE, "Classrooms without Phones," p. 6
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Educators could choose more than one answer, so the shares do not add to 100%.
9. In NH DOE's spring 2026 survey, 68% of teachers and 84% of administrators said they were satisfied with their school board's phone policy. More teachers wanted a stricter policy (46%) than a looser one (9%); among administrators, 31% wanted it stricter and 7% looser.
   - citation: NH DOE, "Classrooms without Phones," p. 9
   - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Stricter = "much more" plus "a little more restrictive" (teachers 22% + 24%; administrators 10% + 21%); looser = "a little less" plus "much less" (teachers 7% + 2%; administrators 7% + 0%), as stated in the memo's text and chart.
10. NH DOE's 2026 phone survey did not ask students, in part because New Hampshire law (RSA 186:11, IX-d) requires prior notice and parents' written consent for any non-academic survey of students.
    - citation: NH DOE, "Classrooms without Phones," p. 12 (Limitations)
    - source: https://www.governor.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt971/files/media/media_document/nhed-cell-phone-memo.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11

## students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026: School Vaccine Requirements for 2026-27 and the 2025-2026 Vaccine Bills

- kind: rule
- question_type: rules
- description: Which vaccines NH requires for school in 2026-27 (including hepatitis B, chickenpox and Hib), bills to drop requirements, and the 2026 exemption notice law (HB 1584).

1. HB 2 (2025), the budget trailer bill (2025 chapter 141), rewrote New Hampshire's childhood immunization law, RSA 141-C:20-a, I, effective July 1, 2025, so that it names all ten required diseases: diphtheria, mumps, pertussis, polio, rubella, measles (rubeola), tetanus, varicella (chickenpox), hepatitis B and Haemophilus influenzae type B (Hib). Before, the law said the required diseases "shall include, but not be limited to" seven named diseases, and told the DHHS commissioner to adopt rules for others.
   - citation: HB 2 (2025), chapter 141, section 141:456; RSA 141-C:20-a, I
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%202%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-29
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb2-2025-chaptered-copied.pdf, identical to the LBA PDF; section 141:456 is on chaptered-text p. 146). The struck-out words in 141:456 were "certain diseases. These diseases shall include, but not be limited to," and "The commissioner shall adopt rules under RSA 541-A relative to other diseases which require immunization." The current RSA 141-C:20-a (Wayback snapshot 2026-08-11) shows the ten diseases and the source note "2025, 141:456, eff. July 1, 2025."
2. HB 2 (2025) also repealed RSA 141-C:6, XIII, the DHHS commissioner's authority to require immunization for other childhood diseases by rule. Adding or removing a required school vaccine in New Hampshire now takes a change in state law.
   - citation: HB 2 (2025), chapter 141, section 141:457
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/lba/budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/House_Finance/HB%202%20-%20Chaptered%20Final%20Version%20-%207-1-25.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-29
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The second sentence is derived: with the list fixed in RSA 141-C:20-a, I and the rulemaking paragraph repealed, the required diseases are set only by statute. DHHS rules still set doses, ages and intervals (RSA 141-C:20-a, II(b)).
3. For 2026-27, New Hampshire DHHS requires these vaccines for kindergarten through grade 12: DTaP (4 or 5 doses for children 6 and under; at least 3 for age 7 and older), one Tdap dose before entering grade 7, polio (3 or 4 doses), hepatitis B (3 doses), MMR (2 doses) and varicella (2 doses).
   - citation: NH DHHS, "New Hampshire School Immunization Requirements 2026-2027" (dated April 13, 2026), p. 1
   - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt476/files/documents2/2026-2027-nh-school-imm-reqs-final.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. For preschool children ages 3-5 in 2026-27, New Hampshire DHHS requires DTaP (4 doses), polio (3 doses), MMR (1 dose), Hib (4 doses, or fewer on a catch-up schedule), hepatitis B (3 doses) and varicella (1 dose, or lab proof of immunity). Hib is not required for children over 5.
   - citation: NH DHHS, "New Hampshire School Immunization Requirements 2026-2027," p. 4 (Pre-school Students 3-5 Years Old)
   - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt476/files/documents2/2026-2027-nh-school-imm-reqs-final.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. In New Hampshire, a child must show proof of all required vaccines, proof of immunity, or a valid exemption to enroll in any school. A child may be enrolled "conditionally" with proof of at least one dose of each required vaccine and an appointment date for the next dose. A lab test showing immunity is accepted for hepatitis B, measles, mumps, rubella and varicella.
   - citation: NH DHHS, "New Hampshire School Immunization Requirements 2026-2027," p. 1; RSA 141-C:20-a, II
   - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt476/files/documents2/2026-2027-nh-school-imm-reqs-final.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
6. New Hampshire's HB 357 (2025) would have limited the required childhood vaccines to seven diseases and let the DHHS rules requiring varicella, hepatitis B and Hib expire on June 30, 2026. It passed the House 195-174 on March 27, 2025, but the Senate killed it (inexpedient to legislate, voice vote) on May 1, 2025, after its committee recommended that 5-0.
   - citation: HB 357 (2025) bill status and docket (HJ 11 P. 31; SC 19; SJ 11); HB 357 as introduced, section 1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=444&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-23
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from Wayback copies (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb357-2025-billinfo-wayback-20260923.html; bill text hb357-2025-text-wayback-20260819.html, from https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2025&id=444&txtFormat=html).
7. New Hampshire's HB 1811 (2026) would have repealed the state's childhood immunization requirements, including the school and child care entry rule, the exemption section and school immunization reporting, and barred the state and local governments from requiring vaccinations. The House killed it on February 19, 2026: an amendment failed 166-171, a motion to pass it failed 155-192, and the House then voted it inexpedient to legislate by voice vote.
   - citation: HB 1811 (2026) bill status and docket (HJ 5 P. 77, 80)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1561&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-06-05
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb1811-2026-billinfo-wayback-20260605.html). Bill content from the introduced text (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb1811-2026-text-wayback-20260205.html, from https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1561&txtFormat=html): it would have repealed RSA 141-C:20-b to 20-e and said DHHS "and any political subdivision ... shall not require vaccinations under any circumstance." Its fiscal note estimated $6.8 million less in vaccine purchases in FY2027, paid through the insurer assessment of the NH Vaccine Association.
8. New Hampshire's HB 1719 (2026), "removing Hepatitis B from the list of diseases for which immunization is required under state law," passed the House 186-168 on February 12, 2026. The Senate sent it to interim study by voice vote on May 7, 2026, so it did not pass in 2026.
   - citation: HB 1719 (2026) bill status and docket (HJ 4 P. 36; SC 17; SJ 11)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1573&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-06-05
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb1719-2026-billinfo-wayback-20260605.html). The Senate committee vote for interim study was 3-1.
9. New Hampshire's HB 358 (2025) would have required no more for a religious exemption than a parent's signed written statement that the child has not been immunized because of religious belief, and removed the law's reference to a form. Governor Ayotte vetoed it on July 15, 2025, writing that "the State already has an established process by which parents can claim a religious exemption, and I see no reason to change it." On December 17, 2025, the House vote to override was 176-175, short of two-thirds, so the veto was sustained.
   - citation: HB 358 (2025) bill status and docket (HJ 19); HB 358 veto statement, Governor's press release of July 15, 2025
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=448
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-23
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Veto statement at https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-ayotte-signs-101-bills-law. Bill content from the final text, proposed RSA 141-C:20-c, II (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb358-2025-text-wayback-20260819.html, from https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2025&id=448&txtFormat=html). Status read from the Wayback copy (hb358-2025-billinfo-wayback-20260923.html).
10. Governor Ayotte signed New Hampshire's HB 1584 (2026) on July 15, 2026 as 2026 chapter 328. As its title says, it directs DHHS "to provide notice of medical and religious exemptions from immunization requirements"; it also covers notices about prescription drug pricing. Section 1 took effect October 13, 2026, and the rest of the law takes effect January 1, 2027.
    - citation: HB 1584 (2026) bill status and docket ("Signed by Governor Ayotte 07/15/2026; Chapter 328")
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2450&inflect=2
    - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-06
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: Read from the Wayback copy (raw/state-issues/students-school-vaccine-requirements-2026/hb1584-2026-billinfo-wayback-20261006.html). Title: "directing the department of health and human services to provide notice of medical and religious exemptions from immunization requirements and relative to notice of drug pricing options." The Governor's July 15, 2026 release (https://www.governor.nh.gov/news/governor-signs-20-bills-law) lists it among bills signed.
11. Under RSA 141-C:20-c, as DHHS describes it, a New Hampshire child may be exempt from school and child care vaccines for medical reasons, if a licensed physician or authorized health care provider certifies a vaccine may be detrimental to the child's health, or for religious reasons, if a parent signs the DHHS religious exemption form. Since July 19, 2022 the religious form no longer needs a notary. During an outbreak, exempt children may be excluded from school if the Division of Public Health recommends it.
    - citation: NH DHHS, "Immunization Exemptions for Children" (RSA 141-C:20-c; HB 1035 (2022))
    - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/programs-services/disease-prevention/nh-immunization-program/immunization-exemptions-children
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
12. Every public and private school in New Hampshire must submit an Annual School Immunization Report to DHHS by November 15.
    - citation: RSA 141-C:20-e, as stated in NH DHHS, "New Hampshire School Immunization Requirements 2026-2027," p. 3
    - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt476/files/documents2/2026-2027-nh-school-imm-reqs-final.pdf
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15

## gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite: The 2024 Rewrite of the School Approval Rules (Ed 306)

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: The State Board's 2024 rewrite of NH's minimum school approval standards (Ed 306): what changed, the 2027 reading and math deadlines, and the link to adequacy after HB 1815.

1. NH DOE engaged the National Center for Competency Based Learning to lead a team drafting new minimum standards for public school approval (Ed 306). On August 14, 2024, the State Board of Education voted unanimously to approve the final proposal for the first half of the rules, Ed 306.01-306.26, and sent it to the legislature's Joint Legislative Committee on Administrative Rules for review.
   - citation: NH DOE press release, "State Board of Education approves final proposal to amend rules governing some of the Minimum Standards for Public Schools Approval," August 14, 2024
   - source: https://education.nh.gov/news-and-media/state-board-education-approves-final-proposal-amend-rules-governing-some-minimum-standards-public
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
2. When the New Hampshire State Board approved the Ed 306 final proposal in August 2024, its chair, Drew Cline, said the update "consolidates and streamlines the minimum standards in a manner that educators can easily understand," and that the board was "confident that these improvements further align the rules with the goal of competency-based learning."
   - citation: NH DOE press release, August 14, 2024
   - source: https://education.nh.gov/news-and-media/state-board-education-approves-final-proposal-amend-rules-governing-some-minimum-standards-public
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. The New Hampshire State Board of Education adopted both parts of the rewritten Ed 306, Ed 306.01-306.26 and Ed 306.27-306.39, on December 12, 2024. They took effect December 13, 2024 as Documents #14150 and #14151.
   - citation: NH DOE, "Minimum Standards" page (adopted rule text, "Voted on and adopted by the State Board December 12, 2024"); N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306, Revision Note #4
   - source: https://education.nh.gov/who-we-are/deputy-commissioner/office-governance/administrative-rules/minimum-standards
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Document numbers and effective date from Revision Note #4 on https://gc.nh.gov/rules/state_agencies/ed300.html (copy downloaded 2026-11-08, raw/state-issues/gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite/rules-ed300-downloaded-2026-11-08.html; gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11).
4. Before the 2024 rewrite, most of New Hampshire's Ed 306 rules dated from 2014 (Document #10556). They reached their expiration date on March 27, 2024, but stayed in force under RSA 541-A:14-a until the new rules replaced them.
   - citation: N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306, Revision Note #4
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rules/state_agencies/ed300.html
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from the copy downloaded 2026-11-08 (raw/state-issues/gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite/rules-ed300-downloaded-2026-11-08.html); gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11. The note refers to "Document #14050" in this sentence, apparently a typo for #14150.
5. The 2024 rewrite of New Hampshire's Ed 306 repealed five sections of the school approval rules: Custodial and Maintenance Services, Basic Instructional Standards, School Calendar, Statistical Reports and Accountability, and Delay in Full Compliance. The remaining rules were renumbered, so former Ed 306.01-306.29 became Ed 306.01-306.25.
   - citation: N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306, Revision Note #4 (Document #14150)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rules/state_agencies/ed300.html
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The school calendar requirement now sits in the school year rule: "Each school shall maintain a school calendar which provides for 180 days of instruction or the ..." (Ed 306.15(a), adopted text p. 12).
6. The 2024 rewrite of New Hampshire's Ed 306 added a new rule, Ed 306.26, under which the State Board sets state academic standards for the subjects listed in RSA 193-E:2-a, I, after at least one public hearing announced at least 20 days ahead. It also renamed the "Class Size" rule "Student-Educator Ratios," renamed "Distance Education" "Remote Learning," and split the high school curriculum rule into a curriculum rule (Ed 306.22) and a graduation requirements rule (Ed 306.23).
   - citation: N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306, Revision Note #4 (Document #14150); Ed 306.26(a)-(d)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rules/state_agencies/ed300.html
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Ed 306.26 wording checked in NH DOE's adopted text (raw/state-issues/gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite/2024-41_adopted-text_0.pdf, pp. 33-34).
7. In the program-area half of New Hampshire's Ed 306, the 2024 rewrite repealed the separate rules for a Career and Technical Education Program, a Career Education Program, a Family and Consumer Science Education Program, and a Holocaust and Genocide Education Program, and renumbered former Ed 306.31-306.49 as Ed 306.27-306.39. Holocaust and genocide instruction is now required within the Social Studies Program rule, Ed 306.37(c).
   - citation: N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306, Revision Note #4 (Document #14151); Ed 306.37(c)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rules/state_agencies/ed300.html
   - as_of: 2026-11-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Ed 306.37(c) checked in NH DOE's adopted text (raw/state-issues/gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite/2024-42-adopted-rule.pdf). CTE programs remain covered by Ed 306.16 ("CTE Programs") in the first half.
8. New Hampshire's rewritten Ed 306 defines "learning opportunities" as educational experiences "including but not limited to in-person, online, blended, and self-guided classes, ELOs, work-based learning, and alternative learning plans that lead to achievement of competencies," and says the term includes "course." It defines "competency-based" education by tenets such as competencies being explicitly stated and measured and students advancing "to new learning opportunities upon proficiency."
   - citation: N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306.02(f) and (t) (adopted December 12, 2024)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/2024-41_adopted-text_0.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
9. New Hampshire's rewritten Ed 306 requires schools, no later than July 1, 2027, to provide "measurable, evidence-based literacy instruction" for all students through grade 5, covering phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary and comprehension, and the grammar and mechanics of writing, and to teach "mathematics reasoning and mathematics calculation including fluency" for all students through grade 5.
   - citation: N.H. Admin. Rules, Ed 306.21(c)(1)b.2 and f.2 (adopted December 12, 2024)
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/sonh/2024-41_adopted-text_0.pdf
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
10. New Hampshire's adequacy law, RSA 193-E:2-a, I(a), as rewritten by HB 1815 (2026 chapter 19, effective May 26, 2026), now begins: "An adequate education shall be defined and identified as the school approval standards in the following learning areas," followed by 11 subjects. Before HB 1815, the paragraph instead defined "the specific criteria and substantive educational program that deliver the opportunity for an adequate education" as the school approval standards.
    - citation: RSA 193-E:2-a, I(a) and source note (2026, 19:2, eff. May 26, 2026)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-E/193-E-2-a.htm
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: RSA copy downloaded 2026-11-11 by the money-hb-1815-adequacy-definition group (copied to raw/state-issues/gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite/rsa-193-E-2-a-copied-from-money-hb-1815.htm). HB 1815 = chapter 19, signed March 27, 2026, per its bill status page (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1534&inflect=2; copy hb1815-billinfo-copied-from-money-hb-1815.html). The struck words are bracketed in the chaptered text, section 19:2 (hb1815-chaptered-copied-from-money-hb-1815.pdf). HB 1815 is covered in depth by money-hb-1815-adequacy-definition.
11. New Hampshire's adequacy law, RSA 193-E:2-a, IV(a), still says that changes the State Board makes to the school approval standards by rule "shall not be included within the standards that constitute the opportunity for the delivery of an adequate education without prior adoption by the general court." So rule changes to the school approval standards made after that paragraph took effect count toward the legal definition of an adequate education only if the legislature adopts them.
    - citation: RSA 193-E:2-a, IV(a)
    - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-E/193-E-2-a.htm
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-08-15
    - note: Copy downloaded 2026-11-11 by the money-hb-1815-adequacy-definition group (raw/state-issues/gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite/rsa-193-E-2-a-copied-from-money-hb-1815.htm). The second sentence restates the quoted text. The paragraph applies to rule changes made after the section's effective date; whether the legislature has adopted the 2024 Ed 306 changes was not checked (see Not retrieved).
12. As of November 11, 2026, NH DOE's list of initial rule proposals approved by the State Board shows proposals for Ed 505.01, Ed 318.21-318.22 and several Ed 507 teacher credential rules, but none for Ed 306.
    - citation: NH DOE, "Initial Proposals" page
    - source: https://education.nh.gov/who-we-are/deputy-commissioner/office-of-governance/administrative-rules/initial-proposals
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-05-11

---

# Students and Safety

**Sources and notes for this section**

- 7 topics, 66 facts, checked November 11, 2026: 2026 state test results (12), enrollment decline (9), HB 1727 Summer EBT (11), SB 575 bullying study committee (9), SB 507 staff-violence study committee (7), HB 1503 school emergency maps (6), firearms-and-schools bills and current law (12). No chronic absenteeism topic: no newer primary data was found (see Not retrieved).
- Sources: NH DOE press releases (via Wayback, because education.nh.gov refused direct downloads), NH DOE assessment data files and iPlatform enrollment reports (the copies the site loads), NH DHHS Summer EBT pages (Wayback), USDA FNS Summer EBT pages, the General Court's bill status, docket and chaptered-text pages (saved before gc.nh.gov stopped answering mid-session; later pages via Wayback), RSA 193-D and 198:15-y (Wayback), and the U.S. Code (Cornell LII). One bill text (HB 1830) is from a third-party copy and is flagged for recheck.
- Review dates: enacted NH bills and RSAs 2027-08-15; open items (committee reports due November 1, 2026) 2027-05-11; bills that died, data, agency pages and federal law 2027-11-11.
- Raw copies: `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/`.

## students-2026-state-test-results: 2026 State Test Results (NH SAS and SAT): Statewide Proficiency and Trends

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH's 2026 state test results (NH SAS and grade 11 SAT): percent proficient in math, reading and writing, and science by grade, SAT scores and the trend since 2019.

1. On September 2, 2026, the NH Department of Education announced its 2026 state test (NH SAS) results: statewide math proficiency rose from 42 percent in 2025 to 44 percent in 2026, and reading and writing proficiency stayed at 55 percent.
   - citation: NH DOE press release, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," September 2, 2026
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: NH DOE's server refused direct downloads on 2026-11-11 (403); read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260922135152/https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results (raw: nhed-2026-assessment-results-release-wayback-20260922.html). "Proficient" means Level 3 or 4 (see measure-nh-sas-achievement-levels). The statewide 55% and 44% match NH DOE's 2026 data file ("All grades," All students rows).
2. In NH DOE's 2026 results, the share of students proficient in math was 54% in grade 3, 52% in grade 4, 43% in grade 5, 43% in grade 6, 44% in grade 7, 40% in grade 8 and 30% in grade 11.
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," Math Assessment Data (percent proficient) table
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The table is an image (raw: nhed-2026-release-math-table.png). The figures match the "Above prof% (lvl 3&4)" column of NH DOE's 2026 data file, State Level, All students (raw: state-level-all-students-2019-2026.csv).
3. In NH DOE's 2026 results, the share of students proficient in reading and writing (English language arts) was 51% in grade 3, 52% in grade 4, 58% in grade 5, 52% in grade 6, 57% in grade 7, 51% in grade 8 and 62% in grade 11.
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," ELA Assessment Data (percent proficient) table
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw: nhed-2026-release-ela-table.png; matches NH DOE's 2026 data file.
4. New Hampshire's overall math proficiency on the state tests was 48% in 2019, 38% in 2021, 40% in 2022, 42% in 2023, 41% in 2024, 42% in 2025 and 44% in 2026. No state tests were given in 2020 because of the pandemic.
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," Math Assessment Data table, "Overall Student Proficiency" row and footnote
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The overall row covers the grades in the table (3 through 8 and 11).
5. New Hampshire's overall reading and writing proficiency on the state tests was 56% in 2019, 52% in 2021, 51% in 2022, 52% in 2023, 53% in 2024, 55% in 2025 and 55% in 2026.
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," ELA Assessment Data table, "Overall Student Proficiency" row
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
6. In grade 11, New Hampshire's math proficiency was 42% in 2019 and 2021, 37% in 2022, 35% in 2023, 30% in 2024, 31% in 2025 and 30% in 2026. Grade 11 reading and writing proficiency was 66% in 2025 and 62% in 2026.
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," Math and ELA Assessment Data tables, grade 11 rows
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Grade 11 results come from the SAT (see measure-sat-grade-11-state-test). Grade 11 participation was lower than in other grades (fact 11).
7. NH DOE's 2026 release said reading and writing proficiency rose from 49% to 52% among students who moved from third to fourth grade, and from 53% to 58% among students who moved from fourth to fifth grade. It also said grades 5 and 7 had 2026 reading and writing rates above their 2019 levels (58% vs. 57% in grade 5, and 57% vs. 56% in grade 7).
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results," text and ELA Assessment Data table
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The "moved from" comparisons are grade-to-grade comparisons across years (grade 3 in 2025 vs. grade 4 in 2026), as the release presents them; the 2019 figures are from the table.
8. On New Hampshire's 2026 state science test, 38% of fifth graders, 37% of eighth graders and 40% of eleventh graders were proficient; NH DOE said science results were level, with grade 11 "up slightly from 2025."
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
9. In NH DOE's data files, statewide science proficiency (grades 5, 8 and 11 together) was 40% in 2019, 37% in 2021, 2022 and 2023, 36% in 2024, and 38% in both 2025 and 2026. Grade 11 science proficiency was 39% in 2025 and 40% in 2026.
   - citation: NH DOE public disaggregated assessment data files, 2019 and 2021–2026, State Level, Science, All students, "Above prof% (lvl 3&4)"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/2026/publicdisaggregateddata2026_csvformat.csv
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: NH DOE's server refused direct downloads on 2026-11-11 (403). Figures read from the copies the site loads (education-backend assets/assessments/assessments-2019.csv through assessments-2026.csv; the 2026 file's Report Date is 8/21/2026); the state rows are saved in raw: state-level-all-students-2019-2026.csv.
10. New Hampshire's average SAT scores in 2026 were 509 in reading (516 in 2025) and 479 in math (481 in 2025).
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The 2026 data file's grade 11 "Avg Score" is 509 (reading) and 479 (math), matching the release.
11. In 2026, 95% of New Hampshire students in the tested grades took the state math and reading tests, but participation in grade 11 was 87% in math and in reading, and 80% in science.
   - citation: NH DOE 2026 public disaggregated assessment data file, State Level, All students, "Participate%" column
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/sites/g/files/ehbemt326/files/inline-documents/2026/publicdisaggregateddata2026_csvformat.csv
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Read from the site's copy (assessments-2026.csv) because NH DOE blocked the download; raw: state-level-all-students-2019-2026.csv. Grades 3–8 ranged from 95% to 98%.
12. NH DOE's 2026 release named four school districts with gains in both math and reading and writing from 2024 to 2026: Wakefield ("Nearly 14 percent improvement in reading and writing and 14 percent improvement in math"), Weare (about 10 percent in reading and writing and 14 percent in math), Epsom (about 10 percent and 13 percent) and Keene (about 7 percent and 11 percent).
   - citation: NH DOE, "NHED Announces 2026 Student Assessment Results"
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/nhed-announces-2026-student-assessment-results
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-09-22
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The release says "percent improvement"; it does not say whether these are percentage points. Kept as the release words them.

## students-enrollment-decline: Declining Public School Enrollment in New Hampshire

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How many students attend NH public and charter schools (fall 2025 and 2026), how far enrollment has fallen since 2005-06, and the drop in births behind it.

1. NH DOE's official fall 2025 count, released November 14, 2025, was 160,323 students in New Hampshire public and public charter schools, compared with 162,660 at the start of the 2024-25 school year.
   - citation: NH DOE press release, "New Hampshire adapts to changing student population," November 14, 2025
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/new-hampshire-adapts-changing-student-population
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-12-01
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: NH DOE's server refused direct downloads on 2026-11-11; read from http://web.archive.org/web/20251201034603/https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/new-hampshire-adapts-changing-student-population (raw: nhed-adapts-changing-student-population-wayback-20251201.html). NH DOE's iPlatform ten-year report run November 5, 2026 gives 160,322 for 2025-26 (fact 4), one fewer than the release.
2. NH DOE's November 2025 release said more than 205,000 students were enrolled in New Hampshire public and public charter schools 20 years earlier, in the 2005-06 school year.
   - citation: NH DOE press release, "New Hampshire adapts to changing student population," November 14, 2025
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/new-hampshire-adapts-changing-student-population
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-12-01
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. NH DOE links the enrollment decline to a smaller school-age population driven by declining birth rates; its November 2025 release said births in New Hampshire fell from about 14,000 a year in the early 2000s to roughly 11,000 to 12,000 a year.
   - citation: NH DOE press release, "New Hampshire adapts to changing student population," November 14, 2025
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/new-hampshire-adapts-changing-student-population
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-12-01
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
4. NH DOE's ten-year enrollment reports count 181,339 students in New Hampshire public schools (district schools, public academies, public charter schools and interstate schools, preschool through postgraduate) in fall 2015 (2015-16), 176,168 in fall 2019 and 160,322 in fall 2025. That is a drop of 21,017 students, about 11.6%, from 2015-16 to 2025-26 (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "State Totals Ten Years Public and Private Fall Enrollments" (Report ID 17), 2007-2008 through 2016-2017 and 2017-2018 through 2026-2027, "Total Public Schools" rows
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: derived: 181,339 − 160,322 = 21,017; 21,017 ÷ 181,339 = 11.6%. Raw: iplatform-state-totals-ten-years-2007-08-to-2016-17.xlsx and iplatform-state-totals-ten-years-2017-18-to-2026-27.xlsx (copies of the reports the site loads, run November 5, 2026; the newer one has Report Date 11/5/2026, "Type of Data: Final"). 2025-26 parts: district schools 149,885, academies 4,036, charters 6,336, interstate 65.
5. NH DOE's ten-year enrollment report, dated November 5, 2026, counts 157,617 students in New Hampshire public schools in fall 2026 (2026-27), 2,705 fewer than in fall 2025 (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "State Totals Ten Years Public and Private Fall Enrollments, 2017-2018 Through 2026-2027" (Report ID 17), "Total Public Schools" row
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: derived: 160,322 − 157,617 = 2,705 (about 1.7%). The report labels its data "Final"; NH DOE's annual enrollment release for fall 2026 had not been found by 2026-11-11.
6. Enrollment in New Hampshire's public charter schools rose from 3,011 in fall 2015 to 6,336 in fall 2025 and 7,071 in fall 2026, while enrollment in public district schools fell from 174,015 to 149,885 and then 146,428.
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "State Totals Ten Years Public and Private Fall Enrollments" (Report ID 17), "Public Charter Schools" and "Public District Schools" rows
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
7. New Hampshire's nonpublic (private) schools reported 16,562 students in fall 2015 and 16,726 in fall 2025. NH DOE's enrollment report notes that Education Freedom Account (EFA) data is not included in its counts.
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "State Totals Ten Years Public and Private Fall Enrollments" (Report ID 17), "Total Nonpublic Schools" rows and footnotes
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The report says nonpublic enrollment is collected through the ESS A12C General Fall Survey. The fall 2026 nonpublic column was blank in the November 5, 2026 report.
8. Kindergarten enrollment in New Hampshire public schools was 11,415 in fall 2017, 10,727 in fall 2025 and 10,584 in fall 2026. In fall 2025, public schools enrolled 12,403 twelfth graders.
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "State Totals Ten Years Public and Private Fall Enrollments, 2017-2018 Through 2026-2027" (Report ID 17), "Total Public Schools" rows for kindergarten and grade 12
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
9. In NH DOE's November 2025 enrollment release, Commissioner Caitlin D. Davis said, "These shifts may require school districts to make thoughtful adjustments as they work to prepare and finalize their budgets in March for the next school year."
   - citation: NH DOE press release, "New Hampshire adapts to changing student population," November 14, 2025
   - source: https://www.education.nh.gov/news-and-media/new-hampshire-adapts-changing-student-population
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-12-01
   - review_by: 2027-11-11

## students-hb-1727-summer-ebt: HB 1727 (2026): Automatic Summer EBT Enrollment From School Meal Data

- kind: rule
- question_type: rules
- description: HB 1727 (2026): automatic Summer EBT (SUN Bucks) enrollment for students eligible for free or reduced-price meals unless parents opt out, and NH's Summer EBT history.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1727 (2026), on student data sharing for the Summer EBT program, was signed by Governor Ayotte on July 15, 2026 and became 2026 chapter 331. Most of it took effect July 15, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1727 (2026), chapter 331
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2178&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Docket: "Signed by Governor Ayotte 07/15/2026; Chapter 331; eff. I. Secs 2&3 eff as prov sec 4 II Rem eff 07/15/2026." Raw: hb1727-2026-billinfo.html and hb1727-2026-docket.html.
2. Under HB 1727 (2026), the NH Department of Education must give the Department of Health and Human Services the name, date of birth and household address of each student eligible for free or reduced-price meals, so the student can be automatically enrolled in Summer EBT. No other personally identifiable information may be sent.
   - citation: RSA 189:11-f, I, as inserted by 2026 ch. 331:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2178&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Chaptered final version (raw: hb1727-2026-chaptered-text.html). The section cites 42 U.S.C. 1762(c)(1)(A). RSA 189:11-f may not yet appear in the online RSA.
3. Under HB 1727 (2026), school districts and SAUs must give the parent or guardian of each student a separate Summer EBT notice and opt-out form at the same time as the free or reduced-price meal decision. On paper, the opt-out form must be a separate insert; online, it must be a prominent, separate step.
   - citation: RSA 189:11-f, II, as inserted by 2026 ch. 331:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2178&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. Under HB 1727 (2026), a student found eligible for free or reduced-price meals is automatically enrolled in Summer EBT if the parent or guardian has not returned the opt-out form within 15 days of receiving the meal decision.
   - citation: RSA 189:11-f, III, as inserted by 2026 ch. 331:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2178&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. Under HB 1727 (2026), the student data sent for Summer EBT is confidential, may be used only to run and distribute Summer EBT benefits, and must be deleted once that summer's benefits are distributed unless federal law requires otherwise. The transfers are governed by a memorandum of understanding between NH DOE and DHHS.
   - citation: RSA 189:11-f, IV-V, as inserted by 2026 ch. 331:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2178&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
6. HB 1727 (2026) also requires DHHS to set up a secure electronic way to receive the student data and to adopt rules on the standard wording of the parent notice and opt-out form, secure transmission, data retention and deletion, and the minimum terms of the agreement with NH DOE. The notice must tell parents that completing the school meal application may result in automatic Summer EBT enrollment unless they opt out.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 331:2-6 (RSA 161:15 or 161:16, depending on SB 615 of 2026)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2178&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The act inserts the same DHHS section as RSA 161:15 if SB 615 (2026) did not become law, or as RSA 161:16 if it did (ch. 331:4-5). Whether SB 615 became law was not checked; see Not retrieved.
7. HB 1727 (2026) passed the House with an amendment by voice vote on March 11, 2026 (committee vote 14-1) and the Senate with a different amendment by voice vote on May 7, 2026 (committee vote 4-0). A committee of conference settled the differences, and both chambers adopted its report by voice vote on June 4, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1727 (2026) docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2178&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: House amendment 2026-0847h; Senate amendment 2026-1748s; conference report 2026-2113c; enrolled bill amendment 2026-2195e. The bill was introduced as "relative to the inter-agency data sharing for the purpose of distributing summer electronic benefit transfer (EBT) benefits" (bill status page, Wayback snapshot 2025-12-29, raw: hb1727-2026-billinfo-wayback-20251229.html). Sponsor: Rep. Bordes.
8. New Hampshire first ran the federal Summer EBT program in 2024. In July 2025 DHHS said New Hampshire had received USDA funding to take part for "a second consecutive year," giving $120 per eligible child, and that the Legislature approved funding to administer the program in HB 2 (2025), signed by Governor Ayotte in June 2025.
   - citation: NH DHHS press release, "NH DHHS Announces Availability of Summer EBT," July 21, 2025
   - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/news-and-media/nh-dhhs-announces-availability-summer-ebt
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-09-01
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: "First ran in 2024" is derived from "second consecutive year" in 2025. DHHS's server refused direct downloads on 2026-11-11; read from http://web.archive.org/web/20250901170949/https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/news-and-media/nh-dhhs-announces-availability-summer-ebt (raw: dhhs-announces-availability-summer-ebt-wayback-20250901.html). The release says Summer EBT benefits are fully funded by USDA's Food and Nutrition Service.
9. New Hampshire's first Summer EBT benefits, for children eligible during the 2023-24 school year, were still being issued in late 2024: DHHS's Summer EBT page in December 2024 said families not automatically enrolled could apply between November 16 and December 15, 2024.
   - citation: NH DHHS, "Summer EBT" program page (snapshot of December 9, 2024)
   - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/programs-services/food-meals-assistance/summer-ebt
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2024-12-09
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw: dhhs-summer-ebt-page-wayback-20241209.html. The page said households with SNAP, TANF or Medicaid and enrolled in school meals would get benefits automatically, and households income-eligible at or below 185% of the federal poverty guidelines could apply.
10. Before HB 1727 took effect, DHHS's 2026 Summer EBT page said children who got free or reduced-price school meals in 2025-26, but whose households did not receive SNAP, TANF or Medicaid, would automatically receive benefits only if the family gave DHHS its address through the NH EASY portal. The 2026 benefit was a one-time $120 per eligible child.
   - citation: NH DHHS, "Summer EBT" program page, "S-EBT Benefits for 2025-2026 School Year" (snapshot of July 14, 2026)
   - source: https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/programs-services/food-meals-assistance/summer-ebt
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-07-14
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw: dhhs-summer-ebt-page-wayback-20260714.html (captured the day before HB 1727 was signed).
11. USDA's list of 2026 Summer EBT participating agencies shows Summer EBT available in New Hampshire in 2026.
   - citation: USDA Food and Nutrition Service, "Summer EBT Participating Areas," 2026 Participating Agencies (page updated August 14, 2026), New Hampshire entry
   - source: https://www.fns.usda.gov/sebt/new-hampshire
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The New Hampshire entry reads "Good News! Summer EBT is Available in Your Location" and links to the DHHS Summer EBT page. Raw: fns-sebt-new-hampshire.html and fns-sebt-participating.html (map at https://www.fns.usda.gov/sebt/participating).

## students-sb-575-bullying-study-committee: SB 575 (2026): Committee to Study School Bullying

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: The school bullying study committee created by SB 575 (2026): members, duties, its November 2026 report date and the bill's other changes.

1. New Hampshire's SB 575 (2026) was signed by the governor on July 2, 2026, became 2026 chapter 269, and took effect July 2, 2026.
   - citation: SB 575 (2026), chapter 269
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1274&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Docket: "Signed by the Governor on 07/02/2026; Chapter 269; Effective 07/02/2026." Raw: sb575-2026-billinfo.html, sb575-2026-docket.html.
2. SB 575 (2026) created a legislative committee to study school bullying, made up of one state senator appointed by the senate president and three state representatives appointed by the speaker of the house. Members are paid mileage only.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 269:1-2
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1274&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Chaptered final version (raw: sb575-2026-chaptered-text.html). The members appointed were not found; see Not retrieved.
3. Under SB 575 (2026), the bullying study committee's duty is to "study school bullying and means to prevent it."
   - citation: 2026 ch. 269:3
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1274&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. Under SB 575 (2026), the bullying study committee elects its own chair, its first meeting is called by the senate member within 45 days of the law taking effect, and three members are a quorum.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 269:4
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1274&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. Under SB 575 (2026), the bullying study committee must report its findings and any recommended legislation to the senate president, the house speaker, the senate and house clerks, the governor and the state library on or before November 1, 2026.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 269:5
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1274&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Whether the report was filed was not confirmed; see Not retrieved.
6. SB 575 (2026) also changed New Hampshire's Right-to-Know law so that "educator" certification records (instead of "teacher" certification records) at the Department of Education are exempt from disclosure; the department must still make certification status available.
   - citation: RSA 91-A:5, V, as amended by 2026 ch. 269:6
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1274&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
7. SB 575 (2026) also provides that, in New Hampshire education law, "superintendent" includes personnel assigned by the superintendent or by the school board to perform superintendent services.
   - citation: RSA 194-C:4-a, as amended by 2026 ch. 269:7
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1274&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Before the change, only personnel assigned by the superintendent were included.
8. SB 575 (2026) passed the Senate by voice vote on March 5, 2026 (Education Committee vote 5-0). The House Education Policy and Administration Committee rewrote it with a new title (vote 18-0), the House passed that version by voice vote on May 14, 2026, and the Senate agreed to the House version by voice vote on May 21, 2026.
   - citation: SB 575 (2026) docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1274&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: House amendment 2026-1853h. The House also held hearings on non-germane amendments 2026-1708h and 2026-1715h on May 4, 2026.
9. SB 575 (2026) passed the Senate under the title "establishing a study committee to study the issue of school bullying." The House amendment gave it a new title that added the changes to educator certification records and superintendent services.
   - citation: SB 575 (2026), bill status page as of March 8, 2026 (title); 2026 ch. 269 (final title)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1274
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-03-08
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260308160856/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1274 (raw: sb575-2026-billinfo-wayback-20260308.html). The final title and sections are in the chaptered text (raw: sb575-2026-chaptered-text.html).

## students-sb-507-staff-violence-study: SB 507 (2026): Committee to Study Violence Against School Staff

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: The study committee on violence against school staff created by SB 507 (2026): duties, members, report date and how the bill began.

1. New Hampshire's SB 507 (2026) was signed by the governor on June 19, 2026, became 2026 chapter 191, and took effect June 19, 2026.
   - citation: SB 507 (2026), chapter 191
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1291&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Docket: "Signed by the Governor on 06/19/2026; Chapter 191; Effective 06/19/2026." Raw: sb507-2026-billinfo.html, sb507-2026-docket.html.
2. SB 507 (2026) created a legislative committee to study violence in schools directed at staff members and the obstacles to disciplining or expelling students in such cases. It has one state senator appointed by the senate president and three state representatives appointed by the speaker of the house, paid mileage only.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 191:1-2
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1291&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Chaptered final version (raw: sb507-2026-chaptered-text.html). The members appointed were not found; see Not retrieved.
3. Under SB 507 (2026), the study committee must consider state and federal laws on violence in schools directed at staff, mental health concerns affecting students, potential liabilities for school districts, and the emotional and psychological trauma of staff members who must keep interacting with their assailants after an incident.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 191:3
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1291&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
4. Under SB 507 (2026), the committee elects its own chair, its first meeting is called by the senate member within 45 days of the law taking effect, and three members are a quorum.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 191:4
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1291&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. Under SB 507 (2026), the committee on violence against school staff must report its findings and any recommended legislation to the senate president, the house speaker, the senate and house clerks, the governor and the state library on or before November 1, 2026.
   - citation: 2026 ch. 191:5
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1291&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: Whether the report was filed was not confirmed; see Not retrieved.
6. SB 507 (2026) was sent to the Senate Education Finance Committee, which recommended replacing its text with the study committee (amendment 2026-0378s, vote 6-0). The Senate adopted the amendment and passed the bill by voice vote on February 5, 2026; the House Education Policy and Administration Committee recommended passage 18-0, and the House passed it by voice vote on May 14, 2026.
   - citation: SB 507 (2026) docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1291&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
7. SB 507 (2026), sponsored by Senator Keith Murphy with Senators Victoria Sullivan, Avard, Innis and Pearl, was introduced under the title "relative to the financial obligation of a school district concerning students expelled for committing assault against a school staff member." The Senate's amendment replaced it with the study committee and a new title.
   - citation: SB 507 (2026), bill status page as of December 30, 2025 (title and sponsors)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1291
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-12-30
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from http://web.archive.org/web/20251230032710/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_Status/billinfo.aspx?id=1291 (raw: sb507-2026-billinfo-wayback-20251230.html). The introduced bill text itself could not be retrieved (gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11), so its provisions are not described here.

## students-hb-1503-school-emergency-maps: HB 1503 (2026): School Emergency Maps From the Public School Infrastructure Fund

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1503 (2026): the Public School Infrastructure Fund may pay for maps of schools for police, fire and other emergency responders.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1503 (2026), "allowing the department of education to allocate funds to create a database of maps of public schools for emergency use cases," was signed by Governor Ayotte on April 22, 2026, became 2026 chapter 38, and took effect April 22, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1503 (2026), chapter 38
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1696&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Docket: "Signed by Governor Ayotte 04/22/2026; Chapter 38; eff. 04/22/2026." Raw: hb1503-2026-billinfo.html, hb1503-2026-docket.html.
2. HB 1503 (2026) added a new allowed use of New Hampshire's Public School Infrastructure Fund: to "enhance public school emergency readiness with critical incident mapping technology to allow emergency response personnel and internal teams to operate from a unified understanding of site-specific nomenclature and other place characteristics associated with building infrastructure and grounds."
   - citation: RSA 198:15-y, III(h), as inserted by 2026 ch. 38:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1696&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Chaptered final version (raw: hb1503-2026-chaptered-text.html).
3. New Hampshire's Public School Infrastructure Fund is a nonlapsing fund held by the state treasurer and administered by the Department of Education. The public school infrastructure commission may authorize the department to spend from it, with the approval of the legislature's fiscal committee, for the purposes listed in the law.
   - citation: RSA 198:15-y, II-III
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-15-y.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-08-03
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11; read from http://web.archive.org/web/20250803223956/https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-15-y.htm (raw: rsa-198-15-y-wayback-20250803.html), which predates HB 1503's paragraph III(h).
4. Before HB 1503, the uses New Hampshire allowed for the Public School Infrastructure Fund included repairs where a school's condition is a clear and imminent danger to life or safety, fiber optic connections, the Division of Homeland Security and Emergency Management's school emergency readiness program to improve school security, ADA compliance projects, school buses and other student transportation, and other needs the governor identifies with the commission.
   - citation: RSA 198:15-y, III(a)-(g)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/198/198-15-y.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2025-08-03
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. HB 1503 (2026) passed the House by voice vote on February 5, 2026, after the Education Funding Committee recommended it 16-0, and passed the Senate by voice vote on March 26, 2026, after the Senate Education Committee recommended it 4-0.
   - citation: HB 1503 (2026) docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1696&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
6. HB 1503 (2026) was sponsored by Representatives Ladd, Popovici-Muller, Peeples, Damon and Kofalt and Senator Victoria Sullivan. The law permits the use of the fund for school mapping but does not itself appropriate a dollar amount.
   - citation: HB 1503 (2026), chapter 38 (chaptered text)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1696&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The chaptered text has two sections: 38:1 (the new subparagraph) and 38:2 (effective on passage); no appropriation section.

## students-firearms-school-bills-2026: Firearms and Schools: 2026 Bills and Current Law

- kind: rule
- question_type: rules
- description: What happened to NH's 2026 bills on guns and schools (HB 1034, HB 1830, SB 463, HB 1793) and current law on safe school zones and federal gun-free school zones.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1034 (2026), sponsored by Representative Horrigan and others, would have made it a class A misdemeanor to knowingly possess a firearm on public school property, including buildings, grounds, school buses and vans. It exempted people picking up or dropping off a student if the firearm stayed in the vehicle, people authorized by name in writing by the school board after a public hearing, and law enforcement officers; it also would have barred schools from hiring armed guards who had not met the qualifications to work with students, including a criminal background check.
   - citation: HB 1034-FN (2026), as introduced, proposed RSA 159:19-b and 159:19-c
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1939&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-01-28
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11 after the status pages were saved; text read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260128061002/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?id=1939&txtFormat=html&sy=2026 (raw: hb1034-2026-introduced-text-wayback-20260128.html). The bill's analysis: "prohibits the hiring of armed guards in schools absent the satisfaction of a criminal background check."
2. HB 1034 (2026) did not become law. The House Criminal Justice and Public Safety Committee's majority recommended "inexpedient to legislate" by a 7-6 vote on February 11, 2026, with a minority recommending passage; the record shows no House floor vote, and the bill's status is "Died, Session Ended."
   - citation: HB 1034-FN (2026) docket and status
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1939&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: hb1034-2026-billinfo.html, hb1034-2026-docket.html.
3. New Hampshire's HB 1830 (2026), sponsored by Representatives Roy and Sabourin dit Choiniere, would have required at least one hour a year of age-appropriate firearms safety training in every public school starting in 2027-28, as part of health, physical education or civics. Grades K-5 would have focused on avoidance and reporting and grades 6-12 on handling, storage and firearms laws, with no live firearms or ammunition, and parents could have obtained an exception for their child.
   - citation: HB 1830-FN (2026), as introduced, proposed RSA 193-L:4-6
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2334&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11 and the Wayback copy of this page holds only a bot-check page, so the "As Introduced" text was read from the NH Liberty Alliance's copy (https://bills.nhliberty.org/bills/2026/HB1830/revision/50254, revision dated Jan. 16, 2026; raw: hb1830-2026-introduced-text-nhla-mirror-rev50254.html). Recheck against gc.nh.gov. The bill named the NRA's Eddie Eagle GunSafe Program "or equivalent programs approved by the department of education" as examples, and the parent exception was to follow RSA 186:11, IX-c.
4. HB 1830 (2026) did not become law: the House Education Policy and Administration Committee voted 17-0 to recommend "inexpedient to legislate," and the House agreed by voice vote on February 12, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1830-FN (2026) docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2334&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Raw: hb1830-2026-billinfo.html, hb1830-2026-docket.html.
5. New Hampshire's SB 463 (2026), sponsored by Senator Fenton and others, would have made it a class A misdemeanor to knowingly carry a firearm in a safe school zone. It exempted people picking up or dropping off a student if the firearm stayed in the vehicle unloaded and in a locked container or rack, people authorized in writing by the school board or its designee, and on-duty law enforcement officers, trained school resource officers and members of the armed forces or National Guard.
   - citation: SB 463-FN (2026), as introduced, proposed RSA 159:19-b
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1445&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-01-19
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260119062412/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1445&txtFormat=html (raw: sb463-2026-introduced-text-wayback-20260119.html); gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11.
6. SB 463 (2026) was killed in the Senate: the Judiciary Committee recommended "inexpedient to legislate" by a 3-2 vote, and the Senate agreed on a 16-8 roll call vote on February 5, 2026.
   - citation: SB 463-FN (2026) docket and roll call
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1445&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Docket: "Inexpedient to Legislate, RC 16Y-8N, MA === BILL KILLED ===; 02/05/2026; SJ 3." Raw: sb463-2026-billinfo.html, sb463-2026-docket.html.
7. New Hampshire's HB 1793 (2026), about public colleges and universities rather than K-12 schools, would have barred them from restricting the possession, carrying, storage or lawful use of firearms and non-lethal weapons on campus, and let people sue for violations with damages of at least $10,000. The House passed it 188-165 on February 5, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1793-FN (2026), as introduced, proposed RSA 159:28-31; House roll call, February 5, 2026
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2361&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The House vote is on the bill status page (raw: hb1793-2026-billinfo.html, hb1793-2026-docket.html). The bill's provisions are from the introduced text, read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260530003538/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&v=HI&id=2361&txtFormat=html (raw: hb1793-2026-introduced-text-wayback-20260530.html). It was to take effect January 1, 2027.
8. The Senate passed a narrower HB 1793 on a 14-8 roll call on May 14, 2026, covering only non-lethal weapons and firearms carried by faculty and adding a study committee. The House did not agree and asked for a committee of conference, and the Senate refused it on May 21, 2026, so HB 1793 did not become law.
   - citation: HB 1793-FN (2026) docket; Senate floor amendment 2026-1882s
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=2361&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The Senate version's content is from the "As Amended by the Senate" text, read from http://web.archive.org/web/20260522022320/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?v=SA&id=2361&sy=2026&txtFormat=html (raw: hb1793-2026-senate-amended-text-wayback-20260522.html). Docket: "Sen. Gannon Refused to Accede to House Request for Committee of Conference, MA, VV; 05/21/2026."
9. Under New Hampshire's safe school zones law, a "safe school zone" is any school property or school bus. "School property" means all real property, buildings and equipment used for school purposes, including playgrounds and buses, public or private, and "school" means any public or private elementary, secondary or vocational-technical school in the state, but not home schools.
   - citation: RSA 193-D:1, II, III and V
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-D/193-D-mrg.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-08-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from the merged chapter http://web.archive.org/web/20260811212313/https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/xv/193-d/193-d-mrg.htm (raw: rsa-193-D-mrg-wayback-20260811.html); gc.nh.gov refused connections on 2026-11-11.
10. Under New Hampshire's safe school zones law, an "act of theft, destruction, or violence" includes unlawful possession or sale of a firearm or other dangerous weapon under RSA 159. A person convicted of such an act in a safe school zone may get an extended prison term, and school employees who witness one must report it in writing; the principal must report it to local police.
   - citation: RSA 193-D:1, I(d); RSA 193-D:3; RSA 193-D:4, I(a)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193-D/193-D-mrg.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-08-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from the merged chapter snapshot (raw: rsa-193-D-mrg-wayback-20260811.html). Derived from reading the chapter: RSA 193-D has no section that by itself makes carrying a firearm in a safe school zone an offense; SB 463 and HB 1034 proposed adding one to RSA 159. Expulsion of students for firearms is in suspension-and-expulsion (RSA 193:13).
11. The federal Gun-Free School Zones Act of 1990 makes it unlawful to knowingly possess a firearm (one that has moved in or affects interstate commerce) at a place a person knows, or has reasonable cause to believe, is a school zone. A school zone is in or on the grounds of a public, parochial or private school, or within 1,000 feet of the grounds.
   - citation: 18 U.S.C. 922(q)(2)(A); 18 U.S.C. 921(a)(26); short title, Pub. L. 101-647, title XVII, § 1702(a)
   - source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/922
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Definition and short-title note from https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/921 (raw: usc-18-921-cornell.html; usc-18-922-cornell.html). "School" means a school providing elementary or secondary education as determined under state law (921(a)(27)).
12. The federal school zone ban does not apply on private property that is not part of school grounds, to someone licensed by the state where the school zone is (if the state verifies the person's qualifications before licensing), to an unloaded firearm in a locked container or locked rack on a vehicle, to school-approved programs or contracts, or to law enforcement officers acting officially. A violation can bring up to 5 years in prison, and the law does not stop states from making their own gun-free school zone laws.
   - citation: 18 U.S.C. 922(q)(2)(B), (q)(4); 18 U.S.C. 924(a)(4)
   - source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/922
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Penalty from https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/18/924 (raw: usc-18-924-cornell.html). Another exception covers an unloaded firearm carried across school grounds, with school permission, to reach hunting land.

---

# Teachers, Pay and Dual Enrollment

**Sources and notes for this section**

- **Topics:** 5 topics, 40 facts: the teacher workforce and shortage lists (10), the SB 236 teacher shortage study (8), statewide teacher pay (6), HB 1270 on part-time teachers (8) and HB 1202 / HB 716 on dual enrollment funding (8).
- **Sources:** General Court bill status pages, chaptered bill texts and RSAs (gc.nh.gov); NH DOE iPlatform reports (FTE staff count, teacher average salary, teacher salary schedule) and NH DOE Bureau of Credentialing pages; NCES IPEDS completions files; the U.S. Department of Education's Teacher Shortage Areas site; the SB 236 committee's final reports; the Economic Policy Institute's August 2026 report. The NH Fiscal Policy Institute's October 2026 issue brief and NHPR's October 13, 2026 story were used only to find the primary sources.
- **Dates:** every source was read on 2026-11-11. gc.nh.gov stopped answering this session's direct requests partway through, so some of its pages were archived with the Wayback Machine's Save Page Now on 2026-11-11 and read from those snapshots (marked "Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11"). EPI blocks automated requests, so its report was read from a Wayback snapshot of 2026-10-31.
- **Review dates:** enacted and failed bills and statutes 2027-08-15; the status of NH DOE's shortage list 2027-05-11; data, reports and research 2027-11-11.
- **Raw copies:** `additional-data/raw/state-issues/<topic-slug>/`. The NH DOE spreadsheets were copied from the backend's assets (`backend/app/alembic/assets/staff/`) into each topic's `nhdoe/` folder. The IPEDS folder keeps the original zip files plus New Hampshire extracts.
- **Derived figures:** every percentage change, count of list items, and comparison between lists or years was calculated here and is marked "derived" with the method in its note.

## staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages: New Hampshire's Teacher Workforce: Staffing Trends, Education Degrees and Shortage Areas

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: How many teachers NH public schools have against 10 years ago, education degrees awarded, and the state and federal teacher shortage area lists.

1. NH DOE's staff count shows 14,761.6 full-time-equivalent (FTE) teachers in New Hampshire public schools in 2015-16 and 14,173.4 in 2025-26, a drop of 588.2 FTE, or about 4.0% (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "2015 - 2016 Full Time Equivalent Staff Count by District" and "2025 - 2026 Full Time Equivalent Staff Count by District" (Report ID 38), State Total row, Teachers column
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/nhdoe/2016-FTE-Staff.xlsx (run 4/24/2025) and 2026-FTE-Staff.xlsx (run 11/1/2026), copied from the backend's assets; iPlatform blocks scripted downloads. Derived: (14,173.4 - 14,761.6) / 14,761.6 = -3.98%. The State Total is the sum of traditional districts, public academies and JMAs, and charter schools. The 2025-26 figure is also in measure-staff-categories.
2. In NH DOE's staff count, teachers in traditional school districts fell from 14,119.7 FTE in 2015-16 to 13,393.3 FTE in 2025-26 (about 5.1%, derived), while teachers in charter schools rose from 276.0 to 525.6 FTE and in public academies and joint maintenance agreement schools fell from 365.9 to 254.5 FTE.
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "Full Time Equivalent Staff Count by District" (Report ID 38), 2015-2016 and 2025-2026, "Traditional District Total," "Public Academies & JMA's Total" and "Charter Schools Total" rows, Teachers column
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Derived: (13,393.3 - 14,119.7) / 14,119.7 = -5.14%. Same raw files as fact 1.
3. NH DOE's teacher average salary report, which counts teachers in public school districts, public academies and joint maintenance agreement schools but not charter schools, counted 14,493.7 FTE teachers in 2015-16 and 13,647.8 in 2025-26, about 5.8% fewer (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE, "Teacher Average Salary in Public School Districts," School Year 2015-2016 (April 4, 2016), and "2025 - 2026 Teacher Average Salary in Public School Districts" (Report ID 20), State Average Salary row, "All Teachers" column and footnote *
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
