   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/nhdoe/2016-Teacher-Salary.xls and 2026-Teacher-Salary.xlsx. Derived: (13,647.8 - 14,493.7) / 14,493.7 = -5.84%. The NH Fiscal Policy Institute's October 6, 2026 issue brief reports a 5.7% drop, from 14,472.5 FTE in 2015-16 to 13,647.8 in 2025-26, citing NH DOE. The 2015-16 report saved here (data as of January 21, 2016) shows 14,493.7; a later re-run of the report may differ, and iPlatform could not be reached to check. Use this fact, not the 5.7%, unless the 14,472.5 figure is confirmed. The two NH DOE reports count teachers differently (fact 1 includes charter schools), so their totals differ.
4. Colleges and universities in New Hampshire awarded 534 bachelor's degrees in education in 2009-10, 567 in 2014-15, 442 in 2019-20 and 283 in 2024-25, according to federal IPEDS data; 283 is about 47% fewer than in 2009-10 and 50% fewer than in 2014-15 (derived).
   - citation: NCES, IPEDS Completions files C2010_A, C2015_A, C2020_A and C2025_A: bachelor's degrees (award level 5) in CIP 13 (Education) at institutions located in NH, first and second majors
   - source: https://nces.ed.gov/ipeds/datacenter/DataFiles.aspx?year=2025&surveyNumber=3
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Derived by adding every 6-digit CIP 13.xxxx row at award level 5 for NH institutions (state from the HD file of the same year); 2010, 2015 and 2020 use the revised (final) files. First majors alone: 511, 498, 388 and 255. Award years run July 1 to June 30. The 2024-25 data are IPEDS's provisional release. Zips from https://nces.ed.gov/ipeds/datacenter/data/C2010_A.zip (and C2015_A, C2020_A) and https://nces.ed.gov/ipeds/complete-data-files/C2025_A.zip; NH extracts in raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/ipeds/. The totals match the figures in the NH Fiscal Policy Institute's October 2026 brief and NHPR's October 13, 2026 story (534 in 2010, 283 in 2025). An education degree is only one route to teaching, and not every graduate teaches in NH.
5. As of November 11, 2026, NH DOE's posted critical shortage list is the one dated March 11, 2025, which stays in effect "until replaced by a new list"; it names 23 teacher endorsements (count derived), including chemistry, physics, life science and physical science (grades 7-12), middle and upper level mathematics, early childhood education (N-3), several special education endorsements, computer science, and English for speakers of other languages, plus school counselor and reading and writing specialist.
   - citation: NH DOE Bureau of Credentialing, "Critical Shortage List" memo dated 3/11/25 ("CRITICAL SHORTAGE LIST (effective 3/11/25 until replaced by a new list)")
   - source: https://nhdoepm.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/CHD/pages/347963415/Critical+Shortage+List
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-05-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/critical-shortage-list-page.json (Confluence page last updated 2025-03-11). The 23 teacher endorsements are: blind and vision disabilities; career and technical education specialty endorsements; chemistry (7-12); comprehensive family and consumer science; computer science; deaf and hearing disabilities; early childhood education (N-3); early childhood special education (N-3); emotional and behavioral disabilities; English (5-8); English for speakers of other languages; health education; intellectual and developmental disabilities; life science (7-12); mathematics, middle level; mathematics, upper level; music education; physical education; physical science (7-12); physics (7-12); special education teacher; specific learning disabilities; world languages teacher (French, Spanish). No NH DOE list labeled 2026-27 was found; the lead that described one probably took the subjects from the NH Fiscal Policy Institute's brief, which describes this March 2025 list.
6. NH DOE says its critical shortage list comes from an analysis of teacher supply and demand based on a survey and other data, and that the endorsements on it are available under the Site-Based Licensing Plan.
   - citation: NH DOE Bureau of Credentialing, "Critical Shortage List" memo dated 3/11/25, opening paragraph
   - source: https://nhdoepm.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/CHD/pages/347963415/Critical+Shortage+List
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The memo reads: "The Department of Education has completed an analysis of the teacher supply and demand in NH. Based on the results of a survey and other data available to the Department, the following endorsements have been identified as critical shortage areas and are available under the Site-Based Licensing Plan."
7. Compared with NH DOE's previous critical shortage list (effective September 1, 2023), the March 2025 list added physics, life science, computer science, music education, physical education, comprehensive family and consumer science, and career and technical education specialty endorsements; it dropped English (5-12), social studies (5-12), technology and engineering, reading and writing teacher, physical and health disabilities, school psychologist, school social worker, library media specialist, elementary mathematics specialist and career and technical education principal (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE Bureau of Credentialing, "Critical Shortage List" memos dated 3/11/25 and 8/15/2023 (the latter under "Previous Critical Shortage List, 9/1/2023 - 3/11/2025")
   - source: https://nhdoepm.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/CHD/pages/347963415/Critical+Shortage+List
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Derived by comparing the two lists on the same page. The 2023 list had 21 teacher endorsements and the 2025 list 23 (counts derived). Both lists name school counselor and reading and writing specialist.
8. Under New Hampshire law, a superintendent, with the approval of the local school board, may determine that a critical staffing shortage exists in specific teaching areas in the school district even without a state determination, and must notify NH DOE within 30 days.
   - citation: RSA 189:39-a
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-39-a.htm
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from a snapshot made with Save Page Now on 2026-11-11 (https://web.archive.org/web/20261111052308/https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-39-a.htm) because gc.nh.gov stopped answering this session; raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/rsa-189-39-a-wayback-20261111052308.htm. Source note: 2002, 117:1, eff. July 2, 2002.
9. NH DOE says a district may declare a local critical shortage for any endorsement not already on the state list, except school nurse, school psychologist, assistant principal, principal, district administrator, assistant superintendent and superintendent, by sending NH DOE a superintendent's letter stating that the school board approved it.
   - citation: NH DOE Bureau of Credentialing, "Local Critical Shortage" page
   - source: https://nhdoepm.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/CHD/pages/2072838145/Local+Critical+Shortage
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/local-critical-shortage-page.json (page last updated 2026-07-02).
10. For 2025-26, the U.S. Department of Education's Teacher Shortage Areas site lists 22 New Hampshire shortage areas, including physics, chemistry, physical science and life sciences (grades 7-12), mathematics (grades 5-9), special education, career and technical education, English (grades 5-8), English as a second language, computer science, and guidance and counseling. The site says it shows shortage areas eligible for federal TEACH Grants and that states are not required to report all of their shortage areas.
    - citation: U.S. Department of Education, Teacher Shortage Areas, View Reports, New Hampshire, 2025-2026; site notice on the home page
    - source: https://tsa.ed.gov/
    - as_of: 2026-11-11
    - review_by: 2027-11-11
    - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-workforce-and-shortages/tsa-nh-2025_2026.json (the site's report API, 22 rows) and tsa-about.html (notice: "States are not required to report all teacher shortage areas. This website only displays the top 5 percent of teacher shortage areas. The shortage areas displayed are those that are eligible for TEACH Grants."). The other areas are French and Spanish, physical education, health science, consumer home economics, reading resource specialist, and several special education areas (generic, early childhood, emotional disabilities, developmental disabilities, visual impairment, deaf/hearing impairment). The same query for 2026-27 returned no New Hampshire rows on 2026-11-11 (tsa-nh-2026_2027.json).

## staff-sb-236-teacher-shortage-study: SB 236 (2022): The Legislature's Teacher Shortage Study and Its 2023 Reports

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: What the legislature's teacher shortage study committee (SB 236, 2022) found and recommended in 2023 on shortages, pay, housing and the teacher pipeline.

1. SB 236, chapter 150 of the Laws of 2022, created the Committee to Study New Hampshire Teacher Shortages and Recruitment Incentives, a joint legislative study committee chaired by Senator Ruth Ward, which filed majority and minority final reports on November 30, 2023.
   - citation: Final Reports of the Committee to Study New Hampshire Teacher Shortages and Recruitment Incentives, SB 236, Chapter 150:1, Laws of 2022, cover memo dated November 30, 2023, and pp. 2, 14
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Read from https://web.archive.org/web/20261023024602/https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf because gc.nh.gov stopped answering this session; raw/state-issues/staff-sb-236-teacher-shortage-study/sb236-final-report-wayback-20261023.pdf (151 pages, including meeting minutes). The signature page (p. 24) lists Senators Ruth Ward (chair) and Donovan Fenton and Representatives Rick Ladd, Mel Myler and Oliver Ford. The act's title also covers "defining secondary school grades for teacher loan forgiveness programs."
2. Both SB 236 committee reports found that "the teacher shortage is real" and that its top concerns are stress and burnout, student behavior and discipline, school culture, and low salaries.
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority finding 1 (p. 2) and minority finding 1 (p. 14)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
3. The SB 236 reports said a new New Hampshire teacher's starting salary was $40,478, "much less than $56,727 per year, the average cost of living in the state," and that property-wealthy communities offer higher pay while smaller, rural and property-poor communities "cannot afford to compete."
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority findings 8-9 (p. 6); minority findings 8-9 (p. 18)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The reports don't say which year or source the $40,478 starting salary is from. For current starting pay, see staff-teacher-pay-statewide and NEA's starting salary table (nea_starting_teacher_salary in national-comparisons.md).
4. The SB 236 reports said many beginning teachers were unable to take jobs because of the lack of affordable housing and the overall cost of living in New Hampshire.
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority finding 10 (p. 6); minority finding 10 (p. 18)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
5. A table in the SB 236 reports, from federal Title II data, showed the number of people completing educator preparation programs at New Hampshire colleges falling from 983 in 2013-14 to 530 in 2021-22 (a preliminary count). The reports also said the number of educators on alternative pathways to certification had grown from 400 four years earlier to 1,200.
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority finding 3, Table 1 "NH Higher Education Educator Preparation Program Graduates" (p. 4), and finding 4 (p. 4)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: 983 and 530 are the table's "Subtotal IHE EPP completers" row; its "Total Program Completers (Title II)" row reads 983 for 2013-14 and 679 for 2021-22. 2021-22 counts are marked "preliminary counts - not finalized in TII yet." The "four years ago" comparison is as of the committee's work in 2022-23.
6. The SB 236 reports recommended, among other steps, a rural and underserved area educator incentive program; a state salary bonus or loan replacement program for new teachers from in-state colleges who teach in a documented shortage area or in a rural or high-poverty district where achievement is below state averages; more interstate reciprocity for teaching credentials; a separate salary grid for career and technical education instructors based on industry experience and credentials; and a review of retirement system benefits.
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority recommendations 2, 3, 5 and 8 (pp. 8-12); minority recommendations 2, 3, 5 and 8 (pp. 20-23)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Other recommendations in both reports: paid student teaching, fewer repeated background checks (each said to cost $150), teacher mentoring, "grow your own" programs starting in high school and CTE centers, more education funding for smaller communities, and mental health resources for teachers. Recommendation 1 also says the state budget then in effect raised state aid to districts by more than $169 million over two years.
7. Both SB 236 reports noted testimony that fear in teaching controversial topics "due to the passage of HB2 in 2021" was affecting teacher attrition and retention, stated that the law "does not restrict or prohibit discussing, as part of a larger course of academic instruction, the historical existence of ideas and subjects," and recommended training on teaching controversial topics.
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority recommendation 6 (p. 11); minority recommendation 6 (pp. 22-23)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: The full sentence: "The committee heard testimony that fear in the teaching of controversial topics and critical thinking skills due to the passage of HB2 in 2021, has placed an unhealthy pall over teaching, and consequently influencing attrition and retention of teachers."
8. The two SB 236 reports are largely the same, but the majority report has two findings the minority report does not: that school climate and culture "has been impacted by the overall political climate of our state," and that teachers "expressed concerns over the shift in community and public opinion regarding the state's public schools."
   - citation: SB 236 Final Reports, majority findings 11 and 14 (pp. 6-7); minority findings 1-15 (pp. 14-19)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/committees/1576/reports/SB%20236-%20Final%20Report.pdf
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-23
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Derived by comparing the two reports' texts: the majority report has 17 findings and the minority report 15; the other findings and the 10 recommendations match. The majority finding 11 also says teachers leaving "most often cite the climate and culture as the biggest factor in their departure." The extracted text does not show which members signed which report.

## staff-teacher-pay-statewide: Teacher Pay in New Hampshire: Statewide Average, Starting Pay and the Gap With Other Graduates

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: NH teacher pay: the average teacher salary over 10 years, starting salaries across districts, and the Economic Policy Institute's teacher pay penalty estimate.

1. NH DOE's statewide average teacher salary was $56,616 in 2015-16, $69,531.78 in 2024-25 and $71,857.32 in 2025-26; the 2025-26 average is about 26.9% higher than in 2015-16, before adjusting for inflation (derived).
   - citation: NH DOE, "Teacher Average Salary in Public School Districts," School Year 2015-2016 (April 4, 2016), and "2024 - 2025" and "2025 - 2026 Teacher Average Salary in Public School Districts" (Report ID 20), State Average Salary row
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-pay-statewide/nhdoe/2016-Teacher-Salary.xls, 2025-Teacher-Salary.xlsx, 2026-Teacher-Salary.xlsx. Derived: 71,857.32 / 56,616 = 1.269. All three years cover public school districts, public academies and joint maintenance agreement schools; charter school data are for reference only. NEA's average for 2024-25 is $69,432 (rank 25) and its average starting salary $44,010 (rank 42); both are already in national-comparisons.md (nea_avg_teacher_salary, nea_starting_teacher_salary) and come from NEA's own reports, so they differ from NH DOE's figures. The lead's "$69,432" is NEA's figure, not NH DOE's.
2. In NH DOE's 2025-26 salary schedule report, the lowest step for a teacher with a bachelor's degree ranged from $36,148 in Bath to $59,396 in New Castle among the districts that reported.
   - citation: NH DOE iPlatform, "2025 - 2026 Teacher Salary Schedule" (Report ID 22), BA rows, Minimum Salary column
   - source: https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-pay-statewide/nhdoe/2026-Teacher-Starting.xlsx. Derived from 162 districts with a BA row; Croydon, Pasquaney, Coe-Brown Northwood Academy and Pinkerton Academy "Did Not Report," and charter and interstate districts are not included. Jackson's BA minimum is listed as $4,600 (its 2024-25 figure was $46,000), apparently a missing digit, and is left out. Next lowest: Warren $36,851, Pittsfield $37,113; next highest: Rye $57,783, Lyme $57,134. The site's own salary schedule data (measure-teacher-salary-schedule) give each district's figures.
3. The Economic Policy Institute (EPI) estimates that public school teachers in New Hampshire earned 33.8% less per week than other college graduates in the state, using pooled 2020-2025 survey data.
   - citation: Sylvia Allegretto, "The teacher pay penalty remained high in 2025: Teachers and students nationwide are still paying the price of decades of neglect," Economic Policy Institute, August 25, 2026, Figure C
   - source: https://www.epi.org/publication/the-teacher-pay-penalty-remained-high-in-2025-teachers-and-students-nationwide-are-still-paying-the-price-of-decades-of-neglect/
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-31
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: epi.org returns 403 to scripts; read from https://web.archive.org/web/20261031074706/https://www.epi.org/publication/the-teacher-pay-penalty-remained-high-in-2025-teachers-and-students-nationwide-are-still-paying-the-price-of-decades-of-neglect/ (raw/state-issues/staff-teacher-pay-statewide/epi-teacher-pay-penalty-2025-wayback-20261031.html). Figure C lists New Hampshire at -33.8%. The lead's "about 34%" and NHPR's "a third less" round this figure.
4. EPI's teacher pay penalty compares the weekly wages of public school teachers (elementary, middle and secondary) with those of other college graduates in the same state, using full-time workers aged 18 to 64 with at least a bachelor's degree from the Current Population Survey, and adjusts for characteristics such as age, gender, race and ethnicity, and marital status.
   - citation: EPI, "The teacher pay penalty remained high in 2025," August 25, 2026, "Data and relevant information" and "Relative teacher weekly wage penalties by state"
   - source: https://www.epi.org/publication/the-teacher-pay-penalty-remained-high-in-2025-teachers-and-students-nationwide-are-still-paying-the-price-of-decades-of-neglect/
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-31
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: EPI says it uses weekly wages "to account for the 'summers off' issue that affects teachers but not other college graduates," and that the 2025 data exclude October because of the federal government shutdown. The state estimates pool six years (2020-2025) for adequate sample sizes.
5. In EPI's state estimates, only Colorado (40.7%), Missouri (35.9%), Arizona (34.6%) and Kentucky (34.3%) had larger teacher pay penalties than New Hampshire; the penalty was 23.0% in Massachusetts, 21.0% in Maine and 12.3% in Vermont, and the smallest was 10.4% in Rhode Island.
   - citation: EPI, "The teacher pay penalty remained high in 2025," August 25, 2026, Figure C
   - source: https://www.epi.org/publication/the-teacher-pay-penalty-remained-high-in-2025-teachers-and-students-nationwide-are-still-paying-the-price-of-decades-of-neglect/
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-31
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: Figure C covers the 50 states and DC, sorted from largest to smallest penalty; EPI does not number ranks, so "only four states were larger" is read from the order (derived).
6. EPI estimates that nationally, public school teachers earned 25.2% less per week than comparable college graduates in 2025, and that after counting teachers' larger benefits, their total compensation was 14.5% lower.
   - citation: EPI, "The teacher pay penalty remained high in 2025," August 25, 2026, Key takeaways and Table 1
   - source: https://www.epi.org/publication/the-teacher-pay-penalty-remained-high-in-2025-teachers-and-students-nationwide-are-still-paying-the-price-of-decades-of-neglect/
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-10-31
   - review_by: 2027-11-11
   - note: EPI reports the 2024 wage penalty as 26.9% and the 2024 total compensation penalty as 16.7%. Benefits counted: health and life insurance, retirement plans and payroll taxes.

## staff-hb-1270-part-time-teachers: HB 1270 (2026): Part-Time Teachers Without a State Credential

- kind: rule
- question_type: rules
- description: Who may teach part time in NH public schools without a teaching credential under RSA 189:39-c (HB 90, 2025; HB 1270, 2026), and when the changes took effect.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1270 (2026), "clarifying the definition of part-time teacher," was signed by Governor Ayotte on May 8, 2026, became 2026 chapter 52, and took effect July 7, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1270 (2026), chapter 52, docket and chaptered text ("Approved: May 08, 2026; Effective Date: July 07, 2026")
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1725&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-hb-1270-part-time-teachers/hb1270-billinfo.html and hb1270-chaptered.pdf. The prime sponsor was Rep. Margaret Drye (R). The lead's "May 12, 2026" signing date is not supported by the record.
2. HB 1270 passed without roll calls: the House Education Policy and Administration Committee recommended it 18-0 and the House passed it by voice vote on February 5, 2026; the Senate Education Committee recommended it 4-1 and the Senate passed it by voice vote on March 26, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1270 (2026) bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1725&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: House committee report January 22, 2026 (HC 5 P. 5); Senate committee report dated March 26, 2026 (SC 11). The bill was not amended.
3. Under New Hampshire's part-time teacher law, a part-time teacher does not need a state board of education credential if the person works up to 20 hours a week, seeks a criminal history record check clearance from NH DOE before a final job offer, is employed or contracted as full-time or adjunct faculty by a University System of New Hampshire or Community College System of New Hampshire institution, and has expertise or significant professional experience to teach the subject.
   - citation: RSA 189:39-c, I(a)-(d)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-39-c.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: raw/state-issues/staff-hb-1270-part-time-teachers/rsa-189-39-c.htm (shows the 2026 amendment). The statute lists the four conditions as items (a)-(d) after "provided that the individual."
4. Under RSA 189:39-c, a part-time teacher may teach a subject offered by the school or district, or an approved college dual or concurrent enrollment course in the high school that has the same quality and rigor as the course offered on campus at the sponsoring college or university.
   - citation: RSA 189:39-c, I(d)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-39-c.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
5. HB 1270 added that the part-time teacher's expertise or professional experience is "as determined by the superintendent or school principal."
   - citation: HB 1270 (2026), chapter 52:1, amending RSA 189:39-c, I(d)
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1725&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Added text appears in bold italics in the chaptered text (raw/state-issues/staff-hb-1270-part-time-teachers/hb1270-billtext-legacy.html). The bill's analysis says it requires "a superintendent or school principal approval of part-time teachers' expertise to teach in a subject area."
6. HB 1270 replaced the rule that part-time teachers "shall practice the principles identified in the New Hampshire code of conduct and ethics for educational professionals" with one stating that "the professional code of ethics and the professional code of conduct shall apply to all individuals working as a part-time teacher."
   - citation: HB 1270 (2026), chapter 52:1, amending RSA 189:39-c, II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1725&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
7. A person whose educator credential, license or certification was revoked, or who is otherwise ineligible to be employed as an educator, may not teach under New Hampshire's part-time teacher law, and the state board of education must adopt rules to carry out the law; HB 1270 did not change these parts.
   - citation: RSA 189:39-c, III-IV; HB 1270 (2026), chapter 52:1
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/189/189-39-c.htm
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Revocations under RSA 189:14-c or 189:14-d. Whether the state board has adopted those rules was not checked.
8. New Hampshire's part-time teacher law, RSA 189:39-c, was created by HB 90 (2025), which Governor Ayotte signed on July 7, 2025 as chapter 151, effective that day; the House passed HB 90 by a 198-139 division vote on March 26, 2025, and the Senate by voice vote on May 15, 2025.
   - citation: HB 90 (2025), chapter 151; RSA 189:39-c source note ("2025, 151:1, eff. July 7, 2025")
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=128&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from a Save Page Now snapshot (https://web.archive.org/web/20261111052031/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=128&inflect=2), raw/state-issues/staff-hb-1270-part-time-teachers/hb90-2025-billinfo-wayback-20261111.html. HB 90's title: "relative to the definition of part-time teachers"; prime sponsor Rep. Ladd. The House committee split 10-8 (majority ought to pass with amendment, minority inexpedient to legislate); in the Senate, a committee amendment failed 9-14 on a roll call before the bill passed without it.

## students-hb-1202-dual-enrollment-funding: HB 1202 (2026) and HB 716: Dual Enrollment Funding Bills

- kind: how_it_works
- question_type: governance_money
- description: HB 1202 (2026) on state-funded dual enrollment courses (Running Start, Early College) at the community colleges, the 4-credit limit, and the failed HB 716.

1. New Hampshire's HB 1202 (2026), on dual and concurrent enrollment courses at the community colleges, was signed by Governor Ayotte on April 22, 2026, became 2026 chapter 31, and took effect June 21, 2026; it is the amendment to RSA 188-E:27 that took effect that day.
   - citation: HB 1202 (2026), chapter 31, docket and chaptered text ("Approved: April 22, 2026; Effective Date: June 21, 2026")
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1785&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: raw/state-issues/students-hb-1202-dual-enrollment-funding/hb1202-billinfo.html; chaptered PDF read from a Save Page Now snapshot (hb1202-chaptered-wayback-20261111.pdf). This resolves the "2026 ch. 31:1, eff. 2026-06-21" citation in dual-enrollment-rules, whose fact 3 matches the amended text.
2. HB 1202's official analysis says the bill "is a request of the community college system of New Hampshire"; its prime sponsor was Rep. Ladd (R).
   - citation: HB 1202 (2026), chapter 31, analysis and sponsors
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1785&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: The analysis also says the bill "allows oversight of dual or concurrent enrollment of high schoolers in the community college system." raw/state-issues/students-hb-1202-dual-enrollment-funding/hb1202-billtext-legacy-wayback-20261111.html.
3. HB 1202 passed without roll calls: the House Education Funding Committee recommended it 16-0 and the House passed it by voice vote on February 5, 2026; the Senate Education Committee recommended it 5-0 and the Senate passed it by voice vote on March 26, 2026.
   - citation: HB 1202 (2026) bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1785&inflect=2
   - as_of: 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: House committee report January 27, 2026 (HC 5 P. 4). The bill was not amended.
4. HB 1202 removed the sentence "Each course funded pursuant to this paragraph shall not exceed 4 credits" from New Hampshire's dual and concurrent enrollment funding rule, so the law no longer limits the credits of a state-funded course.
   - citation: HB 1202 (2026), chapter 31:1, amending RSA 188-E:27, II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1785&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Removed text appears in brackets with strikethrough in the chaptered text. The limit of 4 funded courses in each of grades 10, 11 and 12 was not changed.
5. HB 1202 made the state funding of up to 4 dual or concurrent enrollment courses per grade "subject to policies adopted by the community college system of New Hampshire."
   - citation: HB 1202 (2026), chapter 31:1, amending RSA 188-E:27, II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1785&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Added text appears in bold italics. This may bear on the question noted in dual-enrollment-rules (fact 3 note): CCSNH's own page offers up to two tuition-free courses a year, subject to funding; the statute now makes the funded courses subject to CCSNH policies. That reading is not stated in either source.
6. Before HB 1202, a student could take more than 4 dual or concurrent enrollment courses a year, "or courses exceeding 4 credits," at the student's own expense; HB 1202 removed the words about courses over 4 credits and kept the option to take more than 4 courses a year at the student's own expense.
   - citation: HB 1202 (2026), chapter 31:1, amending RSA 188-E:27, II
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=1785&txtFormat=html
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
7. HB 716, filed in the 2025 session, would have appropriated money to the Community College System of New Hampshire for scholarships and program support for the dual and concurrent enrollment program: $3,000,000 in each of fiscal years 2026 and 2027 as introduced, reduced to $2,500,000 a year in the version the House passed by voice vote on February 20, 2025.
   - citation: HB 716-FN (2025), as introduced and as amended by the House (amendment 2025-0168h), section 1 and LBA fiscal notes
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=656&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Texts read from Save Page Now snapshots of 2026-11-11 (raw/state-issues/students-hb-1202-dual-enrollment-funding/hb716-introduced-wayback-20261111.pdf, hb716-as-amended-house-wayback-20261111.pdf). Both versions made the money non-lapsing and in addition to other CCSNH appropriations; the fiscal notes show general fund money. Prime sponsor: Rep. Ladd. The House Education Funding Committee recommended the amended bill 18-0 on February 4, 2025.
8. After the House passed HB 716 it went to the House Finance Committee, which retained it, then voted 25-0 on October 30, 2025 that it was inexpedient to legislate; the House adopted that recommendation by voice vote on January 7, 2026, which killed the bill.
   - citation: HB 716-FN (2025) bill docket
   - source: https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=656&inflect=2
   - as_of: Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11
   - review_by: 2027-08-15
   - note: Read from https://web.archive.org/web/20261111051640/https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=656&inflect=2 (raw/state-issues/students-hb-1202-dual-enrollment-funding/hb716-billinfo-wayback-20261111.html); general status "HOUSE," committee status "INEXPEDIENT TO LEGISLATE," House journal HJ 1 P. 72. The lead's "failed in the House on March 27, 2026" is not supported by the record; that date appears only on third-party trackers.

---

The topic states what the reenacted section says without a word-by-word comparison.

The Supreme Court's case page (https://www.courts.nh.gov/media/requested-cases/supreme-court/civil/2026-0150-steven-rand-v-state-new-hampshire) listed nothing after the State's August 14, 2026 brief, and the posted docket card was printed August 18, 2026, when the plaintiffs' brief was due October 6, 2026. The only November 3 filing found is the ACLU/ACLU-NH/NEA-NH amicus brief. Any later extension order was not found.

The court's oral argument calendars posted through November 24, 2026 (nov_10_2026.pdf, nov_23_2026.pdf, nov_24_2026.pdf, saved in raw) do not list the case.
- **A primary record of the State's legal spending on ConVal and Rand.** The Attorney General's office gave the Concord Monitor totals instead of records under a right-to-know request; no Fiscal Committee or Executive Council item naming these cases was found. The Executive Council's October 2025 approval of $4.3 million was for the Department of Justice's litigation costs generally, per the same news report. Fact 12 of money-rand-appeal-2026 cites the news report and says so.

The Supreme Court and Superior Court ConVal case pages (raw money-conval-aftermath/) list no filing after July 24, 2025. What followed in the Legislature is in money-hb-1815-adequacy-definition.

### Special Education Aid, School Buildings, Medicaid and Federal Funding

- **HB 1104 (2026), HB 366 (2025-26) and HB 295 (2025-26), school building aid bills.** gc.nh.gov returned empty replies from about 00:06 on 2026-11-11 for every address (bill status, RSA and LBA pages), so their status pages could not be read. Leads, unverified: HB 1104 (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1721&inflect=2) would have removed the priority for projects not funded the prior year (it did not add one, as the task lead said); the docket as reproduced by bills.nhliberty.org shows a 16-0 committee vote for inexpedient to legislate and a House ITL voice vote on 02/05/2026. HB 366 (new title "modifying the priority of applications for school building aid grants") passed the House 195-155 on 01/07/2026 and was killed in the Senate by voice vote on 03/05/2026 after a 6-0 committee ITL vote; one search summary says the introduced bill set a $60 million yearly floor. HB 295 ("making school building aid program funds nonlapsing") was voted ITL by the House 191-150 on 01/07/2026. Bill ids for HB 366 and HB 295 were not found. Re-check on gc.nh.gov.
- **NH DOE facility data on building ages** ("more than 150 of 461 schools built 1950-70"). Found only in a New Hampshire Business Review page; no NH DOE dataset. The LBA presentation "Division II - NHED School Building Aid - 02-01-25.pdf" (https://gc.nh.gov/LBA/Budget/operating_budgets/2026-2027/HF_Division_II/) may have it but gc.nh.gov was blocked and Wayback has no copy.
- **LBA "Division II - NHED Special Education Aid Overview - 02-25-25.pdf"** and the DHHS Medicaid briefing book (2-21-25): gc.nh.gov blocked; no Wayback copy found (CDX timed out).
- **The "2% cap on growth" for special education aid in the 2026-27 budget.** Not found in HB 2 (2025, chapter 141) or HB 1 (chapter 140). The 2% yearly increases in the 2025 budget are for adequacy amounts (RSA 198:40-d) and the extraordinary need and fiscal capacity grant thresholds, already in the library. The "at least 80%" rule is confirmed (topic money-special-education-costs-trends, fact 9).
- **Out-of-district special education placements.** NH DOE's special education data page (https://www.education.nh.gov/who-we-are/division-of-learner-support/bureau-of-student-support/special-education-data) has no placement report; the federal IDEA Section 618 educational environments files were not reached (the data.gov catalog API returned 404).
- **Statewide total special education spending** (including related services and transportation), such as the reported $842.7 million for 2021-22 or $978 million for 2023-24. The NH DOE report that breaks out these costs was not found; the 2021-22 figure appears in a Dover public meeting document quoting a "DoE report".
- **The reported $29 million in lost Medicaid to Schools funding (2019).** Appears in a Concord Monitor article (December 2019) without attribution; the Governor's fact sheet and DHHS documents found give no total.
- **Effect of the 2025 federal budget law (P.L. 119-21) on school-based Medicaid in New Hampshire.** No DHHS, NH DOE or CMS document stating an effect was found. The June 2026 Medicaid to Schools item does not mention it.
- **RSA 186-C:25** (school districts as Medicaid providers): gc.nh.gov blocked; cited only as DHHS describes it.
- **Final FY2026 Title I and IDEA allocations to New Hampshire.** ED's state tables give FY2026 "estimates" (compiled March 16, 2026); ED's final allocation tables for FY2026 were not located.
- **Governor and Council approval of the school building aid list or of individual FY2026-27 building aid awards.** Not searched beyond NH DOE's pages.

### School Choice

- **NH DOE count of districts with open enrollment.** No Department of Education count of districts that have adopted open enrollment under RSA 194-D was found. Tried: the Department's news feed (https://www.education.nh.gov/content/api/news?text=open%20enrollment, two unrelated results), its Pathways pages, and web searches. InDepthNH (June 4, 2026) reported that "nearly 100 school districts" adopted policies at spring 2026 meetings not to let their students take part, and an older NH Bulletin story named only Prospect Mountain and Franklin as adopters; neither is a Department figure, so neither is used.

The state estimates found are the Department of Education's (about $51.6 million for 2025-26; $68.1 million calculated for 2026-27) and the LBA fiscal note for HB 1834 ($62.6 million for FY2027 under the law as it stood). ### Rights and Classroom Rules

Justia's docket page (https://dockets.justia.com/docket/circuit-courts/ca1/24-1690) returned 403; CourtListener has no appellate docket for it. The April 8, 2025 argument date is from NHPR, not the docket. Whether the appeal ended without an opinion (for example, by order) could not be checked.

- HB 1792 Senate committee amendment 2026-1744s (rejected on the floor): the Wayback capture of https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&txtFormat=amend&id=2026-1744s returned a bot-check page. News reports describe provisions on student names, grading and classroom neutrality that may come from this amendment; they are not used.
- Text of HB 1788 (2026) as passed by the House and HB 50 (2025) as passed by the House: only titles and dockets were read from gc.nh.gov; the House-amended texts were not retrieved.
- NEA-NH v. NH Attorney General summary judgment filings (Docs. 51, 61) and the stipulation in NEA v. U.S. Department of Education (Doc. 99): PACER only; ACLU-NH's copy of the plaintiffs' memorandum (https://www.aclu-nh.org/app/uploads/2025/08/51-1-SJ-Memo-of-Law.pdf) was not read.
- Direct downloads from gc.nh.gov failed from about 00:05 to 01:00 on 2026-11-11 (connections closed with no reply). Facts read in that window rely on Wayback captures, marked in their as_of lines.

### School Climate, Students and School Standards

- **gc.nh.gov live pages (all topics):** from about 00:06 to at least 00:50 on 2026-11-11, gc.nh.gov closed every connection from this machine (curl "Empty reply from server"; Chrome "ERR_SOCKET_NOT_CONNECTED"), retried about every 20 seconds for 20 minutes. Only the SB 434 status page (https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=1219&inflect=2) was read live. Other bill pages, bill texts and RSA pages were read from Wayback snapshots (dated in each fact) or from copies other groups downloaded earlier that day; the Ed 300 rules page is a 2026-11-08 copy. Re-check these against the live pages.
- **RSA 193:42 (sports law causes of action):** https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/XV/193/193-42.htm. Unreachable live and no Wayback snapshot. Its content is stated from the Tirrell court's quotation of it; the 2-year filing limit in RSA 193:42, IV was seen only in a non-official mirror and is left out.
- **HB 1584 (2026) final text and new RSA 141-C:20-g:** https://gc.nh.gov/rsa/html/X/141-C/141-C-20-g.htm and https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2450&txtFormat=html. Unreachable live; the only Wayback copy of the bill text (June 3, 2026) predates the conference report, and the RSA snapshot (February 9, 2026) predates the law. A non-official mirror (bills.nhliberty.org) shows the chaptered text adding RSA 141-C:20-g, which requires DHHS materials on required childhood immunizations distributed for school and child care enrollment to state: "Medical and religious exemptions are available under New Hampshire law." Not used as a fact until confirmed on gc.nh.gov.
- **SB 33 (2025-2026), "relative to the regulation of public school materials":** https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/billinfo.aspx?id=219. Unreachable and not in Wayback. A non-official mirror shows the House passed it with an amendment 181-157 on January 7, 2026 and the Senate voted not to concur on February 5, 2026, with no later action; not confirmed.
- **HB 1205 (2024) bill status and votes:** the 2024 bill status page could not be searched (gc.nh.gov unreachable; bill id unknown). The signing date and chapter come from the Tirrell court opinion; the House (189-182, March 21, 2024) and Senate (13-10, May 16, 2024) votes seen in mirrors are not used.
- **JLCAR action on Ed 306:** the Joint Legislative Committee on Administrative Rules' 2024 votes (news reports say it tabled part of the proposal on September 19, 2024 and later approved it) and any 2025-26 JLCAR action on Ed 306 were not retrieved; JLCAR minutes and the Rulemaking Register are on gc.nh.gov (https://gc.nh.gov/rules/jlcar/). The 2026-11-08 copy of the Ed 300 rules shows no filing affecting Ed 306 after Documents #14150 and #14151, and NH DOE's initial-proposals page lists no pending Ed 306 proposal (gov-ed-306-2024-rewrite, fact 12).
- **Legislative adoption of the 2024 Ed 306 changes under RSA 193-E:2-a, IV(a):** whether the General Court has adopted any of the 2024 changes into the standards that define an adequate education was not checked.
- **NH DOE technical advisory on the phone law:** the 2025 NH DOE "Technical advisory: Guidance to public and chartered public schools for implementing HB2's 'bell-to-bell' prohibitions" cited in NH DOE's survey memo was not located on education.nh.gov.
- **U.S. Supreme Court, West Virginia v. B.P.J. and Little v. Hecox (decided June 30, 2026):** the opinions were not read; the Tirrell topic states only what the district court's orders say about them.

### Students and Safety

- **Chronic absenteeism, newer data (topic not written).** No NH DOE statewide chronic absenteeism rate for 2023-24 or 2024-25 was found (searches of NH DOE pages and news; NH DOE's site also refused direct downloads with 403 on 2026-11-11). Manchester's 2024-25 rates (just over 30% districtwide; 21% elementary, 27% middle, 37% high school), presented to the Board of School Committee's Student Conduct Committee on January 21, 2026, appear only in a news report (https://manchester.inklink.news/?p=272764, January 24, 2026); the district site (https://www.mansd.org/) returned a JavaScript bot check to curl, and no board document with the figures was found. The existing topic chronic-absenteeism-nh-data stands as is.
- **HB 1830 (2026) text from gc.nh.gov.** https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&id=2334&txtFormat=html: gc.nh.gov refused connections (empty reply) from mid-session on 2026-11-11, and the only Wayback capture holds the site's bot-check page. Fact 3 of students-firearms-school-bills-2026 was read from the NH Liberty Alliance's copy of the "As Introduced" text; recheck against gc.nh.gov.
- **SB 507 (2026) introduced text.** https://gc.nh.gov/bill_status/legacy/bs2016/billText.aspx?sy=2026&v=SI&id=1291&txtFormat=html: gc.nh.gov refused connections; no Wayback capture found. Only the original title (from a December 30, 2025 Wayback copy of the status page) is used; what the original bill would have required of districts is not described.
- **Members and reports of the SB 575 bullying committee and the SB 507 staff-violence committee.** Both reports were due November 1, 2026. The General Court's study committee pages (https://gc.nh.gov/statstudcomm/) could not be reached (refused connections; WebFetch "socket hang up"), and web searches found no appointment lists or reports.
- **SB 615 (2026) status (affects HB 1727's RSA numbering).** Not checked on gc.nh.gov (unreachable). A secondary bill-tracking site reports SB 615 became 2026 chapter 200, inserting RSA 161:15 (a SNAP study commission); if so, HB 1727's DHHS section is RSA 161:16 under 2026 ch. 331:4-5.
- **NH DHHS 2026 Summer EBT press release.** The July 2026 release ("third consecutive year," $120 per child, benefits by July 17, 2026) was found only as a repost (https://news.rochesternh.gov/nh-dhhs-announces-summer-ebt-federal-program-offsets-loss-of-school-meals-during-summer-months/); the dhhs.nh.gov original URL was not found, and dhhs.nh.gov refused direct downloads (403). Facts use the DHHS program page (Wayback) and USDA's participating-areas page instead.
- **Birth counts and enrollment projections.** CDC's natality report PDF (https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/data/nvsr/nvsr74/nvsr74-1.pdf) returned an HTML page instead of the PDF; no NH Office of Planning and Development or NH DOE enrollment projection was retrieved. The enrollment topic uses NH DOE's own statement on births.
- **NH DOE fall 2026 enrollment release.** Not found by 2026-11-11 (NH DOE usually releases enrollment with adequacy estimates around November 15). The fall 2026 figure (157,617) comes from the iPlatform ten-year report dated November 5, 2026.
- **NH DOE pages directly.** https://www.education.nh.gov/ and https://www.dhhs.nh.gov/ returned "Access Denied" (403) to curl on 2026-11-11; the press releases and program pages were read from Wayback snapshots, and the 2026 assessment data from the copies the site loads.

### Teachers, Pay and Dual Enrollment

- **gc.nh.gov from this session:** after the first few downloads (about 00:05 on 2026-11-11), gc.nh.gov returned empty replies to every request (curl exit 52), for bill pages, RSAs and PDFs alike. Pages needed after that were archived with Wayback Save Page Now (https://web.archive.org/save/...) and read from those same-day snapshots; they are marked "Wayback snapshot 2026-11-11." Re-check them directly when gc.nh.gov answers.
- **Senate committee reports for HB 1270 and HB 1202:** the report PDFs on the bill pages open only through ASP.NET postbacks (`__doPostBack`), and the guessed direct `pdf.aspx` links returned errors before the site stopped answering. Not needed for the facts above.
- **The NH Fiscal Policy Institute's 14,472.5 FTE for 2015-16** (its brief, https://npr.brightspotcdn.com/d6/b4/6cf69c6748cfbf60b5ef8634aa2a/new-hampshires-educator-workforce-faces-growing-recruitment-retirement-and-affordability-pressures-002.pdf, p. 2): not reproduced. The saved NH DOE 2015-16 teacher salary report shows 14,493.7, and NH DOE's iPlatform (https://my.doe.nh.gov/iPlatform) blocks scripted access (Akamai), so a current re-run could not be checked. Its 10.2% drop in instructional support staff was not reproduced either (the FTE report shows 7,038.0 to 6,439.5, -8.5%).
- **A 2026-27 critical shortage list:** none found. NH DOE's Critical Shortage List page (https://nhdoepm.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/CHD/pages/347963415/Critical+Shortage+List) still shows the March 11, 2025 list, and the federal Teacher Shortage Areas report API (https://tsa.ed.gov/api/ViewReportsAPI/Reports) returned no New Hampshire rows for 2026_2027.
- **A 2026 law giving career and technical education centers more hiring flexibility:** not confirmed. The news story with that headline (https://www.nhpr.org/post/new-law-gives-career-tech-centers-more-hiring-flexibility-0) is dated May 9, 2021 and describes a law signed by Governor Sununu; it names no bill number. Searches found HB 354 (2025), reported as chapter 147, an alternative licensing route for CTE instructors, but it is a 2025 law and its bill page could not be checked on gc.nh.gov. No 2026 CTE hiring bill was found, so none is included.
- **NH DOE's July 15, 2025 release on educator credential renewals** ("New Hampshire Reaches Highest Three-Year Cohort of Educator Renewals on Record," reported as 8,014 renewals): education.nh.gov returns 403 to scripts, and no Wayback copy was located; only a Patch republication was found (https://patch.com/new-hampshire/concord-nh/new-hampshire-reaches-highest-three-year-cohort-educator-renewals-record).
- **Workforce age and job projections** in the NHFPI brief (Census Quarterly Workforce Indicators, 33.7% of school workers aged 55 or older in 2025; NH Employment Security's 1,114 annual teaching openings for 2024-2034): not retrieved from the primary sources (https://lehd.ces.census.gov/data/ and https://www.nhes.nh.gov/elmi/), so not included.
- **EPI's report directly:** https://www.epi.org/publication/the-teacher-pay-penalty-remained-high-in-2025-teachers-and-students-nationwide-are-still-paying-the-price-of-decades-of-neglect/ returns 403 to curl and WebFetch; the 2026-10-31 Wayback snapshot was used.
- **State board rules for part-time teachers** (RSA 189:39-c, IV): not checked in the Ed rules (https://gc.nh.gov/rules/).
